Mkhwanazi Inquiry: Lt Gen Senthumule and Mr Fadiel Adams

Ad Hoc Committee to Investigate Allegations made by Lieutenant General Nhlanhla Mkhwanazi

10 March 2026
Chairperson: Mr S Lekganyane (ANC)
Share this page:

Meeting Summary

Video (Part 1)

Video (Part 2)

Ad Hoc Committee on Gen Mkhwanazi's Allegations

The Ad Hoc Committee met to hear evidence from Lieutenant-General Hilda Khosi Senthumule of the South African Police Service (SAPS) and Member of Parliament Mr Fadiel Adams (NCC) as part of its inquiry into allegations raised by KwaZulu-Natal Police Commissioner Lieutenant-General Nhlanhla Mkhwanazi. The inquiry concerns the alleged unlawful disbandment of the Political Killings Task Team (PKTT), claims of political interference in SAPS operations, allegations of corruption involving senior police officials, and broader concerns that organised crime networks may have infiltrated elements of law enforcement.

Acting Deputy National Police Commissioner Lieutenant-General Senthumule testified about her interaction with businessman Vusimuzi “Cat” Matlala in connection with the sponsorship of a SAPS awards event. She acknowledged that it had been “regrettable” to request sponsorship from Matlala without first conducting background checks. The issue arose after Matlala’s company sponsored bar fridges for the event. Senthumule stated that the request had been made in good faith but conceded that, given her senior role in SAPS, she should have exercised greater caution and conducted due diligence before approaching him.

Senthumule explained that she first encountered Matlala at an engagement celebration for the son of Deputy National Commissioner Lt Gen Shadrack Sibiya in September 2024 at the Sibiya family home. After the event, she contacted Matlala to discuss the sponsorship proposal and was later referred to one of his associates, who handled the logistical arrangements. She stated that this was the full extent of her interaction with Matlala until his name later appeared in media reports.

Members of Parliament questioned why a senior police official had not undertaken basic checks before seeking sponsorship from Matlala. Several members argued that even simple due diligence could have revealed concerns about his background. Lt Gen Senthumule acknowledged the criticism and accepted that the situation reflected poorly on her judgment. She stated that although the interaction appeared innocent at the time, it did not appear appropriate in retrospect and could affect her professional reputation.

Committee members also raised allegations circulating publicly that Lt Gen Senthumule had been involved in a personal relationship with Matlala. She responded to these claims during questioning. The discussion took place against the broader backdrop of allegations that organised crime groups had gained influence within law enforcement structures. At a separate inquiry, the Madlanga Commission, Crime Intelligence head Lieutenant-General Dumisani Khumalo testified that Matlala was associated with the so-called “Big Five” drug trafficking cartel.

Members also criticised Lt Gen Senthumule’s decision to approach a service provider for sponsorship, questioning whether it was appropriate for a senior police official responsible for investigative functions to solicit such support from private individuals. It was noted that Matlala had been charged in 2025 with offences including the attempted murder of a former partner, conspiracy to commit murder, fraud, and defeating the ends of justice.

Lt Gen Senthumule also addressed the disbandment of the Political Killings Task Team. She stated that she had only become aware of the decision nearly a month after it had been taken. According to her testimony, she received a letter dated 4 February 2025 from Deputy National Commissioner Shadrack Sibiya informing her that the unit had been disbanded, although the original instruction had reportedly been issued on 31 December 2024. She said she had not seen that earlier letter at the time.

She explained that Crime Intelligence head Lt Gen Dumisani Khumalo had informally mentioned that Sibiya had instructed him to transfer PKTT case dockets to her division. She told Lt Gen Khumalo the instruction had been directed to him rather than to her and that she therefore could not take the matter further.

The committee also heard testimony from Mr Fadiel Adams, who made several allegations relating to corruption and misconduct within SAPS and the handling of intelligence information. Mr Adams claimed that large quantities of seized mandrax worth approximately R100 million had disappeared from police custody and called for an investigation.

Mr Adams also alleged financial irregularities relating to the accommodation of PKTT members, stating that the team had been housed in accommodation costing around R10 000 per night. He suggested that this arrangement could form part of a larger money-laundering scheme and urged the committee to subpoena financial records to determine how public funds had been spent.

During questioning, Mr Adams was repeatedly asked to provide evidence to support his allegations, including claims involving Matlala and links between senior officials and corrupt activities. He stated that some information had been provided anonymously, including documents slipped under his office door, and that he had opened a case with the police and forwarded the material to relevant authorities.

Members expressed mixed reactions to Mr Adams’s testimony, acknowledging the seriousness of the allegations but emphasising the need for corroboration. The committee reiterated that witnesses were being questioned to establish the facts and prepare a report for Parliament.

Meeting report

Chairperson: Good morning, Members and to the staff of Parliament. Good morning, General.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Good morning, Chairperson, and good morning to all the committee members.

Chairperson: Honourable members, can I check if we have apologies?

Mr J Malema (EFF): Honourable L Mathys (EFF) said she would arrive around 10:30.

Adv G Breytenbach (DA): Honourable I Cameron (DA) may be late, and he may not make it at all, and I have other obligations in the House.

Mr D Skosana (MKP): My two other colleagues, Honourable S Nomvalo (MKP), will come at lunchtime. He is still at the airport. I think it departed already, and Honourable C Shongwe (MKP) will join us later or tomorrow morning.

Chairperson: Honourable Sokanyile, do we have apologies?

Ms T Sokanyile (ANC): Yes, Chair. Honourable M Ntuli (ANC) is not well. Honourable K Sangoni (ANC) is delayed by the transport because there is an accident on the road. Honourable X Nqola (ANC) is attending a meeting at 09:00. He will be here. He will join us later. Honourable Sangoni has arrived due to the delay in the transport.

Chairperson: What is the meeting he is attending?

Ms Sokanyile: Portfolio Committee. He was summoned there.

Chairperson: The Portfolio Committee, where he is the chairperson?

Ms Sokanyile: I did not go that far in asking the Chair.

Chairperson: Advocate Arendse, Good morning.

Adv Norman Arendse, Senior Counsel: Good morning, Chair, Honourable Members and General Senthumule. Sorry that I was a bit late.

Chairperson: I am handing over to you as the evidence leaders. Are there any remarks that you want to make?

Adv Arendse: Honourable Chair, just that fairly late in the day, when was it, last night, this morning, last night, we were advised of a change in the order of that witnesses will appear. We were expecting Honourable Adams to be first up this morning, and then we only got advised late by Vhonani that it will be General Senthumule, which caused a bit of not panic or concern, but Adv Mkhize will be leading her. Yes, but we are here and thank you, General Senthumule, for accommodating the late change, and we are ready to proceed.

Chairperson: Can you also confirm that the witness that is before us is the witness that the committee expected today?

Adv Arendse: Thank you, Honourable Chair. Yes, the witness is General Hilda Khosi Senthumule. She has been cooperating with the evidence leaders for the past two weeks or so, and she is here to present her evidence.

Chairperson: Can we swear her in?

Adv Arendse: Yes.

Chairperson: Welcome, General Hilda Khosi Senthumule. Our proceedings here. Every time we conduct them, we start by swearing our witnesses. Before we swear you in, let me make the following remarks that you have been invited here to this ad hoc committee. Then a committee appointed by the National Assembly to investigate allegations that have been made by the KwaZulu-Natal Provincial Commissioner, Lt Gen Nhlanhla Mkhwanazi. Your information or evidence that you provide before this committee will help it in its oversight and investigative role. The allegations include, amongst others, number one, the alleged unlawful disbandment of the political killings task team, otherwise known as the PKTT or the NPKTT. Number two, the alleged moratorium on the filling of vacancies within the South African Police Services, the SAPS. And number three, an alleged corrupt relationship between senior leadership within the SAPS and members of the public. And the last that we investigate is alleged political interference within the work of the SAPS.

General Senthumule, in extending the invitation, the chairperson of the ad hoc committee has indicated to you that you may provide such information to the committee if you so wish under oath in terms of the Powers, Privileges, Immunities of Parliament and Provincial Legislatures Act of 2004. Please be informed that by law you are required to answer fully and satisfactorily all the questions lawfully put to you or to produce any document that you are required to do so in connection with the subject matter of the inquiry, notwithstanding the fact that the answer or the document could incriminate you or expose you to criminal or civil proceedings or even damages. General Senthumule, you are, however, protected in that evidence given under oath or affirmation before a House or a committee of Parliament may not be used against you in any court or place outside Parliament except in criminal proceedings concerning a charge of perjury or a charge relating to the evidence or documents required in these proceedings. Please be aware that in terms of section 17(2) of the Powers, Privileges and Immunities of Parliament and Provincial Legislatures Act, a person who wilfully furnishes a House or committee with information or makes a statement before it which is false or misleading commits an offence and is liable to a fine or to imprisonment for a period not exceeding two years. You are required to take an oath to affirm that the evidence you are about to give is truthful. Which one do you elect? Oath or affirmation?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Thank you, Chairperson. I will take an oath.

Chairperson: You have elected to take an oath. May you please follow me as I dictate. May you please say I and state your name in full.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I, Hilda Khosi Senthumule.

Chairperson: Swear that the evidence I shall give.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Swear that the evidence I shall give.

Chairperson: Shall be the truth.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Shall be the truth.

Chairperson: The whole truth.

Lt Gen Senthumule: The whole truth

Chairperson: And nothing but the truth.

Lt Gen Senthumule: And nothing but the truth.

Chairperson: So help me God.

Lt Gen Senthumule: So help me God.

Chairperson: You are now under oath. May I hand back again to your evidence leader and the General to proceed with the presentation of evidence.

Adv Bongiwe Mkhize: Thank you, Chairperson. Morning General. In paragraph one of your statement, please provide your current position for the record.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Thank you, advocate. My current position is that of Acting Deputy National Commissioner for Crime Detection within the SAPS at the rank of Lieutenant-General.

Adv Mkhize: When did you start in this role?

Lt Gen Senthumule: My acting capacity resumed on the 22nd of July 2025.

Adv Mkhize: At section B of your statement, you set out the educational qualifications. If I can just ask you to run through those from paragraph three of your statement.

Thank you very much. I matriculated at Kenneth Masekela High School in Kwa-Thema in 1988, and I went on to study for a Bachelor of Arts in Psychology and Sociology at the University of Fort Hare, and I further obtained a Bachelor of Arts Honours at the same institution in 1993. Currently, I am registered for a master’s degree in management at Stadio.

Adv Mkhize: You mentioned that in paragraph five of your statement, you mentioned that you joined the National Intelligence Agency in 1998. Can you elaborate on the training you obtained at the National Intelligence Agency?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Having joined the NIA just after amalgamation in 1998, my operational training started with basic intelligence training, and I think that is where you do the entire spectrum of intelligence, including legislation, counter-intelligence, domestic intelligence, and priorities, as well as your estimates. Then I went further to study within counter intelligence, the defensive armour of counter intelligence, where I was trained in vetting investigations, integrity cycle profiling, and evaluations of those investigations. That training took place at the SANDF. I see here, sorry Chairperson, it says SADF, actually supposed to be South African National Defence Force.

Adv Mkhize: We then move to the section where you deal with your employment within law enforcement. We are now at paragraph six of your statement, where you say that following your time at the National Intelligence Agency, what was the trajectory of your career within the SAPS?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I am not sure if the Advocate wants me to go to paragraph seven because that is where the SAPS commences, or should I go back to paragraph six?

Adv Mkhize: You may start at paragraph seven.

Lt Gen Senthumule: My last posting within the intelligence environment was as a product manager for border intelligence within the NICOC, and I think whilst I was there, I was then approached at the time to apply for a post, and I must say that it was a lower post. It was on counterterrorism. So, as a result, I applied for that post. I went for all the interviews, and in 2008, I was successfully employed as a director to head counterterrorism investigations within crime intelligence, and I must say in this role that I played an instrumental role during the World Cup, where we actually were supposed to secure the event together with the FIFA security committee. So at that young age, and I think at that junior level, because all the generals and the commissioners there were sitting, and General Mkhwanazi and I were the only two directors sitting in that committee of the World Cup, chaired by General Cele. At that time, he was still the National Commissioner.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 8, you mentioned that you then joined the Directorate for Priority Crime Investigation (DPCI).

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes, on the 1st of September, I was then appointed to head organised crime investigations, and I must say it was a new post at the time. So I was the first appointed organised crime DPCI, commonly known as the Hawks.

Adv Mkhize: At the next paragraph, it looks like you subsequently moved. You did not stay very long at the DPCI, but then you moved laterally. Could you explain?

Lt Gen Senthumule: After engagement with General Cele at the time, who then said my skills were more required in crime intelligence, I was then laterally transferred to crime intelligence to go and head counter and security intelligence, a position I occupied again up until 2012.

Adv Mkhize: And you spent a period of about five years. At paragraph 10, you were again laterally transferred.

Lt Gen Senthumule: In 2016, General Phahlane became the acting national commissioner, and at the time, I was the component head for leadership management and development within HRD. General Phahlane saw it fit that I actually go and also be deployed within the Detective Services: Organised Crime. And again, this was a new post emanating from the reviewed Act of the DPCI, where SAPS was also given the mandate to investigate organised crime that is outside the DPCI mandate. So I then became the first head of organised crime of the SAPS.

Adv Mkhize: You then became the divisional commissioner of detectives and forensic services. Could you explain the significance of this role and how it prepared you for your subsequent appointment?

Adv Mkhize: Being appointed the DivCom for detective and forensic services meant that you are responsible for the entire criminal investigation business value chain, starting from your local criminal record centre, your crime scene management, going into the analysis of your exhibits and into the investigation environment.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, we are now at paragraph 11 of your statement. In paragraph 11, you say that on the 1st of August 2025, you were appointed to act as the national deputy commissioner, as you have already testified. What does this role entail, and how does it leverage your previous experience?

Lt Gen Senthumule: This role is broader in that it now covers a third element of another crime detection environment, which is crime intelligence. So this is about synergising the three critical environments or the backbone of the criminal justice system. So in this regard, my role makes sure I actually lead, oversee, and manage all the environments that are under those three divisions, which are the crime intelligence division, detective services, with the new structure, as well as the division of forensic services.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 12 of your statement, you basically detail everything that you have testified about over the past 27 years in law enforcement, which is, in essence, the experience that you have had over a period of time. Could you briefly just deal with what you were expected to do when you joined forensic services? We are at paragraph 12, starting from what you detail at 12.1.

Lt Gen Senthumule: As a country, over the years, we have seen crime escalate, which means that the volumes were increasing. However, our forensic science laboratory footprint was stagnant, along with capacity. So one of the things that we were tasked with was to expand the forensic science laboratory footprint. And in this regard, I was then responsible for leading a team that expanded the Gqeberha Forensic Science laboratory, which was actually a laboratory, but it did not have all the services. So for it to be a full lab, we had to add the DNA laboratory. And this initiative was launched by the President in August of 2023. Thank you, Lieutenant General.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant, if I may ask, you mentioned specifically this project relating to Gqeberha. How many laboratories do we have in South Africa that you particularly look after?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In the country, we have four full laboratories. We have got one in the Western Cape, one in Gauteng, the Eastern Cape, and KwaZulu-Natal, which was not working over a period of time owing to the floods that took place in KwaZulu-Natal. However, now we have managed through the help of the Portfolio Committee and the Minister of Public Works and the Minister of Police, we now have a lab, a temporary lab at Mayville, which makes it four laboratories in the country.

Adv Mkhize: At 12.2 of your statement, you also mentioned that you championed initiatives that increased resources for the division. Can you provide examples of these initiatives and their outcomes?

Lt Gen Senthumule: There has been an outcry on the capacity within the detective services, particularly the investigators. So one of the key things that we actually assist with, which is still in progress, is to add the numbers of the investigators. However, we went broader and also added the numbers within your local criminal record centres, particularly the people who actually have to respond to crime scenes for us to be able to support the initiatives of the investigators in putting those who are responsible for crime behind bars. In this regard, we added numbers. I think if I may just recall, we were given 4 600 positions, and I think they were phased in. To date, we have been able to take at least 2.8. I think with the last intake; we still have to recruit more detectives.

Adv Mkhize: Thank you, Lieutenant General. At paragraph 12.3 of your statement, you mentioned that you were the co-chair of the SAPS Business Participative Partnership. What were the main mandates and achievements of this partnership?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I still am the co-chair of this initiative, and I must add that this is a game-changer in terms of law enforcement and business in the country. We, as the police, I think we have seen the fiscal government shrinking, and as a result, we are also directly impacted, particularly in operations. So in leveraging the ecosystem of business, we can partner with businesses, and in this regard, we have identified six programmes, if maybe you can allow me to just go through them quickly. And this is in relation to a police improvement plan for the police. We have five priorities. If I may just take the committee through those priorities. It is the police station modernisation. It means automation of police stations and also includes the virtual police stations, where we can offload non-core services online rather than having them done at the police station. The second one is also leveraging technology. This will also include the detectives where we have your e-docket, not in terms of what we have now, e-scanning, but basically it would mean that your docket will no longer be a physical structure, but rather it will be automated. And I think any other copy, we now have iCloud, where we can manage our knowledge and also minimise the issue of corruption and the loss of documents. The other one is airborne support. There are areas which are not policeable with the police vehicle, and even in other areas which are rural, though you will have your mounted units. I mean, looking at those areas, we leverage with business in terms of their air support. And then we can be able to penetrate, particularly the gang areas here in the Western Cape as well, where police cannot be able to enter using vehicles. Then it is the forensic services. I spoke about the expansion of the laboratories. And then again, it is the issue of looking at the increasing detective capacity. So those are the five areas on which we are working.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraphs 13 to 13.5, you say that, beyond your primary roles, you have also held several additional positions, such as project manager for the International Association of Women Police Conference and the regional coordinator for the Southern African Regional Police Chiefs Cooperation Organisation (SARPCCO). How did these roles contribute to your overall expertise within law enforcement?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Both positions are actually within the region. And the other one is operating in the global space. The IAWP, which is the International Association for Women Police, is responsible for coordinating as well as bringing all women in law enforcement together, where we can share skills and we can talk about our experiences. We also have training programmes aimed at identifying your high flyers or those who can be earmarked for future leadership. So this conference was the first of its kind in Africa. And to date, we have been the most attended. So in that role, I think my project management skills were really enhanced. I mean, this was a big conference. And I think the way we pulled it off, we even got commendation from the then Commissioner General Riah Phiyega. In terms of the region, again, it is about getting women. If we can look at the history of policing, women, I think, came into being in the police in the ‘70s. So there is still that discrepancy in terms of women growing and women being seen as operationally capable. So in these structures, we then championed, for example, in the region, the issue of promoting opportunities for women in operations, but also ensuring that those women are given support and training.

Adv Mkhize: You have set out your background very eloquently of all the years that you have spent within the SAPS. I would now like to just take us to the next point, which is your role and responsibilities as the Divisional Commissioner of Detective and Forensic Services. It starts at paragraph 14, and it goes all the way to paragraph 18.4. Perhaps you can just summarise your role and responsibilities, but specifically, for you to set out your primary responsibilities concerning strategic management, operational coordination, and the overall development of Detective and Forensic Services. It starts at paragraph 14.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Before I get into 14, I want to mention that the division, as configured, has eight components, and these include your general detectives. These include commercial crime investigations, organised crime investigations, serious violent crime investigations, family violence, child protection, sexual offences, criminal records centre, crime scene management, forensic science laboratory, and quality management. Previously, these were two divisions which were merged into one. My responsibility is to ensure that we have an effective investigation of crime. I am also responsible for coordination, particularly across the board with other law enforcement agencies, but also internally, as well as in the global space. I will not talk about the results, because that is an obvious one, but more than anything, it is about setting performance standards as well as performance reviews.

Adv Mkhize: Thank you, Lieutenant General. This is quite important for the testimony that you are giving today because the questions relate to your time, specifically when you were the Divisional Commissioner, so I will spend a bit of time here. Can you explain the specific critical competencies that you lead within SAPS, focusing on the strategic management, effective investigation, and specialised investigative capacities? You are now at paragraph 15.1 to 15.3.

Lt Gen Senthumule: In this regard, and I think as I said, it is about developing intervention mechanisms and recovery plans for performance, ensuring that there is implementation of all our instructions that regulate how we do investigations, as well as the SOP, particularly in the forensic environment. Again, when we investigate the result, it is the successful persecution of perpetrators. So in this regard, we then guide investigations on the ground. We have meetings with teams where, for example, let us say it would be a case, and I think I can cite the recent ones, the case around maybe witnesses that we have, or maybe they have been eliminated for one reason or another. So we coordinate those investigations, and at times we do involve other law enforcement agencies. Coordination is quite critical, because for complex investigations, you do need other law enforcement agencies like your FIC and your SARS. So we then coordinate those competencies and bring them together with the investigators.

Adv Mkhize: Thank you, General. At paragraph 16, you set out the ultimate objective. So, what would you say the ultimate objective is that you aim to achieve in your current role? Well, that is the role before you started acting, particularly concerning community safety and crime prosecution.

Lt Gen Senthumule: And I think this goes to the police in general, that ours is to contribute effectively and in a more professional manner towards an increased feeling of safety in our communities. Again, I think we have had a lot of debates and discussions around organised crime. We have an obligation to really tackle and deal with organised crime successfully, and we can do so in a collaborative and coordinated approach. So we do need to work with our sister agencies to ensure that all that we do is properly investigated and that our forensic evidence is gathered, so that the justice cluster can be able to say safely that we have put the perpetrators of crime behind bars successfully and convicted.

Adv Mkhize: From paragraph 19 of your statement, which would be at capital letter E, section E, you set out the background of the issues that are before this committee. Could you kindly elaborate on the purpose of your testimony this morning?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I will take it back to the 7th of November last year. After the testimony of Adv Johnson, I felt the need to come and address the committee on specific areas which I felt were not covered. And also, I think the Chairperson then subsequently invited me to come and sit. So in this particular setting, I will address the issue of the disbandment of the PKTT. Additionally, I will look at issues raised by Adv Johnson. And then lastly, I will look at the issues before the Madlanga Commission, particularly the allegations made by General Sibiya against me as a person.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, you mentioned that one of the reasons why you are here is to testify on the disbandment of the PKTT. What is your involvement within the PKTT? We are at section F of your statement.

Lt Gen Senthumule: If I can take the committee through the historical considerations to get to where we are today. In December 2024, as is the norm in the police, we are all deployed as senior officers to assist the provinces in monitoring and overseeing police operations during the festive season. And I was deployed in the Western Cape with General Patekile. I returned home in Gauteng on the 3rd of January. And from Monday the 6th, I started my leave. I returned from leave on the 17th of July.

Adv Mkhize: So, Lieutenant General, when did you learn about the disbandment of the PKTT?

Lt Gen Senthumule: General Khumalo and I, General Khumalo is the DIVCOM for Crime Intelligence. We work within the same programme. So, many times we will be in the same meetings. I will not recall the date. And I think I did say before that some of the chronology I have lost in the process. I met him in one of the meetings. So, normally, during tea, we would sit together. And he informed me that I am not sure if I can say it in Zulu, when he said your boss said I should hand over the PKTT dockets to you. And I asked him again in Zulu, why. And he said, no, he did not know. He was just given an instruction. So, I said to him that, unfortunately, an instruction in the police is everything. So, you were given an instruction, and I was not. So, we laughed about it, and I said I could not take it further.

Adv Mkhize: Thank you, Lieutenant General. You mentioned that you had come back from the Western Cape on the 3rd of January. And then you subsequently took leave from the 6th of January. When did you go to the office? And when, what did you then have the conversation with Lt Gen Khumalo?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It would have been on that week of the 17th when I returned from leave.

Adv Mkhize: So, we are now at paragraph 23 of your statement. What was your understanding of the situation at that point after you had that exchange with Lt Gen Khumalo?

Mr Malema: Lieutenant General, the question of when you learn about the disbandment of PKTT was not sufficiently answered because you cannot say you learned about it when you are having a conversation with General Khumalo, when you return from leave. Because, as a general, as it were, even if you are on leave, the matter became a big issue. And anyone, even on leave, should have paid attention to what is really happening. And from there, you can say, I will deal with it when I return to work. But otherwise, this was topical from when the announcement was made. So, when did you learn about it and how?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Honourable Malema, I understand the direction of your question. And I did say this even on other platforms. When I was on leave, I went away with my kids, and I do not think I was in touch with my colleagues. I will give an example where there was a break-in. Actually, one of the members of the lab was kidnapped or allegedly kidnapped. And I only got to know when General Sibiya called me to say, do you know about this matter and I then referred him to the acting. So, because we really do not get time to be with our families. When I was on leave at that time, I remember I was not in touch with most of the things in the office. So, the actual time I learned about, not the disbandment, but the movement of the dockets from crime intelligence to detective was when General Khumalo engaged me.

Mr Malema: And the disbandment, that one I hear of the dockets, the disbandment?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The disbandment then came when I received a letter from General Sibiya on the 4th of July, which then specified that the Minister had given a directive that the disbandment should take place. Even General Sibiya, when we discussed the matter, said to me, have you spoken to Khumalo about the movement of dockets of the PKTT from crime intelligence into the detectives? And I did say to him, yes, Khumalo informed me. And as I said to Khumalo, there was no instruction to me, and therefore, I could not deal with it. So, it was at that peripheral level, but there was no intent or actually a conscious intent to sit me down and say, " This is the Minister’s instruction, bring Khumalo, let us have a meeting, and how do we do it? So, in my position at the time, I think it was learning on it as and when I had meetings or maybe like General Khumalo and General Sibiya.

Mr Malema: General, the story becomes big on the 31st of December. You only got to hear about it from Sibiya on the 4th of July. It cannot be correct. A person at your level, even if you are on leave, we all go on leave, we get tired of the EFF, we switch off, and we are like, we do not want, but anything significant, there is no way it will not reach me as a senior person in the EFF. I accept the formalities, but how do you get to know about it for the first time, even if it is not formal? It cannot be the 4th of July because by the 4th of July, it was an old issue, actually.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Again, I will say I do hear you, Honourable Malema. Let me give an example. There were many meetings that took place from the beginning until General Khumalo spoke to me, and General Sibiya also spoke to me. I was not in the know of all those meetings, even the letter to General Rabie and the instruction to him, those things I never got to know. If I respond in the affirmative, I think I will be lying to the committee, and I think I want to leave it at that with respect. Thank you, Honourable Malema.

Adv Mkhize: Thank you, Chairperson. So, General, your testimony is that at the time that you, General Khumalo, informally had a conversation with you regarding the movement of the dockets, what was your understanding of that? What was the purpose of the movement of the dockets, if not for the directive of the 31st?

Lt Gen Senthumule: When General Khumalo explained it to me, he said that the DNC said that the dockets must move from crime intelligence, which actually is conducting investigations, not necessarily court-directed, and they are doing so in support of the detectives. So, the dockets must then go to a structure that was dealing with the detectives, with the investigations, which was my understanding.

Adv Mkhize: Okay. So, your testimony is that you were not aware of the letter of the 31st of December. This is in January. Roughly after the 17th of January.

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, I was not aware.

Adv Mkhize: But you only became aware on the 4th of July.

Lt Gen Senthumule: On that letter that General Sibiya sent, he specified that the Minister directed that this should happen.

Adv Mkhize: Just the last follow-up question on that. Are you testifying that that was the time when you saw the letter? Or is your testimony that this is the first time that you learned about the disbandment? Because the question was quite specific. When did you learn about disbandment?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I never actually saw the 31st of December letter at that time. I only saw it being made reference to in the letter that General Sibiya wrote to me. So, in essence, I then learned of the disbandment in that particular letter of the 4th of July. 4th of February. I apologise. No, no. I was apologising that it was the 4th of February when the letter was written to me. So, I actually said 4th of July. So, I was making an apology for that mistake. Thank you.

Mr Malema: That is why it did not make sense at all that there could be such an announcement on the 31st, and you learn about it in July. But when you say February –

Lt Gen Senthumule: I actually apologise. I think I made an error. Apologies for that.

Adv Mkhize: Although we have not reached that section, just for the sake of completion, at Annexure 4, which is on page 77, it is dated 2025-0204. So, that's the 4th of February 2025. Is this the date when you learned about the PKTT Disbandment?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes.

Adv Mkhize: We may now proceed. At paragraph 25 of your statement, actually, if I can just take it one step back. At paragraph 23, what was your understanding? You testified that you had an informal discussion with General Lieutenant Khumalo regarding the PKTT. What was your understanding of the situation at that point?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The mention of the task team or the PKTT took me back to 2018. And when the IMC met in 2018, we were invited as senior generals in the police. At that time, I was the head of organised crime. General Mosikili was the Divisional Commissioner for detectives. General Tsumane was the DNC for detectives. So, the entire programme, in terms of the responsibilities around issues of investigations or maybe what I would call contract killings, we were invited. General Ramatswele, at the time, was the component head for general detectives. He was also invited. So, the four of us were in that meeting where this PKTT was configured.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 26, after your discussion with Lt Gen Khumalo, what transpired when Lt Gen Sibiya enquired about the transfer of dockets?

Lt Gen Senthumule: As I said earlier, my response to him was that Khumalo told me the same thing. And I informed him that there was nothing in writing for me that I could actually use as a point of reference to engage Khumalo in relation to the movement of the PKTT dockets.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 27, you say that on the 4th of February 2025, you received a letter from the DNC, Sibiya, in your capacity as the Divisional Commissioner for Detectives and Forensic Services. That letter is attached as HKS4, and it is on page 76 of the bundle. If you could turn to page 76 of the bundle. What were your instructions? What were the instructions that were contained in that letter? And what was its dated basis regarding the directive?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I must say that my copy is quite bad. I cannot read properly, but I will try. And it said that in compliance with the aforementioned directive of the Minister, you are directed to engage with the Divisional Commissioner for Crime Intelligence with a view to doing a proper handover and to follow all the processes necessary to conduct a handover process. This includes, but is not limited to, a report of all cases, a case register where all the cases were recorded, and the current status of court-going cases and cases under investigation. Paragraph 5.3, all cases, or projects documentation relevant to the Political Police Task Team. This should include evidentiary materials, and that is documents, electronic recordings, et cetera. It must be booked into the SAP13 or in possession of the team, or stored in any storage facility. All human and physical resources are located. They must be listed, as well as a report on all financial expenditure for the duration of the operations of the PKTT.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, this letter was from Lt Gen Sibiya, dated the 4th of February 2025. What was the purpose of the letter?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The purpose was to kick-start a process where I would engage General Khumalo, and then we would start the handing over process of the work of the PKTT from General Khumalo as the project manager, and that will move into the detective environment.

Adv Mkhize: Paragraph 3 of the letter sets out the terms of the Minister's Directive regarding the disbandment of the PKTT, and the date is the 31st of December 2024. Is this the reason you say that it was the first time that you learned that there was a Minister's Directive?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes. In fact, even the 31st of December correspondence, as quoted here, was the first time. That I actually learned about it.

Adv Mkhize: Okay. What was the specific instruction to you within the division that you were heading at the time?

Lt Gen Senthumule: As stipulated, it is just to get a report on all cases, and together with, I mean, if you read it, it said even items which were kept at SAP13 or any other place were supposed to be handed over to the detectives for them to begin their storing process.

Adv Mkhize: On the following page, the instruction continues. If you can read that into the record.

Lt Gen Senthumule: On paragraph 6, it says it is advised that the Divisional Commissioner for Detective and Forensic Services ensures that the cases and investigations are taken care of to ensure that cases are not lost in court because of the deactivation and the disbandment of the task team. Further, in paragraph 7, you are required to ensure that this is done within seven days, and also consider the content of the attached correspondence sent to the DivCom Crime Intelligence, which you will also ensure the closing report is submitted as per the Directive of the National Commission.

Adv Mkhize: In terms of that letter, all the actions that you read out earlier that are set out at paragraph 5, they were to be actioned within seven days. Is my understanding correct?

Lt Gen Senthumule: That is correct.

Adv Mkhize: Okay. If you can, then go back to paragraph 29 of your statement. Paragraph 29, paragraph 30 sets out what you have read into the record based on HKS4. Now, at paragraph 30, you mentioned the 6th of February. Could you explain to the committee what that relates to?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Post receiving the letter, and I must say there is no interrelation between the letter and the meeting of the Minister. It is just a collage of events. On the 6th of February, the Minister of Police, Minister Mchunu, called a sub-top management meeting, and this meeting also involved or included the Deputy Ministers, the National Commissioner, the DNCs, Divisional Commissioners, and Provincial Commissioners. General Patekile was in attendance because he is here in the Western Cape, and the meeting was about briefing the Minister on the key competencies of SAPS. I remember in that meeting, I was supposed to present on the structure of the detectives and our initiative towards capacitating the detectives.

Adv Mkhize: What were the outcomes of that meeting?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Unfortunately, I think on the day we arrived, as we were driving to the venue, which is the provincial office in the Western Cape, I got a call from my cousin that my nephew was killed after he was stabbed, and that actually resulted in me having to return home. So, in essence, I did not conclude the sitting of that meeting. I had to go back home.

Adv Mkhize: How did this event, this tragic loss of your nephew, impact your ability to engage immediately with the PKTT handover process? Because what we have read from the letter is that the Minister's directive was that it ought to be disbanded immediately. That is number one. Number two, as per the author of the letter, Lt Gen Sibiya, he had directed that it must be actioned within seven days. So, how did this event impact your deliverables?

Lt Gen Senthumule: General Khumalo and I were not able to meet because I think I was home fully in Kwa-Thema for the better part of the funeral arrangements, and also General Khumalo had other engagements because, as you can know, our portfolios require us to take part in a meeting. So, the only time we were able to meet was towards the end of February.

Adv Mkhize: What steps did you discuss for the handover process when you met, and what was the PKTT disbandment? What steps did you take for the handover process?

Lt Gen Senthumule: When you are given an instruction, I do not think you go haphazardly and implement. So, we had to find a strategy or a way of how this is going to be done. And I think the starting point would be to have an implementation plan, and also categorise all the issues that will have to be undertaken, and also assign responsibilities, as well as the period it will take you to conclude the handover. So, in this regard, we then developed an implementation plan.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 33, you mentioned that you developed the PKTT disbandment implementation plan. Is the plan set out somewhere? Where can we find this plan, and what were its objectives?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The plan is set out, I think, as an annexure 5, which will be page 78. The objective, as I said, was to produce a strategy on how we are going to go about the disbandment process. And this plan, as you can see, is dated 6 March, because it was also part of what was given to the Minister as a presentation on the 6th of March. In this particular presentation, as per the letter of General Sibiya, where he said we must take stock of all the cases, their status. So, here we have a list of all the cases that were with the PKTT, and this will include your politically related cases, your parallel cases, and other ad hoc cases that they would pick up as they were investigating the cases, and these were linked to the PKTT. And also, it included the workload of the PKTT, which included traditional leadership cases and University of Fort Hare cases. It had all the numbers and the case status in terms of where they are in relation to the investigation. So, in total, at the time of our assessment, General Khumalo, the PKTT had 612 cases. I think as you go down on page 82, it stipulates now the phased-out approach of how the disbandment will take place. In total, 125 dockets were under investigation, and of those, six were politically related, 46 were traditional leadership related, 16 were parallel cases, two were the University of Fort Hare, and one was ad hoc. So, all these cases were under investigation, and 10 dockets were already submitted for decision at the DPP's office.

So, all of these 125 cases, plus 10, were to be transferred to KwaZulu-Natal and Eastern Cape Serious and Violent Crimes, particularly the murder and robbery unit, for investigation. I need to clarify here that General Sibiya said the stalkers must come to the head office, but the plan, as proposed for the Minister, was that the stalkers must go back to the province to a specialised unit. I think we will get into that when we proceed with the...

Adv Mkhize: Yes, we will. Thank you, Lieutenant General. As you are aware of the timeline, I had asked you a bit earlier as well, that we are now on the 6th of March, and you were presenting –

Ms D James (ActionSA): Just clarity, the dockets in the plan, it says that the dockets must go to KZN, Crime Intelligence. Am I correct? Is that from General Sibiya's office, or is that from PKTT's office in KZN?

This was, as per my discussion with General Khumalo, that we were tasked with the handover process. So, when we were discussing, we both felt that the rightful place to investigate the cases would be within KwaZulu-Natal, Serious and Violent Crimes, not necessarily at the head office. And I need to clarify, I think I have said this over and over, that at National, we do not necessarily have the capacity to investigate. Ours is to ensure that we intervene, we monitor, so we would not have been able to have a task team. I think my argument was from that particular perspective, that you cannot disband a team and form a team. It would have meant having a team. So, the logical step to follow was to then say, already there is an established Serious and Violent Crimes environment in KwaZulu-Natal, who are capable of taking the investigations forward.

Ms James: So, basically, in essence, you are saying that those dockets had no business finding their way to General Sibiya's office?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes, I think so.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, perhaps it is also a good time, even though we are still at paragraph 32, perhaps this would be a good time to explain to the committee, at the point at which you were developing this implementation plan, where were the dockets?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The dockets were in KwaZulu-Natal, with the PKTT. And I suppose General Khumalo, though he actually had moved, maybe I should clarify this, Chairperson. General Khumalo was the project manager for the PKTT in KwaZulu-Natal, based in KwaZulu-Natal. In 2022, he got promoted to become the DIVCOM for Crime Intelligence. General Masemola and General Cele agreed that for continuity, General Khumalo should continue as the PM for the PKTT. So, though he was here physically, the team was still based in KwaZulu-Natal, and the dockets were in KwaZulu-Natal.

Adv Mkhize: We are still at paragraph 32 of your statement. You mentioned that General Khumalo, when you saw him, and you had that informal conversation, which you repeat at paragraph 32, had a report. What was contained in that report?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Maybe also to go back to Honourable Malema's question. I think this is Honourable Malema when I said I was totally in the dark. Even this particular engagement between General Masemola, General Khumalo, and General Rabie. General Khumalo only made reference to it to say that on the 22nd of January, they had already started with a report to the Minister on the disbandment. And in that particular report, they stipulated the winding down approach, because it was not possible to either disband immediately or actually within a week of the process.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 34, you mentioned that you appeared before the Portfolio Committee. Can you describe what was specified regarding the transition of cases? And firstly, what was the purpose of that appearance? And secondly, you can then describe what was specified in that meeting.

Lt Gen Senthumule: If my memory serves me well, the rationale behind our appearance at the Portfolio Committee was to deal with the challenges within crime intelligence. So General Khumalo had prepared a presentation on all issues that the Portfolio Committee had asked us to prepare a presentation on. But on that particular presentation, there was a slide that specified the disbandment of the PKTT. And I think in this particular slide, General Khumalo mentioned to the Portfolio Committee that this establishment or the disbandment will take the form of a transition from the NPKTT to component serious and violent crimes within detective and forensic services.

Adv Mkhize: For the sake of completion, the annexure, the slide that you referred to, is annexed as HKS6 at page 87. Is that the slide about which you are talking?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 36 of your statement, you mentioned that a meeting was convened by the Minister on the 6th of March 2025. What was the purpose of the meeting?

Lt Gen Senthumule: That specific meeting was to address progress made after the Minister's directive of the 31st of March 2024. And this will talk to the disbandment or the disestablishment of the PKTT. So the invitation was sent to the National Commissioner, who then extended through his office the invitation to all affected officials, including myself, General Khumalo, General Mosikili, General Sibiya, and the National Commissioner.

Adv Mkhize: Where was the meeting held?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The meeting was held in the Western Cape Provincial Commissioner's Office at Provincial Headquarters of the Western Cape.

Adv Mkhize: What were the deliverables outlined in the plan that you presented at this meeting?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think I did cover it earlier, but I will just break it down again. As I said, 125 dockets are under investigation, and 10 of those, which were submitted to the DPP for decision, were to be transferred to KwaZulu-Natal and Eastern Cape Serious and Violent Crimes. Remember, there were 40 dockets there. So the 122 would go to KwaZulu-Natal, and the three dockets of Fort Hare would go back to the Murder and Robbery Unit of the Eastern Cape. Then secondly, new cases which were to be reported on political killings will immediately be referred to the Murder and Robbery Units in KwaZulu-Natal. And the 297 dockets which were finalised, including 57 withdrawn and nine unfounded, all of those were to be returned to their station of origin for archiving or filing. And lastly, which is very critical, 114 dockets which were on the court roll were to remain with the PKTT until they were all finalised.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 39, you say that during these deliberations, Lt Gen Sibiya, the DNC, was not pleased with the approach of keeping the court's dockets going with the PKTT. What were the differing sentiments during the deliberations, particularly those that were with Lt Gen Sibiya?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Once the proposal was tabled before the Minister and the Deputy Minister, and the National Commissioner, General Sibiya, I think he raised his concerns that if, for example, the 114 dockets were to remain with the PKTT, it would mean that the team was not disbanded. It would mean that the team actually was continuing. So he felt strongly that actually all the dockets, including the court-going dockets, were to be transferred to the Murder and Robbery Units or to the detectives.

Adv Mkhize: Was he the only official at the meeting who had these different sentiments?

Lt Gen Senthumule: From the Minister's office, his Chief of Staff was there. And I think he also was in support of the DNC's proposal that all the dockets, including the court-going dockets, should go to the PKTT. And I think the Minister also shared the same view that no dockets should remain with the PKTT.

Adv Mkhize: So in essence, you answer that the Minister, Lt Gen Sibiya, and the Minister's Chief of Staff were dissatisfied with the manner in which you were approaching the implementation plan for the disbandment of the PKTT.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would not necessarily say dissatisfied. I would say maybe they disagreed, because remember, we are presenting. So they disagreed with the approach of keeping the 114 court going dockets with the PKTT.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 41, you mentioned that there was a Parliamentary briefing that you testified about earlier on the 5th of March 2025, which was the Police Portfolio Committee. How did the parliamentary briefing and the March 6 meeting with the Minister, his Chief of Staff, Lt Gen Sibiya, influence your understanding and legitimise the DNC's instruction for the handover?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Maybe with your permission, Chairperson, if I can go back to paragraph 40, because it is quite critical moving forward. As I said, the meeting deliberated, and then there were differing views, as you said, disagreements. But eventually, the implementation plan was provisionally approved. And the only amendment on that plan was that, which means the meeting agreed that the 114 dockets will remain with the PKTT. However, as and when each case gets concluded, the dedicated detective holding the docket and the combat member assigned for his protection will then withdraw from the team and return to their station of origin, or actually their business unit. Now, coming back to what you asked, and again, I think I will go back to the question of Honourable Malema, because for me, the instruction was said to me out of context. So, as you are implementing, you actually wonder, but why are we not having a meeting where this matter is discussed? Why is it only me, General Khumalo, and General Sibiya? So when the parliamentary briefing took place, I then realised that actually this is an authentic instruction. And also when the meeting of the 6thtook place, it brought perspective to me to say that the DNC's instruction, as articulated in his letter of the 4th of July, was actually legitimised. I felt that, okay, so I can go ahead with the process. The principals were in the know. Parliament was in the know.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 42, you mentioned that during the month of March 2025, there were numerous enquiries and reminders from the DNC about the handover process. What was the nature of the enquiries and the reminders that you received from the DNC?

Lt Gen Senthumule: We were supposed to report to the Minister at the end of March 2025. And I think the DNC would continuously ask, how far are you? Have you received the dockets? And I think he did say also, and I am sorry to bring this to this committee, during his Madlanga Commission, that he did put pressure, particularly on me, to get the dockets from General Khumalo. So they were numerous. I mean, when I say numerous, I mean probably it could have been once a day or once in two days, just to say, get the process going.

Adv Mkhize: You testified that, according to the letter at HKS4 that the DNC addressed to you and General Khumalo, he had said that action must be taken within seven days. You further testified that it is not as simple to put together a plan, an implementation plan. But at this point, we are at the end of March, and two months have essentially, just under two months have essentially elapsed after his instruction for this to be actioned. So you further go on in your paragraph 42, and you say that the DNC insinuated that I was now like Lt Gen Khumalo, and was dragging my feet in implementing the Minister's instruction. What are your comments on that?

Lt Gen Senthumule: As I said, there were actually, maybe say, disagreements in view of how this disbandment should take place. So in my view, there was a view that says disband everything, including the court dockets. And then there was a view, particularly from the team, that said, actually, let us also keep the court going, as the Minister's approval, that when, as and when the cases get concluded, then the team can withdraw back to their station. So the DNC was adamant that, actually, General Khumalo is not pulling his weight. And I must say at that time, they were not on talking terms. So I was the buffer between the two of them. So the DNC would then come to me and say, " Yeah, now you want to be seen like General Khumalo, and you are dragging your feet in implementing the Minister's instruction. So he was saying to me that the Minister also would see me as a person who was not willing or actually cooperative in fulfilling his instruction.

Adv Mkhize: Given that the instruction had come initially from the Minister, from his directive of 31 December 2024, and subsequently from the head of your division, who looked after your division, who would have been Lt Gen Sibiya, where was the differing view coming from? Because the Minister, his chief of staff, and the DNC seem to be speaking in one voice, even as he is pressurising you, it is to action the Minister's directive. So when you mention a differing view, where did that differing voice come from?

Lt Gen Senthumule: For me, the point of question advocate was around the issue of the court going dockets. I think everybody was fine with the dockets under investigation, and those which were with the DPP for decision, but also those which were finalised, withdrawn, and were supposed to go back to the station. General Sibiya, the Minister, and the chief of staff felt that if the PKTT kept the dockets, it meant that the team was not disbanded. One area of the plan that General Khumalo presented was the reduced number of the current staff or members who were deployed in the PKTT. So I think there were about 96 members. The revised staff establishment would have come to 68, which means they were reducing. Other members would have to go back to their station of origin or business unit, and the 68 would continue. So this 68 would focus on the 114 dockets which were caught going. And according to the Minister, General Sibiya, and the chief of staff, that meant that the team was not disbanded. So that basically was the point of contention.

Adv Mkhize: And would it then be fair to say that the point of contention came from, it would have been you and Lt Gen Khumalo, because you were tasked specifically to handle the disbandment and the transfer of dockets?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Because we were the compilers of the plan, we agreed with the 114 dockets staying with the team. General Mosikili and General Masemola were also in the meeting and were in support of actually retaining the 114 dockets. And the rationale is that when the docket is caught, already there is a rapport between the victims, there is a rapport with the prosecution and other role players who are involved. So, to actually give those dockets to a new team, you are actually disturbing. And I think the best way is to talk about the chain of custody, but also the progress, as well as the prospect of success in the investigation. That was the rationale why the dockets had to be kept with the team to conclude in court.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 44 of your statement, you mentioned that a follow-up meeting was convened with the Minister. You would have initially met on the 6th of March, and this is a follow-up meeting about three weeks later. What transpired at this meeting regarding the progress of the disbandment implementation plan?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In my view, the meeting was prematurely urgent. As General Khumalo started presenting, if I may go back to the 6th of March meeting, he presented 125 dockets under investigation. But on the 27th, he presented 121 dockets under investigation. I think this is where the 121 dockets came into the fore. And General Khumalo, during his presentation, explained that four dockets under investigation were successfully enrolled. And that led to the reduction of the dockets from 125 to 121. And the Minister also was worried that, actually, maybe let me say, concerned that no handover process took place. By the 27th, we should have already handed over the dockets from General Khumalo's outfit, which is the PKTT, to the detectives, which is me, and I must say, en route towards KwaZulu-Natal, because they were supposed to stay in the head office. So, at that time, that process had not taken place. So, the Minister was really displeased with both of us.

Ms Sangoni: I am sorry about that. Good morning, General. Please explain this. So, the dockets were going to physically move from KZN to you and then back to KZN. Is that what you mean?

Lt Gen Senthumule: At that time, the modalities of how were not discussed. It was just the roadmap to say, "This is what needs to happen.” So, meaning that eventually, whether it is from KwaZulu-Natal to KwaZulu-Natal to Gauteng, but the dockets by then should have moved from PKTT to the detectives.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, you had just provided evidence regarding the meeting of the 27th of March, you know, also dealing with what transpired at the meeting. At this point, three weeks, I think I had mentioned this already, that three weeks had lapsed since the meeting of the 6th of March. What steps had you and Lt Gen Khumalo taken to ensure that you, in fact, meet the Minister’s directive, that you deal with it and action it?

Lt Gen Senthumule: If I may hasten to mention that, much as the directive was given, I think all of us had to realise that both Lt Gen Khumalo and I are running divisions. So, we were not only seized with the matter of the PKTT, but there were other obligations. I remember on the 26th of March, Lt Gen Khumalo informing me that he was busy with the auditors. And I think before that, he had meetings with NICOC and other role players. So, from my side as well, I mean, running both divisions, we had to find time. All of us had to be realistic that this is not the only matter on the table for us. There were other critical and strategic matters with which we were dealing.

Adv Mkhize: Did your office or Lt Gen Khumalo's engage the DNC's office regarding the delay?

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, at no point.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraphs 45 and 46, you deal with the Minister’s dissatisfaction at this meeting. What was your response to the statement by the minister?

Lt Gen Senthumule: If my memory does not fail me, the Minister said, these two divisional commissioners are not serious about my instruction. And therefore, missile commissioner, moving forward, I will not deal with them; I will deal with you. And I remember, I raised my hand, and I said, Minister, I understood the instruction, and I am not undermining your authority in this one.

Adv Mkhize: How would you describe your relationship? Had you interacted with the Minister before this meeting?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I did.

Adv Mkhize: How would you describe the nature of your relationship?

Lt Gen Senthumule: To be specific, I mean, I have worked with previous ministers and in my view, he was the first Minister that I could at least identify with his vision, particularly in relation to the detectives. And the National Commissioner was in the know, so there were projects that he was running, and lo and behold, I was responsible for them. So, we had a very good relationship.

Adv Mkhize: And how would you describe the Minister’s relationship with the DNC?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Can I be asked not to respond to that because it is speculation? Thank you.

Adv Mkhize: Back at paragraphs 45 and 46, we are still there. What did the DNC instruct you to do afterwards, after the Minister had expressed his dissatisfaction?

Chairperson: Honourable Mathys?

Ms Mathys: Thank you, Chair, and good morning. Good morning, Lieutenant General. Just a question of clarity, do we have a copy of the minutes for that meeting, or where can we get them?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I know there was a secretariat on that day from both the National Commissioner and the Minister. I have never seen the minutes of those meetings, and I do not remember them being requested. I suppose we can ask both officers if they have a record of that meeting.

Ms Mathys: Okay, so we need to ask both the Minister’s office and the national commissioner's office as well.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, I had asked a question. What did the DNC instruct you to do after the Minister had expressed his dissatisfaction?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I guess he actually gained leverage because he reminded me that you see where this has led us. You are not willing to follow the instructions. And then he asked me to hastily and expediently finalise the handover. I must say that from that meeting, both General Khumalo and I, I mean, I was upset because in the meeting it would have seemed as if we did not do what we were supposed to do or maybe that we were not willing. And at that time, I asked the national commissioner, and I said to him, but General, do I really have to get involved? Why do these dockets not go somewhere else? And General Masemola said to me, it is because you are the head of detectives. That is why you need to get involved. But again, I must say that you could tell from that meeting that I think General Khumalo also was adversely affected. And I think the meeting did not end on a good note.

Adv Mkhize: General, I keep going back to the same point because I am actually trying to ascertain whether the Minister and the DNC were acting in a manner that was to put pressure on you. What we know is that the letter had first been written for your attention on the 4th of February. What we also know from what you have testified is that on the 5th of March, there was a portfolio committee meeting where some of these issues were ventilated. And on the following day, on the 6th of March, the Minister addressed you and Lt Gen Khumalo regarding the plan and that it ought to be actioned by the 17th of March. And at the following meeting on the 27th of March, there were still no steps taken. And you have also testified that you, your office, Lt Gen Khumalo's office, nor any of the offices had addressed a letter communicating the delay and perhaps requesting a further deadline. So would you say that on the 28th of March, when the DNC expressed, following the Minister’s dissatisfaction and what you have just testified, that this was now causing some type of tension between the team, was that unreasonable, given the fact that numerous deadlines had now not been met?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In this process, I do not think there is anything that I can deem unreasonable. Probably, I think I would say some of the things were not as feasible as we actually had hoped. So, yeah, actually, there was no, after the 27th, we both knew we had to do it, so there was no reviewing whether the DNC should be informed. After he spoke to me, we spoke to Lt Gen Khumalo. I actually spoke to Lt Gen Khumalo, and I said to him, if it is possible, on the 28th, can we finalise? And if you go to the plan, in my mind, I was thinking, I really have a lot of work to do. I only have three actions on the plan. If I do this, then we can conclude the process. So maybe my focus was not so much on what the DNC said, but more on what the plan required of me.

Adv Mkhize: Still at paragraph 47 of your statement, you further state that you wanted the process to be finalised, particularly your role in the handover process, so that you do not have to be called out for failure to comply with the instruction. It seems that you just wanted to get the job done. Did you believe that the Minister’s directive of 31 December should have been actioned? So what I am asking is, were you of the view that the disbandment ought to have taken place to remove the dockets from the PC’s – the Provincial Commissioner's – office in KZN, to the head office?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think part of your question, I am sure we will cover when I talk about General Mkhwanazi, but as I said earlier, the two meetings, 5th and 6th March, were critical for me to say, this is actually a legitimate instruction that all of us, because in that meeting there was deputy ministers, there was national commissioner, and by the way, their principals, myself and Khumalo, are implementers. So there was nothing wrong, actually, at that time when we were looking at the issue of the disbandment. For me, it came when I realised that the PC was actually not in the picture.

Adv Mkhize: So at paragraphs 47 to 50 of your statement, you deal with the finalisation of the dockets and the handover and transfer of them. What process was involved?

Lt Gen Senthumule: General Khumalo sits in an office in Pretoria East, and we therefore had to make arrangements to meet in his office and do the handover. General Motsepe, who is the component head for serious and violent crimes, would have been the operating officer from my division. Unfortunately, she was not feeling well, and she then delegated Brigadier Kgopodithata, who is the section head for stock theft, to actually be an assistant detective for that matter. People would say, but he is in stock theft, he is an assistant detective who actually worked almost all the environments in the detective. So then he was the one who was given to me by General Motsepe to accompany me with his team to complete the handover process.

Adv Mkhize: You set out the various steps that were taken, starting with the auditing of the dockets at paragraph 51. And what was the process of auditing the dockets? And what did you sign as proof of receipt? We are at paragraph 51.

Lt Gen Senthumule: We subsequently went to Crime Intelligence, General Khumalo's office, to be specific. It was I, Brigadier Kgopodithata, and Colonel Mdluli joined me later. And there were boxes of the dockets, meaning that General Khumalo had brought the dockets to the head office. And at that point in time, which means they actually came before the 27th, before the meeting of the Minister. When we met, they were already there in Crime Intelligence. Then the team, together with General Khumalo's team, had to do proper due diligence. And that would have been your first level of auditing. And that meant that they need to ensure, look at, for example, the complete list of the docket per box and compare it with the actual dockets. But over and above that, open the docket and see if all the documents are complete and properly filed, and also verify that there is nothing missing. And also, then the handing over took place from there.

Adv Mkhize: You rushed over the part when they actually arrived, when they were transferred from KZN to the head office. Could you deal with that in detail? Who brought them from KwaZulu-Natal?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would not be in the know. I think I heard from Colonel Khumalo's testimony at Madlanga that, actually, he was the one who brought the dockets. But at the time, I was not aware of how they came to General Khumalo's office. So, we were the receiver. So, we did not bring them to Gauteng.

Adv Mkhize: Okay. So, your testimony is that your evidence is that both you and Lt Gen Khumalo were not aware of how the dockets were moved from KwaZulu-Natal to the head office. However, they were brought to the head office.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would not say both of us were in the know. My take on this is that General Khumalo is the DIVCOM who had the dockets, instructed his people to get the dockets from KwaZulu-Natal into Pretoria, because that is where I am based. That is my only logical explanation.

Adv Mkhize: When did they reach the head office?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Because they were there already on the 27th. I suppose it would have been shortly before the meeting of the 27th.

Adv Mkhize: You can then continue at paragraph 51, where you set up the steps that you took once you received the dockets.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think I have already covered those. And maybe I can go to 52, where the team physically counted the dockets and against the list that was there in each box to make sure that everything corresponded and that no docket was left behind during the handover process. And once they were done, it was only then that General Khumalo and I co-signed the handover list.

Adv Mkhize: Okay. If I can, then ask you to turn to page 88 of the bundle, that is HKS7. Starting from 88 all the way to 91, particularly at page 91, that is your signature dated the 28th of March 2025. At the front of page 88 is a document which is on the SAPS letterhead, addressed to the project coordinator of investigations and politically related cases in KwaZulu-Natal. The heading is the handing over of the investigation, a national task team investigating politically related cases in KwaZulu-Natal. Is this the list that contains all the dockets that follow the audits that you refer to?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes.

Adv Mkhize: So when you say you did a handover, are you referring to this letter?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Actually, to this process. Yeah, this is just beyond the letter. The dockets are also physically handed over to the team of detectives.

Adv Mkhize: So at paragraph 53, what instructions were given to Brigadier Kgopodithata regarding the safekeeping and transfer of the dockets?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In response to this, I want to go back to the issue of the plan. The plan said the dockets were to be taken to KwaZulu-Natal murder and robbery units. So the instruction was that in the process of waiting for us to travel down, we have all the engagements that those dockets, I mean, he is an assistant detective, so they know the protocols around the safekeeping and guarding of the dockets. And so that will be pending the final handover to the provincial commissioner of KwaZulu-Natal.

Adv Mkhize: So, as Honourable James had pointed out to you, the dockets just made quite a long way to go back to KwaZulu-Natal. They left KwaZulu-Natal. What we know is that they arrived in Gauteng at the head office on the 27th of March. And the process that you have detailed, firstly, of auditing the dockets that were initiated by you and Brigadier Kgopodithata, for now, sending it back to KwaZulu-Natal. Is that correct?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes, if I may. And I think this was the beginning of the problems with the PKTT dockets, them being at the head office. Can I ask that we then cover it after I give my feedback on General Mkhwanazi's response towards the handling over?

Adv Mkhize: Okay, because you had now opened up a question to follow up on what these problems were. So we will park the question, and then we will revisit it at the relevant paragraph. At paragraph 54, you then stated that this was the first and last time you physically saw and interacted with the PKTT dockets. Can you elaborate on why this was the case?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It will even go to the issue of my discussion on how the dockets should be handled. As senior officers, we do not keep dockets in our offices. Dockets belong to the investigators. And therefore, for me, it was just to facilitate, and the people who then keep those dockets for the handover would be the rightful people who are actually handling or heading the investigations.

Adv Mkhize: Okay, so General, from paragraph 55 to 57 of your statement, paragraph 55, you set out the process that was followed for the handover of the PKTT dockets. So you set out that on 1 April 2025, you received a WhatsApp message about the dockets. How did you respond to this message? And what were your reasons for delaying the immediate transfer of dockets to KZN?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think, I am not sure if I may have touched it to say that with Kgopodithata, we, we agreed that on the 1st of April, actually the first week of April, we should take the dockets to KZN for the handover. So Brigadier Kgopodithata sent me a WhatsApp message where he was enquiring if they should start with the paperwork for the trip, meaning that they should plan the itineraries for approval for us to then travel. At the time, I think it was at a critical time where one of the Minister's projects, which relates to what I spoke about earlier, was the automation of police stations and also improving our service model at the police stations. So I was responsible for that project of the SAPS/BACSA [Business Against Crime South Africa] commitment dinner, just basically a partnership. So, where the business was going to commit to SAPS, they are resources in assisting us in improving service delivery at a police station, specifically. I remember Kgopodithata is not part of the action plan, but I am responsible. And also, I could not send a junior officer to the provincial commissioner. So I then asked that maybe they just halt the process, allow me to conclude this particular engagement, which was on the 7th of April. And then the following day, which is 8, 9, and 10, the Minister, along with a commissioner, had arranged a policing summit where all of us, as policing and management, were there. And if Honourable Mathys can also maybe indulge me, at this point, Honourable Mathys would remember that the Eastern Cape alleged rape of a minor happened.
