And I think also as Head of Detectives, I was seized with that province. So that again affected my ability to be able to travel to the Eastern Cape. There was a public outcry on that matter. And I think we had numerous briefings with Parliament on what could have happened in that particular alleged rape of a minor.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, you said that it would take place on the 7th of April, and that you said at paragraph 56. You later say that immediately after that, there was a police summit. When was the police summit?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It was on the 8th, 9th, and 10th of April. And that took place at Emperors Palace in Boksburg.

Adv Mkhize: Okay. You also testified about an issue, a matter that needed your attention in the Eastern Cape. When would that have been?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Still in April. I think it took place in the better part. I think the better part of April. There were contentious reports, and I think the doctors had to be summoned. And I think for two weeks we had to really respond to the matter, particularly to Parliament and the public. Adv Mkhize: Were you physically in the Eastern Cape?

Lt Gen Senthumule: We were in the Eastern Cape, but also we were at National, and at some point, I think we came to Parliament for a briefing.

Adv Mkhize: How long were you in the Eastern Cape?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would not remember, but I can say, as I said, I think the better part of the two weeks from the 14th, I think, up until probably the 21st or so. Not necessarily in the Eastern Cape, but dealing with the matter of the Eastern Cape's alleged rape of a minor.

Adv Mkhize: You then, at paragraph 58, say that Lt Gen Khumalo informed you that they were bringing more dockets from the Eastern Cape. When Lt Gen Khumalo called you on that day, the 23rd of April, regarding the additional dockets, what steps did you take, and why did you not involve Brigadier Kgopodithata?

Lt Gen Senthumule: On the 23rd of April, General Khumalo, maybe let me say, if you see on the letter of the handover, 118 dockets were handed over, not 121. So there were three dockets outstanding, and those would be the four hair dockets that are also listed in one of the amnesties. So on the 23rd of April, that call was to say that General Khumalo now has the three dockets, and that he is going to hand them over. And Brigadier Kgopodithata only came to the college because at the time we had an inter-poll meeting with other member states, and I was chairing that meeting. So the handover took place at the SAPS Tshwane College.

Adv Mkhize: Okay. At HKS 8, which is on page 92, are those the additional documents that you refer to?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes.

Adv Mkhize: Can you go to HKS8? And those are the three additional cases. And the date is the 23rd of April 2025. The Eastern Cape dockets. You then asked Brigadier Kgopodithata to engage Major General Mgobhozi in KZN. What was the outcome of this engagement? And what was the request made to you? This is in paragraph 59 of your statement.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Now that we have all the 121 dockets, I realised that actually time is filtering away at our disposal. So I said, General, Brigadier Kgopodithata, instead of waiting for me, engage the Deputy Provincial Commissioner for Crime Detection to arrange for a handover of the dockets. Because if you are to wait for me, this will never happen. And I think at that time, that's paragraph 59 now, Brigadier Kgopodithata, called the acting DPC, who was Brigadier Kunene, and he is the provincial head for serious violent crimes in the province. He was acting at that time, General Mgobhozi, who was, I think, on sick leave. In the discussion, Brigadier Kunene informed Kgopodithata that, listen, I will not be able to take those dockets. If you want me to take them, please ask the DIVCOM, which is me, to engage the provincial commissioner.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, if it was always available to you to be able to request from staffers in your office to communicate with the KZN team, why did you have to wait a month, the whole of April? We are now at the back end of April, before you could take the step that did not even physically involve you. You are now giving instructions to a brigadier to take action and start the communication. What is your comment on that?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I will again go back, Advocate, to say the intention was for me to go, because I am actually on the action plan, and realising that, you know, I think the pressures of work, and I must say, it is not like you just take the docket, you move. As you work in the police, I always say, you do not plan. I think the national commissioner will attest. So today, you plan that, okay, we are travelling. Tomorrow, you are told, pack all your bags, we are travelling to KwaZulu-Natal. There is a crisis, or we are going to the Eastern Cape. So, I must say that on the structure, there is a dotted line from the DPC to us. So I thought, and we actually work very well with all of them; they report to us on performance. So I thought, Kgopodithata, maybe thinking on my feet at the time, to say Kgopodithata, engage the DPC and see if we can, in the interim, engage. The intention was to engage General Mkhwanazi afterwards. And normally, when we do that with, I mean, I talked to General Khumalo, also in Gauteng, without the PC, and it is how we do business. And I think the PCs understand. So it is never an issue. So I thought, maybe instead of skipping them further, let the team, at least at the bottom, start the process without me. I will engage the DPC afterwards.

Adv Mkhize: Okay, thank you. So, in paragraphs 60 and 61 of your statement, you say that on the 24th of April 2025, you telephonically engaged Lt Gen Mkhwanazi. What was his response to your request to arrange the handover of the PKTT dockets?

Lt Gen Senthumule: His immediate response was confusion because he asked me, " Which dockets are you talking about? So I told him that these are the PKTT dockets in line with the disbandment letter. And I think one of the actions was that the dockets should be handed over to the SVC, Murder and Robbery. General Mkhwanazi was really confused. And he did not know what I was talking about. And I think one of the things he asked me was, how did the docket even get to Pretoria? And I remember him testifying, saying that when he realised he was in with General Khumalo, because he was in the know. General Mkhwanazi was in that meeting on the 5th of March in Parliament. So my assumption was that he had always known about this process. So I was really shocked by his response, which was that he did not know.

Adv Mkhize: Perhaps for the sake of clarity, he did not know about the dockets being transferred to head office on the 27th of March, or he did not know about the dockets now returning to his office. What was he confused about?

Lt Gen Senthumule: First, the dockets are in Pretoria. And secondly, the dockets have to go back to KwaZulu-Natal. And if I may just recall his words, he said that he would not accept those dockets. I think the first thing he said was, like, he really felt affected. I think passion showed to say that, yeah, no, General, [2:22:00], and I am saying, you know, I mean, he actually is the actual docket custodian. He did not know that his dockets had been moved to Pretoria. So that for me made sense. And then secondly, he said, he does not, he will not take the dockets, even if we bring them, he will not be responsible for them.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, you were responding to what was his confusion and where you left off is at paragraph 61, where you said, “waze wangimoshela ilanga General”, and you were explaining where the confusion emanated from.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Him being the actual docket custodian in the province, he is the number one cop in the province, was not in the know that the dockets were removed from the province to Pretoria. I remember testifying, sitting in the chair, him saying that he was angry with General Khumalo as to why he moved the dockets. That was the first part. And then the second part was when he said, I will not take the dockets. So he refused to take them. And then thirdly, he said, even if you bring the dockets to KwaZulu-Natal, I will not be responsible for them. So that is the PC basically saying that if you bring them, you will work with them, work on them with your people, and I will not get involved.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, did it not raise concern that, for a period of almost a month, we are now at 25 April 2025, the dockets had been transferred on the 20th, at least you became aware that they were now at head office from 27 March. So roughly speaking, a month had lapsed. And, is it not concerning that a provincial commissioner responsible for dockets, as you said, he is the custodian of the dockets, had no knowledge that 121 dockets had left his office?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I was concerned, and I think one of the things I did was to apologise to say I was not aware. And I think that is where it dawned on me that actually the process was flawed because the PC should have been the first person to be informed if he was not involved in the process. So the dockets, as they said, at head office on the 27th of March, for a period of a month, basically, your evidence is that they were, they may have been improperly transferred.

Lt Gen Senthumule: If the PC was not involved, if the PC was not informed, and I think I did say in my statement that for me, the whole process was suspicious.

Adv Mkhize: What would have been the status of dockets that belong to another province under a particular provincial commissioner being at the head office without the knowledge of the provincial commissioner? If, in fact the process was flawed, as you have testified, what would this, what was the status then of the dockets for which you were busy implementing a disbandment plan for?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I want to give an example of a recent incident of yesterday, where I got a call from a province requesting that we are actually the family that was refusing to work with the police in the province, and it happens a lot of times. So we intervene, and we can then look for a team that will take the docket over. It could be at the national level, it could be in another province, but it is done together with the province or the provincial commissioner. So, dockets are not moved without the knowledge of the provincial commission. I think about two weeks ago, we had a similar matter where we experienced a security breach in one of our critical meetings on operations. And we had to engage the province, based on the security breach, our mitigating action would be removing the dockets from the province. So it is for me, your standard practise of how we move dockets.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, once you became aware that there was something quite flawed about the process of the transfer of the dockets and you were now tasked to be responsible for their transfer back to KZN, in essence, you were dealing with dockets whose status you were not certain of. What did you do? Did you perhaps take any steps to find out who actioned the transfer of the dockets? Because they are now sitting in your office.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Khumalo and I were the responsible people. And I think where the process was flawed, not only with us, but with the entire value chain, was the deliberate exclusion of the provincial commissioner. And I can safely say that if that did not happen, these committees would not be sitting. Because the process, as it unfolded, was flawed. And my first action after General Mkhwanazi was not to call the DNC because I already knew what his sentiments were around the dockets for them being in Pretoria. I called the national commissioner first to say, General Masemola, I engaged with General Mkhwanazi, and this is his feedback on the dockets.

Adv Mkhize: Did you engage the national commissioner only telephonically, or was there anything in writing?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Telephone.

Adv Mkhize: Did you not think to perhaps follow up with something in writing because of the seriousness of what you were dealing with at the time?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I did not. I think I acted on my feet at the time. Probably, I think as an operative, I opted for strategic distancing.

Ms Sangoni: Sorry, Advocate. I see, it seems like you are moving from this issue. Lieutenant General said the deliberate exclusion of the provincial commissioner. What does she mean by that? Because she said both she and General Khumalo were responsible. So did you deliberately exclude the provincial commissioner?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would not say so. Now, if I look at the events, as I said earlier, I was not there in the beginning. So my thinking would have been that the PC, when that letter was disseminated, would have been informed. But realising that actually, he was not. And for me to be bold enough to call him on something that he was not aware of, then I realised, I think we made an error in judgment. All of us in the value chain.

Chairperson: You blundered.

Lt Gen Senthumule: We definitely blundered.

Ms Mathys: Mine is also just to follow up on Honourable Sangoni. Do you think we can actually consider this just a blunder? Like, outside of it, it is also being procedural that the provincial commissioner, I mean, he literally, I do not know. Well, in his statement to us, he found out that these files were moved after they had already arrived. And the way you describe this whole process, none of you, even outside of following the procedure, just as a WhatsApp, we have been told to take your files. I found that the most bizarre thing that can happen is just to reduce it to a blunder. I am not sure because even the members, and we also asked the lieutenant general this question, even the team that put the files together did not tell him they were coming to collect the files. There is something that someone is not telling us because it is not possible that someone would have not even, even if it were procedural, that it did not need to be included, that someone would not whisper and say, yeah, you better follow up on the files. It is not possible. And perhaps you can tell us outside of a blunder because there has to be another explanation, Lieutenant General.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think I will stand by the fact that I am not sure whether I can link this to a lack of proper communication or maybe the haste of the process, but in essence, we were not supposed to. And when I say all of us, from the Minister, national commissioner, the DNCs, and ourselves, we were not supposed to work outside the provincial commissioner. I assumed that, as he was in parliament at the time when this was discussed in that slide that General Khumalo presented, he was in the know. So that has always been my assumption. And realising that on the day, actually, probably for me, the sirens rang to say, we really made an error in judgement.

Ms Mathys: So the deliberate reason would be what? To exclude him. Because if you are saying it was just an oversight, you did not really answer that. What was the deliberate reason? Because you cannot use the word deliberate exclusion and then say that it was just a blunder.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I gave examples of us moving dockets between provinces, where even the provincial commissioner would be engaged. When I say deliberate, I was not there when the process started. And one of the things I said at some point is that things should not change because the principle has changed. Things should not change, or how we do our processes should not change, because we have a new principle. And I think for that reason, one of the people, if I go back to 2018, I think there are minutes to this effect, and I think they've been declassified, where General Mkhwanazi would be the one to introduce the topic around the PKTT, then General Khumalo would present progress on the dockets to the IMC, which would be the ministers and the DGs who would be there. So for me, it makes operational sense that the starting point, even with the Office of the National Commissioner, should have also included the PC in the process.

Ms Mathys: Maybe we must just ask you, do you think there was a deliberate act from the minister? Because you came in with, now that the principle has changed, the processes should not have changed. Do you think it was the Minister who indirectly said, "Let us exclude him from this process”?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would not speak on what the Minister thought, but the Minister gave an instruction. It is up to us, the bureaucrats, to implement.

Mr M Mncwango (IFP): Well, thank you, Honourable Chairperson. In fact, I am trying to understand, because I am also perturbed by the manner in which General Mkhwanazi was excluded. There was a time when Major General Mgobhozi was actually spoken to, forewarned that the files would be coming his way, and that he should actually be prepared to receive them. Apparently, he was not around, and Brigadier Kunene was informed. He expressly said that he was not prepared to accept those files unless Lt Gen Mkhwanazi is actually informed. So, why was he not informed, even at the advice of Brigadier Kunene?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I can take you back to what I said, that when the handover was supposed to take place, I intended to go to the PC. The only reason Kunene was engaged was because of my workload, and I could not be able to go to the province. So, exclusion did not start there. This was way into the process, but he should have been involved from the beginning of the process. And I gave an example of the previous IMC sittings where General Mkhwanazi led the discussions on the PKTT, and I think all of those are outlined in the minutes of those meetings. So, you can clearly see the significant role that he played in the process.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, at page 87 of the bundle, which is the slide that was presented before the Police Portfolio Committee on the 5th of March, you have testified that General Mkhwanazi was present in that meeting. And when you dealt with your evidence in respect of that meeting, you said it would have started the day before, which is the presentation on the 5th of March, and then the following day, you met with the Minister regarding the implementation plan. Before I ask you about this presentation, was General Mkhwanazi also present at the meeting on the 6th of March?

Lt Gen Senthumule: No.

Adv Mkhize: Okay. Then it is limited only to the slide. When you look at the slide, what you testified is that Lt Gen Khumalo, amongst other issues that he was raising before the Police Portfolio Committee, because it was not a meeting limited only to the disbandment of the PKTT. However, as it was a current issue that was ongoing, he presented on it. And you furnished us with the document as set out on page 87 of the bundle. And that page, basically, even though it does not detail what was canvassed at the meeting before the Portfolio Committee, it deals with the topics that would have been presented regarding the PKTT. The first one is that the Minister of Police issued a directive to the National Commissioner regarding the disestablishment of the National Political Killings Task Team. The second one, various meetings had been held and a detailed progress report submitted to the Minister of Police. The third one that was dealt with is the disestablishment or the disbandment, which will take the form of a transition from the National Political Killings Task Team to a component of serious and violent crimes, murder and robbery unit within the Division Detective and Forensic Services. And lastly, the transition will ensure continuity of cases, particularly those that are under investigation and those that are in court. Now, General Mkhwanazi was present. Lt Gen Mkhwanazi was present at this meeting on the 5th of March.

In your understanding, if you can comment, of course, it is not he that is here giving evidence, but in your understanding, what was this about? What was this meeting before the Portfolio Committee, which dealt with these various aspects of the implementation plan, of the dockets being removed from KZN to Gauteng, and of the transition of the cases from one division to the next? What would you say his understanding of this presentation would have been, which was made by Lt Gen Khumalo? And this is in relation to his surprise, specifically his, you know, that he was surprised that these dockets had now left.

Lt Gen Senthumule: The meeting was online, and I remember General Khumalo, who was actually at his office, but all of us were here in Cape Town, we were sitting in the PC’s [Provincial Commissioner’s] boardroom. And at some point, General Mkhwanazi was asked by the Portfolio Chairperson to also give his remarks on specific matters around crime intelligence. A final feedback report on the finalisation of this process, including confirmation that all task team members have reported back to their station of origin, is awaited on or before Friday, 25 April 2025 and signed.

Adv Mkhize: What was your reaction to the letter? We are at paragraph 68 of the statement.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would like to take the committee back to the meetings of the 6th of March with the Minister and the 27th of March, where a plan was presented. And also, there was no mention, if maybe I can go back, if I may go back, I think the DNC took it further, and there was no mention of the plan of the closed dockets and dockets in court. The plan said the dockets in court will remain with the team, whilst the dockets closed will go back to the station of origin. So, the 297 plus 50-odd withdrawn cases have already been taken back to the station of origin. Now, this instruction says even those dockets must come back to the detectives. And again, in that particular plan, which we presented, the strength of the members was reduced from 96 to 68. And this correspondence says that all task team members must go back to their stations of origin. So, it was contrary to what the Minister and the commissioner deliberated with us on at both meetings of the 5th and the 27th. That is why no action was taken on this letter.

Adv Mkhize: We are now at paragraph 69. Can you confirm the physical location of the PKTT documents from the point of their collection onwards? I think you have dealt with that, but just in case you would like to, perhaps deal with your actual paragraph 69 in case we left anything out.

Lt Gen Senthumule: It is just to submit to the committee that at no stage were those dockets physically in my office. And as I said, as generals, it is not practise that you keep a docket in your office. The dockets are first in the custody of the police station and the provisional commissioner. And only when we recall dockets for specific intervention together with the team. Can you recall? So, those dockets were never in my possession or actually in my office.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 70, you deal with the meeting as it was called by Minister Mchunu on the 23rd of June. What transpired at the meeting of the 23rd of June regarding the PKTT disbandment, and why did you decline further involvement at this stage?

Lt Gen Senthumule: On the 23rd of June, the Minister invited the national commissioner to provide him with information on the disbandment of the PKTT. General Masemola extended the invite to only General Khumalo and General Sibiya. General Sibiya then asked me to join him in the meeting. I mean, we respect protocol in the police. And I think I respectfully said that the invite was not extended to me. And therefore, I would not be able to attend. If the National Commissioner wanted me to be there, he would have included me in the invite. And he did say that if I could come up to his office to assist him in writing the report, which I agreed to. And upon arriving, he was sitting with General Motsepe already compiling the report. Later on that day, we had a meeting with the Minister and with BACSA to present progress on our partnership. And therefore, I had to excuse myself. But I must say that at this time, after having seen that, actually, it is more like now the ball is in the detective's court. So I actually had no interest in getting involved in the matter. Hence, you will see the post the 25th of April; after talking to NatCom, you will not find any footprint of mine in the dockets.

Adv Mkhize: Thank you, General. That concludes the section that we were on. And now we move on to your views, which you have titled your attitude toward the disbandment. We are now at section G of your statement, which starts at paragraph 71.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think in starting this part of the statement, my workings with Minister Mchunu, I must say it was amongst one of the best I have ever experienced of a political principal who dedicated his time working on improving the detectives and also trying to get SAPS to automate SAPS to do things in a way that will make it easy for the victims of crime to report and also have a better feeling when they go to police stations. And I think in this regard, one of the biggest programmes that he did report on this one, where he then said we must have a plan on how to capacitate the detectives. The re-enlistment that we have, I think we are given 353 posts emanating from that submission to the Minister, which he approved. Today, we are sending 287 detectives re-enlisted. I think the 66 remaining; we are now headhunting. So for me, that is one of the good things that came out of his initiatives. And then secondly, the issue of the SAPS, I think BACSA, I spoke about that. And again, the expansion of your forensic science laboratory footprint, particularly the CSIR. The CSIR has offered subs, some of the old labs used during COVID, that we can use for DNA analysis, particularly your reference samples, which are actually your buccal samples or your intelligence samples. So those good things that he did for me really said a lot about progressing the police. But I must say that even on his exit, Minister Cachalia and General Masemola are proceeding with those projects. So it is a positive thing.

But again, I must say that in relation to his disbanding the political task team, I want to go back to the IMC and use, maybe for lack of a better example, this committee. There is a chairperson who is actually overseeing the structure. He cannot decide to say, but tomorrow you cannot sit. For example, he cannot say General Senthumule cannot come and present herself because the committee must meet, deliberate and agree. And I think, as it started, that is also how it should have ended. There would not have been anything anomalous for the Minister to call an emergency IMC to share his concerns around the PKTT, and that to be deliberated. And then I am sure that decisions would have been taken. But at least it would have been a consultative process with his colleagues, who are part of the JCPS cluster.

Am; If we can actually just read your paragraph 73, you have made an analogy, but without context to the people who are listening to you, who are not only limited to this committee. Perhaps if I can, then read it into the record. Lt Gen Senthumule: I think those are very strong words, so I would not necessarily agree with them. And I think that is why, when we talk about integration, it means that we then bring all our brains together and produce an amicable solution that will advance the organisation.

Mr Malema: I think the question is, did the Minister have a business to do with the disbandment of PKTT or not, according to you? Because that is what we are investigating. It does not mean that because the Minister is no longer there, the initiative does not exist. Because remember, it is a government initiative in dealing with a specific topical problem of crime, and ours is to enter into a normalisation and stabilisation phase through these task teams.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, we are now at, because you have now dealt with the IMC aspect of the step, the step that the Minister could have taken by approaching or calling an IMC meeting. At paragraph 74, that is where you deal with that. At paragraph 76 of your statement, you stated that this is, that you stated that the disestablishment of such a team is a complicated process. Can you explain why, and what are the potential consequences of not handling it properly?

Lt Gen Senthumule: If I may respond to this using an example, decommissioning a team cannot be done in one day or in one week. Because within that team, you have diverse competencies. You have got; we have five pillars. So you would have crime intelligence, you would have your visible, proactive policing, you would have your combat, those are the people like the TRT, the NIU, those who actually protect the teams when they go to investigate, but also do your tracking and tracing. You have the detective, that is the investigation or the reactive element, and then you have communication. So, to decommission means that all these teams must cease to exist. Now, in relation to the detectives, when you intervene, you generate dockets or investigations. And by the time you decommission, those investigations could still be ongoing. I want to give an example in one of the provinces, I think it happened I think last year, where the team was decommissioned or actually reduced. However, because the team carried 200 and odd dockets, we then had to request through the NCCF, which is the National Crime Combating Forum, for an extension. So it disrupts the actual ongoing work. That is why you cannot disband. It is a complex process that involves a lot of other role players.

Adv Mkhize: At paragraph 77 of your statement, you have, in part, dealt with this elsewhere in your testimony, but I would like to address it here. I am more interested in asking the latter question. You highlighted that the deliberate exclusion of the Provincial Commissioner of KwaZulu-Natal in the disbandment process, you have dealt with why you have. The question was, why do you say he was excluded? But my question before you now is, what was the impact of that exclusion, if he was in fact excluded?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Need I say more? I am sitting here today to account to the public on what happened. Thank you.

Adv Mkhize: General, if you can just perhaps expand on what you mean by that.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I am basically saying that the exclusion of the PC led us to where we are today. Had the PC been involved and part of the process, I think the outcome would have been different. And I am not saying this because of General Mkhwanazi. I am saying this because it is the process in the police that when it comes to moving the dockets, the PC needs to be engaged. So I do not want to attach it to a person, but to a process. Thank you.

Chairperson: Yeah. So we are going to give them until 13:30 to conclude. Can we go ahead? Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, we are now at paragraph 78 of your statement. Lt Gen Senthumule: Before I respond, Chairperson, I would like to put that this is in relation to the four Orlando dockets, no, three Orlando dockets and one of the letter dockets that were requested by Adv Johnson from General Jacobs to actually go to IDAC. So, to start with, the testimony on the 6th of December 2024, at 15:08, I received a WhatsApp call from Adv Johnson, who requested, and I want to underline my intervention in relation to IDAC dockets, which were kept in General Jacobs safe, which will be the Divisional Commissioner Inspectorate. And I think I pause to mention at this point that at the time this call was made, General Jacobs was the appointed Chairperson of the NATJOINTS committee on ESKOM. That was investigating Maleficent that affected the performance of the utility. So in my mind, these dockets had to do with state capture because the specific mandate of IDAC, as outlined in section 28 of the NPA Act, is that they will deal with serious and complex economic crimes. And I think the first thing was, why would General Jacobs keep the dockets in his safe? And this is again per protocol. I have got an annexure here that comes from his environment that says this is not the practise that we promote. Generals cannot keep dockets in there. Even if we work on them, we must give them back to the investigators. And I am not necessarily saying that he did something wrong. So my engagement was specific that, as the head of detectives, I had to intervene to allow IDAC to do their investigations. And bearing in mind that neither General Jacobs nor Adv Johnson specified what these dockets were all about. One of the provisions in that section says that it is a criminal offence for the director of IDAC to actually inform people about investigations that they are doing. I think that is all I would like.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Based on these two letters, then I engaged General Jacobs and informed him of the request for those dockets. And I need to mention, Chairperson, that I am not General Jacobs' supervisor. In fact, in terms of the rank, he is my senior. Because he was appointed way before me. And my thinking was that they could even charge him for defeating the ends of justice. And again, at this time, neither of them, not Jacobs, not Adv Johnson, specified what those dockets were in relation to. And they were not bound by law to do that.

I only got to know that these dockets were in relation to the address of General Khumalo when General Mkhwanazi called me. I remember I was from overseas on the 26th of July, Saturday. And asked me if I knew that these dockets were in relation, or actually, these are the dockets that led to the address of General Khumalo and others. And I was shocked. At that point in time, I felt the need to then inform the National Commissioner to say, actually, this is the email that came from Adv Johnson. At that time, I, if I may mention it here, advocate that the National Commissioner looked at the time at which the dockets were requested and said to me that this was at the time when the PKTT team was conducting a takedown operation at Katiso Molefe's home. So he drew that inference, but we did not discuss the matter further.

My biggest problem is that it was said under oath by a senior official of the South African police services. General Sibiya is correct, because my back had pins, I could not sit properly. But I do not think it is something that for me he should have ridden on as a man with a badge of honour. An allegation that he just threw from nowhere to say that Mr Matlala paid for it. In 2023, Honourable Sangoni, I had not even seen him at the time. And it is paraded on the global platform to say this general who represents this flag that I wear every day. I wear this every day because I represent my people. When I go to Abu Dhabi, and I sit, I represent South Africa in terms of diplomacy. I am not going to be sorry for what I do to look good. And maybe also juxtaposing General Sibiya with Brigadier Matjeng, I do not even know what Ozempic looks like. You can see Honourable Sangoni, I think I was built for show off. I was not built to be normal.

I do not know what Ozempic, for this thing to be paraded and my name to be attached to it, is rather gravely unfortunate. Lastly, I want to request the committee and the world at large, because I have been getting calls from as far as Paris, from as far as Tanzania, people asking what is going on, that you see these allegations for what they are, with an intent to damage me, my reputation, and also a deliberate attack on my credibility and integrity. I submit, thank you.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General, thank you for clarifying. I would like to now ask you to just deal with the final part of your statement. I will ask you just one question, and you can speak to that question.

Mr Malema: Just before. Now that you strongly feel that Sibiya lied under oath, as a Lieutenant General, what have you done about it? Because you know lying under oath is a crime. What have you done about it?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Two processes that I am pursuing. One is legal, and the other is criminal. I think I will provide feedback to the committee based on my engagement with my counsel on the two matters. Actually, I am mentioning the fact that he is a senior officer, because in my capacity as a woman at that level, the ones who are coming after me should not be afraid that their colleagues will chastise them for things that they think are better for themselves. Thank you, Honourable Malema.

Adv Mkhize: Lieutenant General Senthumule, the last part that I would like to deal with in your evidence, in your statement, is relating to the allegation that you authorised and approved overtime claims for Warrant Officer Monageng and Warrant Officer Segodi in May 2023 without verification that the protection services were allocated or rendered, thus failing to exercise due diligence in ensuring overtime claims were supported by objective proof that was rendered. Do you have any comments on that statement?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think the video that I got said I employed ghost workers, and it is on TikTok. And I am thinking, really now, I need to clarify this, Chairperson. In early 2023, the National Commissioner informed me through a telephone call that there was a member, whom I would call Captain M, in my division, Detective and Forensic Services, whose life was under threat as he was a witness in a corruption matter in Forensic Services. And this member required protection. One of the things that happened to the member was that his kids' pictures were taken from school and were sent to him as a threat. And I think he experienced issues from that. Then he was hospitalised. These people are known; they took the pictures of him and said to him that they would find him wherever he is. For that reason, I was then asked to ensure that Captain M is protected. In this regard, General Khumalo, who was the head of CI and General Mohlala were engaged. A TRA was conducted, and General Mohlala provided security. So those two members were attached throughout the four months to Captain M. There is evidence in this regard. The member's wife also could be engaged, Honourable Malema, if I think we need, what do we call it? A confirmatory affidavit. So the security was for them. One of the recommendations from the TRA was that they be relocated or put into a safe house. However, because the wife is working around Pretoria and the kids go to school in Pretoria for that reason, the wife felt that the security that is being provided was enough. So these members were deployed around Captain M 24/7. There is no way I signed a claim for overtime. The only claim I signed for overtime is for the members who are sitting behind me who are working in my office. However, for my office and those members, I then signed an overtime application. Before you have overtime, you need to be authorised. So that is the only thing I signed. And the fact that General Sibiya then went to open a case of fraud and corruption with no suspect, I think that is what led the matter to be a problem. The initial commissioner was engaged, who actually encouraged us to talk. I think to date I have not received any report from the Independent Police Investigative Directorate (IPID). I did ask, I think on the 26th of February, and I was told that the matter is under investigation. But as far as I know, I never employed any ghost workers. And also in terms of my work, I will never abuse my office. I think for those who know me, they will know I do not abuse my office for any reason. Thank you, Chair. I submit.

Adv Mkhize: The evidence, the supporting documents that Lieutenant General Senthumule has provided in support of what the submission she has just made, the evidence she has given can be found on page 137 of the bundle until page 163 of the bundle. Thank you very much, Chairperson. This concludes the leading of evidence. Thank you, Chair.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Maybe before we conclude, I actually listened to advocates saying that we should provide at least recommendations. Maybe my second point will be noted that this also leads to institutional buffering because we do not work. We sit and listen to this deliberation, particularly if they are going to affect us. I think in my recommendations, I said that SAPS ought to use this opportunity to shake the baobab tree because obviously, I think there is chaff in the baobab tree. And I think this will be for resetting and regeneration. And in other words, what I mean is that we should take a bold step as a country that has significance, consequence, and repercussions for those who are found to be on the adverse. And definitely, we can set up the process for SAPS reforms and reposition SAPS as a credible organisation, trusted and respected by all. So, in other words, this just should not be a process, but it should reset how we take SAPS forward. As a person who was involved in the NSF project, we did research, I think with Minister Sotyu at the time, where we found that actually, within SAPS, reforms did not take place; it was only integration. So maybe we need to then say, what is SAPS in serving our people in this democratic dispensation? The second one is that we should leverage the private sector business ecosystem.

As SAPS, we do not have resources. So maybe again, if we rebrand ourselves, I think we will get more support from the private sector for us to be able to fulfil our mandate. The issue of institutional buffering. Honourable Mathys once asked a question about vetting, and I am surprised she did not ask me today about vetting. Maybe let me address it now. When you get the vetting certificate, you are vetted against a particular function that you are performing. What makes you vulnerable or puts you at risk? But that certificate does not give you integrity. Integrity comes from within yourself. Integrity is not defined by declaring that I have integrity; people see it. So, in dealing with this problem, I think we need to reset counterintelligence. Honourable Kohler Barnard, at some point, said that this is an intelligence failure. If counterintelligence is strengthened in the way it should be, particularly both offensive and defensive, that will then help us to deal with problems proactively than for us to be where we are today as an organisation. I submit, Chair. Thank you.

Ms Sokanyile: Good afternoon, General. I will go straight to your statement. In paragraph 26, you indicate that, if looking at your statement, you say that subsequently, still in January, and after your discussion, January 25, after your discussion with Lt Gen Khumalo, whilst in his office, Lt Gen Sibiya also enquired whether you had engaged Lt Gen Khumalo regarding the transfer of PKTT case dockets to your office. Lt Gen Senthumule: When General Sibiya spoke to me, I took it that he assumed that General Khumalo spoke to me, because at the time he said this, he had not spoken to me, so this was the first time that he was raising the matter. So, what went through my mind was that maybe he was following if I understood what General Khumalo was saying, and if I was going to comply with that request to General Khumalo. Because, remember, the initial instruction was General Khumalo must give me the dockets, but not to me, from General Khumalo to myself.

Ms Sokanyile: Okay, but reading further to your statement, the next paragraph, you are saying that as much as yourself with Lt Gen Khumalo, you have talked about this informally, but you did not want to indicate to Lt Gen Sibiya that you had talked. Why? Why did you not say, no, we have just talked casually about this? Why did you not want to, as if there is something you wanted to say that in this one, I do not want this one to know. To me, it seems it is like that.

Lt Gen Senthumule: If maybe we will read it again. Actually, I did not hide that I spoke to General Sibiya, and I did respond to him that General Khumalo spoke to me informally. Informally means that there was no official attachment to the discussion because he had a letter, and I did not. And I think for that reason, I then asked that if he wants me to act as General Khumalo received a letter, he must do the same to me.

Ms Sokanyile: Then in paragraph 29, paragraph 29, you indicate that according to the Deputy National Commissioner's letter sent to you, that letter is an annexure HKS4. So, the directive by the Minister was based on an assessment which concluded that PKTT no longer made a significant contribution to the broader policing effort. Are you aware of any assessment that has been undertaken on the work of the PKTT, which is maybe looking at their performance, that it can be even pinned down to say that there was an assessment?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Honourable Sokanyile, and I think I did testify to this earlier, that no assessment was made available. There was just a mere mention of the assessment. And I think in my response, I even said that the IMC would have then had to look at that assessment. So, as far as I know, there was no assessment conducted in this regard. And if there was, we never received it. I personally never saw the report. Thank you.

Ms Sokanyile: If an assessment may have been done, would you say that assessment was done by the Minister or by the DNC?

Lt Gen Senthumule: An assessment is in the purview of the operational heads. So, for example, as we provide progress to either the ministers or the national commissioner, that is an assessment of, for example, if you are intervening on political killings or let us say contract killings, in the assessment, we are saying that the threat has been stabilised or the threat requires us to continue with our efforts. So, it is not the Minister who then provides an assessment. It is actually the operational heads and probably led by the national commissioner who then provides the assessment to the Minister, to the ministers who then deliberate. And based on that presentation, they then make a decision whether the threat has been stabilised or we can proceed with the interventions.

Ms Sokanyile: If the Minister has been given a report, a particular report by a particular general to say that they are no longer needed, this PKTT, then would that be an assessment that the Minister can talk about?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I do agree with you. It could be an assessment, but it would still require discussion with all the relevant role players. Thank you.

Ms Sokanyile: Because to me, if I am given a Minister, you come here as head of particular areas, you give me a report assessing the situation that on that corner, it is like this, then I will have a conclusion or a recommendation to say, if we are saying like this, do not you think there was someone who have given the Minister a briefing about the performance of the PKTT?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Honourable, if I may say, let us say that happens, and the Minister is given a briefing. The Minister still has an obligation to call all the operational heads to say, I have this assessment in my possession, and this is what it indicates. Can we engage the author to clarify to us how they arrive at the conclusions? Or maybe we can engage and get ideas from the floor on how to proceed with the matter? I do not think there is anything wrong with an assessment, but I think how you take it forward, it cannot just be General Senthumule doing it. I will have to call my component heads for us to look at it and see how we can take it forward. The most significant part that is said in the statement is that it is no longer making a significant contribution to the broader policing efforts, because that talks now to organisational direction moving forward, meaning that you do not prioritise this matter anymore. So, definitely a discussion is required for something as monumental as the statement. Thank you, Honourable.

Ms Sokanyile: In paragraph 32, you refer to the National Commissioner's proposal of a winding-down and disbandment plan for the PKTT, and further in paragraph 33, you indicate that it was not feasible to implement the handover process within seven days as instructed by the DNC. Were you agreeing with the winding-down approach?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I was agreeing with the winding down approach because it made operational sense.

Ms Sokanyile: Why did you think it was not feasible to implement the handover process within seven days then?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I explained earlier, Honourable Sokanyile, that to disband or to decommission, you have to take into account other processes. There are five competencies involved in any task team that we have, and that is why I would call it a one-stop shop. So, if you disband, as you actually do your interventions, you generate dockets, which will lead to investigations. So, by the time you decide to disband, you need to assess how far those investigations are. If, for example, some of them are ongoing, you definitely cannot say you are going to disband. You will then have to give the investigators more time to investigate, place the dockets on the court roll, and, upon conclusion, you can assess. I think like you had proposed here to say, once the investigator is done with an investigation, then he can go back to his place of origin or place of origin. So, for that reason, it is not possible to just disband a structure like that.

Ms Sokanyile: In paragraph 39, you are saying that during the deliberations regarding the implementation plan on the disestablishment of the PKTT, you indicate that the DNC was not pleased with the approach of keeping the court's dockets with the PKTT, arguing that would imply the team was not disbanded. Further, the National Commissioner and Lieutenant General Mosikili were not in support of the DNC's approach, but rather supported the retention of the court-going dockets with the PKTT, as the handing over of those dockets would adversely impact the court processes. Were you not concerned about the displeasure of the DNC with the implementation plan?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I am not sure if I said this to this meeting earlier, but in this process, we are guided by what is operationally sound and correct. It is not about the sentiments of the individuals. It is about how we serve this process moving forward. So, I was not as much concerned, because at the end of the day, as an investigator, you would know that it is not an easy thing to do, to take a court-going docket, which means already it is on the court roll to give it to someone else. After all, it actually affects other processes, including your chain of custody. Thank you, Honourable.

Ms Sokanyile: Okay. In paragraph 42, you indicate that the DNC insinuated that, if I can quote you, I was now like Lt Gen Khumalo, and you were dragging your feet in implementing the Minister’s instruction. What was your view about this comment that you made, that you are now like Lt Gen Khumalo?

Lt Gen Senthumule: For me, it was a matter of forced compliance, to say that if you do not comply, it means that they will, maybe the Minister himself and chief of staff, will then conclude that I am also anti-implementing the instruction. That was my reading of it.

Ms Sokanyile: Does it mean that even Lt Gen Khumalo was anti? Because here, the DNC says you are lying. Lt Gen Khumalo. Then you are saying that it would mean that you are anti. You did not want to, in other words, you did not want to implement the Minister’s directive. So it means that Lt Gen Khumalo was seen as a person who did not want to implement that directive.

Lt Gen Senthumule: It was the DNC's assessment, and that is why he was insinuating that way.

Ms Sokanyile: So it is the DNC who had that about you and Lt Gen Khumalo. Meaning that you are in one cabal.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I want to submit to this house that this process has actually pinned the police against each other, and I think we are seen to be either for or against. Yes, and as I said, for me, the truth and the process should prevail. It is not, and I did say it is not about General Mkhwanazi; it is not about General Sibiya, it is about what the correct procedure is for us to go through things. And unfortunately, I think as you respond, then immediately you are attached to a person. From where I sit, I think I serve the organisation.

Ms Sokanyile: In paragraph 46, you indicate that after the meeting of the 27th of March 2025, you were upset, and you remember asking the National Commissioner why the dockets should come to you. The National Commissioner responded that it was because you are the head of detectives. Is it your view that the dockets were not supposed to come to your office? Lt Gen Senthumule: And I wish to state that I think my response was emitted in the spirit of that moment. It is about a process. But I think in that spirit of the moment, you actually wish you did not have to find yourself in this particular position.

Ms Sokanyile: You just put them in the safe and told them.

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, they are being investigated. Thank you very much, Honourable Sokanyile. Thank you very much.

Mr Nqola: You say there was a February 2022 meeting that extended the term of the PKTT's existence?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes.

Mr Nqola: Are you aware if the Minister was actually made aware of such a resolution?

Mr Nqola: Yeah, I am asking because the Minister testified here and said the PKTT was not supposed to have extended beyond 2022. In a nutshell, saying it was now irregular beyond 2022. What is your take on that?

Lt Gen Senthumule: My take is, again, if he says it was irregular, it should be backed by a meeting with a decision that then said the team has managed to stabilise political killings in KwaZulu-Natal. And I think as a structure together with the DGs, including the commissioner, then they would have said that there is no further requirement for the PKTT. I gave an example of a project in one of the provinces where that assessment was reached, and a decision to decommission was taken. Mr Nqola: Okay, so you say you have solicited a threat analysis against a police officer who was testifying in a high-profile corruption case. So was this the complaint against you at IPID? Was it related to that?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It was related to that.

Mr Nqola: Who was the complainant?

Lt Gen Senthumule: General Sibiya. I think he said that there was fraud committed in my office because these members, actually, were said to be protecting him. And another allegation was that I am surveilling him using these same members. I remember I spoke to NATCOM to say, Are you aware of this particular matter? And the National Commissioner said to me, yes. So I told him, but are you aware that this is the matter about Captain M? And he said, no. I said, " Yes, General. So, for me, I do not know where the misunderstanding was in relation to that.

Mr Nqola: Do you think it was a misunderstanding?

Lt Gen Senthumule: For me, it was. Because for me, General Sibiya had just come into the organisation. And I think in matters like these, executives, let NATCOM call us and then discuss. And instead of, I mean, I work with you directly. And how do we then move forward? You open a case against my office. There is no suspect. You say that the suspect will pick up as they investigate. So maybe as a person who actually was pro-teamwork, I felt really offended. And I thought that it was unfair.

Mr Nqola: Do you think IPID has been weaponised? Yeah. Sorry if I put you in a corner. I am asking because we have received numerous allocations about IPID since we started this.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Maybe I can respond to it this way, Honourable Nqola. I actually think that the law enforcement agencies could be weaponised. Not only IPID.

Mr Nqola: No, I am specifically asking about IPID. Because we have received numerous testimonies that are directed to IPID.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Can I take it to me personally? I have never had an incident where I probably had an issue with IPID being weaponised.

Mr Nqola: Do you think that your current case at IPID was a genuine complaint against a police official, or that General Sibiya is trying to weaponise IPID to fight battles between you and him? Just be frank. We are here for that.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would not say no, but I would not be informed.

Mr Nqola: So, there is a possibility that there is a weaponisation of IPID?

Lt Gen Senthumule: For me, there is.

Mr Nqola: Okay. Thank you. So you were dancing at a party with Mr Matlala. He testified here and said he only went to... Is it the same party as the engagement of General Sibiya's son?

Mr Nqola: Okay. When you were invited, you were invited to what? That there was... You are hereby cordially invited to attend this and that.

Lt Gen Senthumule: There was no formal invite. He just said he has a ceremony at home and he would like us to come.

[5:30:39 – 5:30:43]

With General Sibiya, he is a very good operator, you know, but he can change today. Let me start from the beginning.
