Then, later on, General Khumalo does not come. Now I am invited, you know. So you would think that actually you are working, and then suddenly, he changes. Now this is where tradecraft comes in. Then you need to, I mean, employ a defence.

Mr Nqola: What makes you uncomfortable with General Sibiya?

Lt Gen Senthumule: His ever-changing mood, and I think his approach to you as a person, you know, and the fact that today you can be in his good books and you are working well, and then tomorrow, suddenly, something happens, and you would not know. He would not engage and say, General Senthumule, I am concerned there are other issues.

Mr Nqola: You attended a number of Sibiya's parties.

Lt Gen Senthumule: As I clarified just now, initially, I was not a guest, but when things turned south with General Khumalo, then I became an attendee.

Mr Nqola: But you always ensure that there is a company with you when you attend.

Lt Gen Senthumule: You know, I am not one to mix work with pleasure.

Mr Nqola: Then why do you come to parties? Because parties are a leisure activity.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes, in the spirit of building a team, we were involved in crime detection. We, I mean, General Khumalo and General Sibiya.

Mr Nqola: So you were detecting crime at parties?

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, definitely not, Honourable. It was just in the spirit of building a team and supporting a colleague.

Mr Nqola: But a colleague you are uncomfortable with, and you are not trusting.

Lt Gen Senthumule: And I would never be comfortable with. I have never been comfortable with.

Mr Nqola: Okay, Divisional Commissioner, Detectives and Forensic Services. Do you accept that it was highly irresponsible of you to facilitate a transfer of documents you do not know what they contain?

Lt Gen Senthumule: No. In this environment, we work on a need-to-know basis. And I think when it comes to certain entities, when a case is opened at a station, it is opened on the subsystem. However, for DPCI and IPID, they already have functions on the system where a docket can be moved over to them. But with the Investigating Directorate Against Corruption (IDAC), it is only now that we are working on that particular, I mean –

Mr Nqola: So you mean it was your responsibility to transfer dockets to IDAC without knowing what they contained? Even if it had been a treasonous act.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I could be a suspect in those dockets. And I think it is not about IDAC. It is about the principle of need-to-know. If I need to know, somebody would tell me. But normally, let us say my son is a suspect. My husband is a suspect. So sometimes, and I think I have learned from intelligence, if you compartmentalise, if you need to know, you definitely would need to know. And again, if you go to Section 28, particularly –

Mr Nqola: But General Senthumule, you did not even know how many dockets about which we are talking.

Lt Gen Senthumule: There were four.

Mr Nqola: You said there were four, but you later discovered that there were actually six or nine. I do not know. Seven? So, do you now accept that it was irresponsible of you to transfer police information to a different institution at NPA without knowing the number of the dockets taken, without knowing what is contained in that docket?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would remain with my answer, Honourable, that in my capacity as the DIVCOM, I did not have to know the contents of the dockets. And transferring dockets, for me, it is not about police information. It is actually law enforcement information. So if IDAC writes it down and says, these are our dockets, I am not the one to say, oh –

Mr Nqola: Where were these dockets kept?

Lt Gen Senthumule: They were in General Jacob's office.

Mr Nqola: Safe?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In his safe, yes.

Mr Nqola: Where do you normally keep dockets?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Dockets are kept at a station.

Mr Nqola: Why were they in a safe at someone's office?

Lt Gen Senthumule: And I think that that is what General Jacobs –

Mr Nqola: Are these dockets the dockets of Honourable Adams?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I checked yesterday.

Mr Nqola: So it is Honourable Adams' case that was kept in a safe?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes.

Mr Nqola: Do you think that he is justified in thinking that you were sabotaging his cases?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Not his cases. He was not the I.O. As you can see, the dockets actually belong –

Mr Nqola: He is the complainant. That is the point. He went to open cases at three police stations. He went to... And he says to us, he was testifying. He is still going to testify today. He was testifying that you... He has gone to three police stations on the basis that he felt that the police of South Africa are sabotaging him. That is the reason he even went to the extent of going to IDAC. And you come here and confirm that the dockets he opened a case on at police stations, where he kept them in a safe of someone's office, not where dockets usually get kept, at SAPS.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I was not part of any sabotage, and I think I would not accede to that. And I would agree with you. And I did say this over and over again that dockets are not supposed to be kept in general's offices.

Mr Nqola: Okay. You had your medical operation on the 16th of October 2023. On the same day, in your bank account, you received R13500, and you received R70 000. What was the source of that money? The same day?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It was not receiving. Let me clarify. Those statements mean it is money that has gone out. As I explained, I gave all the evidence to the evidence leader. Part of the money came from my police bonus. That is the R13 500. You can see from the statement that the amount is more. But when that money goes down, the amount goes down. But on the same day, from my credit card, I transferred R80 000 into my African Bank account. And on the same day, that money went into my checking account. So the R70 000 you see is the money that came into that account. Then on the same day, it went to pay Dr Pert. So there is a trace of the money. It never came from anywhere.

Mr Nqola: Dr Pert is the responsible doctor?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes. She has passed on. God rest her soul.

Mr Nqola: She has passed on. Oh, may her soul rest in peace. Lastly, do you know the extent of the relationship between Mr Matlala and General Shadrack Sibiya? You work together. You party together. You mingle together. You drink together. You dine together. You dance together. Do you know the extent to which they are close?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Can I say this and say this to the whole world? I do not dine with General Sibiya. I do not dance with General Sibiya. I do not go anywhere with General Sibiya. Yes, maybe there were those parties, but for me, it was not like a personal thing that we are friends. I would say the same. There were lots of people in General Sibiya's house. I mean, you can ask. And sometimes we would sit with them, with the national commissioner. We would actually wonder, but who are these people? You know, because there would be a lot of them. But I do not know, and I have never seen, even on that day when Mr Matlala was there, I did not see General Sibiya talking to Mr Matlala because, I mean, he was there. It was in everybody's view. So, I do not have a comment on that.

Ms Sangoni: General Senthumule. We have become very sceptical in this committee of praising people. Sometimes it backfires. I think the one person who is still dedicated to it is Honourable Sauls. But I do want to put it on the record that we have seen a number of generals from the South African Police Service, many of them women. And I think that it is something that we should really acknowledge and applaud, especially just looking through your CV. I think it is a very distinguished and impressive career record. So I wish you more strength in the work that you do, and thank you for the service to the country. I was a bit late this morning, Chair, so I am not sure if General Senthumule had introduced those who are accompanying her. Okay, can I just ask, why is Major Van Rooyen not here? She has accompanied most, if not all, of the Lieutenant Generals.

Lt Gen Senthumule: She actually was with me throughout. However, remember, the sitting was for last week, and she is travelling today with her family on holiday. She will not even be here when the National Commissioner appears.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, so it is not like you are dumped.

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, actually, during Madlanga, she sat with me throughout the consultation, I think up until yesterday, she was with us. Maybe I can also add that she even texted me to say all of this.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, no, that is fine. You have been acting as the DNC Crime Detection since 1st August 2025. Is this Lt Gen Sibiya's post? Were you appointed when he was? Is he suspended? Okay, who was DNC before Lt Gen Sibiya?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It was General Ntshinga who retired. Okay. And before her, General Tsumane, who also retired.

Ms Sangoni: Now, how was General Sibiya appointed? Because at some point, you were all on the same level, right, as DIVCOMs for the different components.

Lt Gen Senthumule: In fact, if I can just clarify, when General Sibiya came into the police, I had just vacated my office as head of organised crime. That post became vacant. So, in his victory based on the court outcome about his employment with SAPS, he came into that position. So in essence, he came and reported to me.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, and then how did you come about to report to him? In the police, there are Lieutenant Generals, and the DNCs are actually your super DDGs. And all of us are DDGs. Then there are Major Generals. So the National Commissioner and the Minister can take somebody at the Major General level, if they feel that that person will suit the post, then that person is elevated. It could be over you.

Ms Sangoni: Is there a process, and I am asking this because, I mean, we heard people complaining about how General Mkhwanazi was made an acting National Commissioner. So, was there a process that was followed to move General Sibiya from head of organised crime to a DNC?

Lt Gen Senthumule: We have a policy, actually, it is part of our directives, which is called Section 45. So a National Commissioner can approve an elevation of a person without advertising the post. I think based on the Minister's approval.

Ms Sangoni: Is it effective? Do you not have fallouts amongst yourselves on that thing? Because it means I just look at you, I like you, and I decide you must be DNC.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think that the downside of it is exactly what you are saying, because it could be open for abuse.

Ms Sangoni: Is it open for abuse? And has it been abused now?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I have never actually implemented it or been near the process, maybe where I became the applicant for somebody. So I would not know, but I can safely say that it is open for abuse.

Ms Sangoni: And it specifies criteria, no?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It does specify. Remember, there are requirements for a person to occupy a certain position. So there is a motivation that is forwarded to the Minister for his approval.

Ms Sangoni: Dr Pert, your doctor. And please, General, do not ever stand up and twirl for anybody. You have a right to do what you please with your body. But I am interested, is this Dr Pert, who is your doctor, the same Dr Pert that Tsakane Matlala was Dr Pert's mother's driver?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes, that is what General Thema told me when she introduced Tsakane. Because I already knew, Dr Pert is General Thema's friend. I think they have come a long way. So in our interaction, I knew Dr Pert. And I think I have been, I think there was a situation where one of her employees passed already. And I think I had to accompany Sandra to the memorial service. So that is the same Dr Pert, yes.

Ms Sangoni: So Ms Tsakane Matlala was a driver?

Lt Gen Senthumule: That is what General Thema informed me.

Ms Sangoni: How did you meet Dr Pert?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Through General Thema. They were friends. They stayed in the same estate as well.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, let us move on to issues of the PKTT. So now, were you –

Lt Gen Senthumule: Sorry, Honourable. I think I did say that General Thema is prepared to actually depose an affidavit on all the issues that you want to clarify.

Ms Sangoni: No, that is fine. I suppose for me, it is like such a coincidence, right? Well, firstly, I did not know Mrs Matlala was a driver. And then it is a coincidence that she just happens to be a former driver of the mother of the doctor. The ministerial directive, were you surprised by that directive when you eventually got to know about it in the letter of the 4th of February?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think it only surprised me when General Mkhwanazi was not in the picture. I said that when we were in parliament on the 5th, we were with the Minister on the 6th.

Ms Sangoni: No, but when you were told that the Minister said disband PKTT, what went through your mind about that?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It is an instruction. I understood the history of the PKTT because I was part of the process. But if General Sibiya says it is an instruction, however you feel, you have to comply. And not only to me, but General Khumalo has also given the same instruction.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, you said, I am quoting you here, to one of the questions that the honourable members, you said you understood the instruction. This is when the Minister was unhappy; you were not undermining his authority. But you then also say he had no mandate or authority to disband PKTT. So which one is it? Because you specifically used the words, I understood the instruction, and I was not undermining his authority. I suppose I am also asking this because somewhere else you say the letter then legitimised the instruction for you. So what is your understanding? I mean, you were busy carrying out this instruction. Why now would you then suddenly say he has no authority?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Where the problem arose was when I engaged General Mkhwanazi as the ultimate custodian of the same dockets that I have in my possession. I think for me, that is when I realised that the process was flawed. For example, General Mkhwanazi was involved. We could have continued, you know. But the fact that he now says, how did they get there? And if you bring them here, I will not be responsible for that, you know. And I did say that for me, it becomes-

Ms Sangoni: If General Mkhwanazi had been involved. Then, you know, like the national commissioner, you were of the view that the Minister could issue the directive. He had no problem. The national commissioner's problem was the how part of it. And your problem was the exclusion of General Mkhwanazi.

Lt Gen Senthumule: If he were involved, he would have probably okayed it because he was consulted. At times, the PC would not necessarily come and sit with us at the head office. But if he is in the know, he would okay the process.

Ms Sangoni: How did you forget General Mkhwanazi? Because you and General Khumalo were running this process. You said you agreed on the steps to be followed. Would you not, in drafting your plan, have General Mkhwanazi as part of the stakeholders you must consult?

Lt Gen Senthumule: As I said, at the meeting of the 5th of March, General Mkhwanazi was in attendance. So I assumed that he had already been briefed. And remember, I was on leave when this whole process was started. General Mkhwanazi, General Khumalo, General Masemola, General Rabie, we were meeting General Sibiya. So when General Mkhwanazi was in that meeting, in my mind, I felt that actually he was part of it. Until I spoke to him.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, I am sorry to be rushing you. I agreed on 20 minutes here. Before the Madlanga Commission, Lieutenant General, I think it was Sibiya, says that you expressed the view that the PKTT was untouchable. And you alleged that, in your opinion, the task team was being used to syphon the budget allocated to it. Can you just give us your view on that? Did you express concerns about the PKTT and the budget?

Lt Gen Senthumule: On this, I would like the committee to hear me for what I say. General Sibiya, throughout, he would actually really, and I said that he was actually strategically recruiting, wants you to agree with him. And I think in that discussion on that audio, I actually employed what I would call your counterintelligence defensive approach to say, I am going to feed him whatever he wants, disinform him, because it was no help. Why am I saying that? Because if you look at what he said, and the documentary evidence, the following day, I called General Mkhwanazi, and I went on with business as usual. So for me, he wanted to corner you, to be able to say, yeah, no, this is wrong. So I thought, do you know what, I am done with this guy.

Ms Sangoni: [5:53:43] that he would even go to the extent of recording you and taking it to Madlanga?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I do not know. And I actually feel that I do not want to get into the pigsty, you know, with him. And I want to grow, be above the situation. I think my conclusion even had a quote about forgiveness, to say, maybe he needs forgiveness. An English saying is that hurt people hurt people. You know, maybe I hurt him with my testimony at Madlanga. Maybe he actually assumed that, actually, I was working with him.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, just for the record, then, General Senthumule, are you saying that you reject a notion that says you do not believe that the PKTT was genuinely engaged in effective policing? Do you disagree that they did not deliver any meaningful investigative outcomes? What is your true reflection of the work of the PKTT?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I believed in the work of the PKTT. Hence, even today, I still work with them. As I said, it was part of my counter to say General Sibiya must be off my back. I mean, there was a point where I said, redeploy me, you know? So for me to survive, I thought, okay, let me handle him this way. But my feeling is still that I never expressed in any document that I do not believe in it. I never even signed their request for an extension for detectives.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, why do you think General Sibiya was putting pressure on you to get those dockets? Did you ever ask him why he was so eager to obtain these documents?

Lt Gen Senthumule: He is not a person you can ask if his mind is made up; he is adamant. So for me, there was no reason to ask him.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, I think Honourable Nqola has already canvassed a lot of the issues around the IDAC. I just want to know how those documents get to General Jacobs’ safe.

Lt Gen Senthumule: From what I gather, he took them from the province, from General Khumalo's office, General Mbuso Khumalo, not Dumisani Khumalo, the DPC, and was given to Colonel Phillips, I think, then to General Jacobs, and he kept them at his office in his safe.

Ms Sangoni: Why?

Lt Gen Senthumule: You know, General Mkhwanazi, when we were discussing this matter, even General Khumalo himself, we asked ourselves as to why. It is something to which I cannot respond. And as I said, there is no police –

Ms Sangoni: I am going to push you because you are the supreme leader of all dockets in the country. If something like this happens, and I mean, you have had a bit of time now, we need to know why General Jacobs took those dockets.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would not know why, but I can tell you that it was not practise. Hence, I even asked his own division to put it down right and to say, even in their line of work, they are not encouraged to actually take the entire docket. I can give examples where, let me say now, during the PKTT investigations, when Colonel Khumalo and the other members are looking for linking dockets, we request the province, and we write there that they must give us copies of the dockets, not the original dockets.

Ms Sangoni: So he takes these dockets, we do not know why, then he gives them to Colonel Phillips, then he puts them in his safe. All of these are breaches. What has happened to General Jacobs since all of this has come to light?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would like to start this from the fact that when the docket story was discussed, my assumptions were these. When I think of IDAC, I am thinking state kept, I am thinking Zondo Commission, I am thinking ESKOM, I am thinking Transnet. I was assuming that these were those dockets, but I only learned later that actually it was based on a complaint to the Minister and national commissioner from Honourable Adams to say that these dockets were not getting [5:57:40]. So that is how he got involved in the process. Hence, I said, I do not think he did anything wrong, save to say he was not supposed to put the dockets in his safe.

Ms Sangoni: Okay. Through you, Chair, can we request that SAPS provide us with a comprehensive report on how these dockets have ended there and also consequence management for what is clearly a breach. My last two questions General. You testified here that Adv Andrea Johnson called you after, I am not sure if after the testimony or... Is it usual for her to call you, and what did she have to say?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Adv Johnson and I worked together for more than 10 years. We have chaired the CIT committees, we have chaired committees on organised crime, and we have attended training together. So we actually do not necessarily talk, but we would interact. And after her testimony, I just shut down because I felt that two minutes of engaging with her became the centre of her testimony. So, one of the things you wanted to know is, why am I quiet? And then I clarified to her that, actually, as you are testifying here, these are the issues, and I felt that you were supposed to clarify. For example, when you get a docket as a prosecutor, you go through a diary, and you see what is contained or actually done on the... You are not supposed to say no, but SAPS are supposed to check. I mean, this is a chain of custom things about which you are talking. So the reason she came was to understand why I was shutting her out.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, but in truth, Adv Johnson threw you under the bus and from your testimony today, it is clear that she lied as well to you and to this committee. Did you raise that with her, and what did she say?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I raised it in earnest to say she was supposed to submit a clarifying affidavit to which she agreed.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, which we have not received, I suppose.

Lt Gen Senthumule: And actually, she promised that before I appear, she would have submitted it.

Ms Sangoni: I am sure there is no clarifying affidavit. Vhonani? No. Okay, maybe then, finally, General Senthumule, given everything that has happened, and I heard how passionately you replied to Honourable Sokanyile that you are not part of factions, will this management of SAPS ever be able to work together again? Are you seeing yourselves at least trying to find one another? Thank you for putting in some recommendations, but I think I am also worried about the human and the leadership element that we have. Will you be able to work together, and what do you think needs to be done to ensure that that happens?

Lt Gen Senthumule: My approach to the question would be based on why we are employed by SAPS. Why is there a government in the country? Why are there government departments? How do they synergise, and how can we deliver a service to the communities? When we serve, we do not serve at our behest. It is actually at the behest of the people of this country. So how we feel or how we see each other should not be a determining factor in our moving forward. I think for me, we are here for a reason, and if, for example, some of us do not belong here, then let it be, but if we are not progressive, we actually affect even those who are coming after us.

Mr Skosana: Chair, when I raised my hand earlier before we started, I wanted to make a follow-up on what Honourable Malema has raised with you to say, unless things have changed, normally we used to have two members from us, from the ANC, from MK, and others, they will have one. Wait, Chair, wait, Chair, please.

Chairperson: Honourable Nomvalo has spoken to me.

Chairperson: I understand what you are saying.

Chairperson: Yes, that approach is still there.

Mr Skosana: Good afternoon, Lieutenant General. I just want clarity. The first one, you said that the National Commissioner and the Minister appoint a person to a post following Regulation 45, am I right? But that is wrong. I think we must agree on that one because that's political interference, because the Minister is not empowered to perform any administrative function, any human resource function, am I right?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Honourable, yes, you are correct. And I said that the Minister approves. So the National Commissioner applies, and the Minister approves. And it is not only the paradigm that the Minister approves. There are a number of things that we do where the National Commissioner becomes the applicant, and the Minister approves.

Mr Skosana: At what rank, if I may ask?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think 45, if I am not mistaken, goes throughout all the ranks. So it is not tied to a specific rank. I have seen it being applied by people who were, for example, colonel-level, who will become brigadiers.

Mr Skosana: So the Minister is empowered to do that? Actually, the National Commissioner is empowered. The Minister has to approve. And I think it is all about concurrence.

Mr Skosana: So if the Minister does not approve, what happens?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Then it does not happen.

Mr Skosana: So you will not be appointed?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Because I think there is, if I am not mistaken, I am not really afraid of it much, but the Minister has to concur with your senior appointments. Though even if he does not, the National Commissioner can go ahead and appoint.

Mr Skosana: Hence, I am sitting on that one, to say, as far as my understanding is concerned, that the Minister cannot stop even if he does not approve the appointment by the National Commissioner. Thank you. And I wanted to ask a quick one before... The issue of this SARPS National Inspectorate, which does your internal oversight division, I mean, it is a division within SAPS. Does it still exist?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In the new structure, it is not there. However, functionally, it is still there.

Mr Skosana: I am asking you because there are a lot of problems with police stations around the country. And I think you know that. And I think they have dropped the ball. Because the complaints which we get from the Portfolio Committee as well, and as members of Parliament, it says that it does not exist at all. Let me start now, as well. I just wanted to find out the clarity on these matters. And thanks for the clarification. And I hope that in the future, others will know exactly what is happening within the police force. And the police service, there is no more force. When did you first become aware of this allegation by Lt Gen Mkhwanazi regarding the interference in the organised crime investigations? And what immediate step did you take upon learning of them, if any?

Lt Gen Senthumule: On that 6th of July briefing that he had, it was the first time that I got to learn about those allegations. And I also wish to submit that at the time, I had reservations that probably could have been done differently. But I think upon realising and also seeing what is coming out of this, I actually saw an opportunity for the organisation to redefine itself. I can imagine if it had not happened, where would we be right now? So if maybe... I normally say to people that the badge, which is the police badge, decides who to kick out. And I think in this process, my analogy was probably that the badge got tired of the rot, and the moment had come to begin to purify the organisation.

Mr Skosana: So you reckon that the ocean cleans itself?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I do not think so. I think it requires people who can go in and begin to remove the charge.

Mr Skosana: So in other words, you agree with what Lt Gen Mkhwanazi has repeated on the 6th of July by exposing all these issues and raising them for the nation to understand the problem which we are encountering as a country.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would say, as a person who did not know, now that I know and I have seen, I think I would agree with what she did.

Mr Skosana: Now, as an acting Deputy National Commissioner for Crime Detention, do you confirm that the units that investigate crime ultimately fall under operational oversight? The organised crime units ultimately fall under your operational oversight.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes.

Mr Skosana: Do you agree on that? Now, are you happy with what is happening in the organised crime units?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Can I also talk about organised crime in general within law enforcement, including DPCI? I do not think we have actually scratched the surface. Yes, we do have takedowns. We do have successes. But in my view, we are not dealing with the people whom we would call in organised crime high flyers, the people who cannot explain their income, the people who cannot explain their assets. Many times, we would deal with the low-level people. I think that is why we are not necessarily succeeding in neutralising organised crime.

Mr Skosana: Now, my follow-up question on that, do you think General Sibiya interfered in that organised crime unit?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In what way? Sorry, Honourable Skosana.

Mr Skosana: What I am saying, as you said yourself now, that we have not scratched, you know, the question now would be, we have not scratched the surface as yet. Do you think there was any direct or indirect interference by General Sibiya when he was heading the detection?

Lt Gen Senthumule: To actually agree with you would mean that General Sibiya is the alpha and omega of organised crime in the country, and he is not. Maybe the element of what we see now, but I think broadly, we can all do better. It is not attached to one person. I mean, this is just a drop in the ocean, but I think that, actually, the organised crime issue is a problem. That is why it is prioritised as well.

Mr Skosana: Yeah, hence why I am asking about General Sibiya. It is not personal. It is more about accountability as a head of the, I mean, the deputy national commissioner responsible for that. Hence, I am asking whether you feel that there was any interference by him?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think that the only time I can speak with certainty is when there are findings around all the allegations, but I think for now I would not be able to comment on that.

Mr Skosana: At any stage, did the Minister of Police communicate with you formally or informally regarding the investigation on operational decisions related to the matters raised by General Mkhwanazi?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Not at any stage.

Mr Skosana: Were any investigations into organised crime, political killings, or syndicate halted, slowed, or reassigned during your tenure as an acting deputy commissioner? And if so, who authorised those decisions?

Lt Gen Senthumule: If we are talking, for example, dockets around your PKTT, I will start with some of the dockets that we actually had to move. The DNC had an outfit in his office that was conducting investigations, so those investigators had to go back to their respective units, and those dockets were reassigned. Depending on the nature of that investigation, it was assigned to the respective responsible unit according to that commodity.

Mr Skosana: You know, I am trying to find if there was any operational obstruction, whether from IDAC or IPID, not necessarily IPID, IDAC in particular, or within the units at SAPS. So you say there was nothing at all.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Not that I know of.

Mr Skosana: Now, can you point out any factual inaccuracies in General Mkhwanazi's public briefing? And if so, what is the documentary evidence you rely on in case there is any? I just want the evidence of your opinion on this one.

Lt Gen Senthumule: General Mkhwanazi gave out allegations, and I think it is up to the investigating arms who are responsible and have been tasked with this particular matter to be able to see what is factual and what remains allegations. But beyond that, I cannot make an informed opinion.

Mr Skosana: Are you satisfied that the current command structure within crime detection has protected investigations from political or any internal interference?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think for me, until we actually conduct maybe a climate assessment, understanding the impact this has had on policing, then we can actually dissect the areas affected. And then we can see if some of the insulation has been compromised. Only then can we say that we can be able to heal what has happened.

Mr Skosana: In preparing your affidavit, which operational reports, investigation files or official correspondence did you rely upon? And can you confirm they have been provided to this committee? You have submitted a very comprehensive document with some attachments.

Lt Gen Senthumule: All those came from my environment, safe from the one that came from legal services in relation to the IDAC matter, as well as the inspectorate. But also particularly on the supply chain, I relied on the national instruction.

Mr Skosana: If any organised crime investigation were compromised or interfered with, or would not that fall within the oversight responsibility of your office as an acting deputy national commissioner for crime detection?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Certainly.

Mr Skosana: So you take the accountability for that. What measures, if any, were taken by the office to protect officers who reported alleged interference in the investigation before these matters became public?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think I spoke at length in relation to Captain M., but also we have had other officers where we would ask for security, particularly when they conduct their investigations. One of those is to deploy a combat team to all complex investigations.

Mr Skosana: You said that you have engaged Lieutenant General Jacobs and informed him about the request from IDAC. I think that's Adv Johnson, as you said, she is going to send us an affidavit. Requesting IDAC for the dockets in his position. You said you requested that Lieutenant General Jacobs hand over the dockets, as he is not supposed to interfere with the criminal matters because he will be charged with defeating the ends of justice. And you said that you submitted at night that Lieutenant General Jacobs and Adv Johnson gave you any details about the dockets. You only knew about the dockets in relation to the arrest of Lieutenant Khumalo. So that was the first time that you knew about this matter.

Lt Gen Senthumule: On the 26th, and I think it was a Saturday, I came back, I think a day before, outside the country. I think I was in Tanzania. General Mkhwanazi called me. That was the first time that I knew that those dockets actually were responsible for the later arrest of General Khumalo.

Mr Skosana: So the arrest of General Khumalo was not about the lady who was recruited from BMW?

Lt Gen Senthumule: From what I read in my discussion with him, it was in relation to that. But as I said, I did not know that, actually, those dockets were the ones which were responsible for the investigation.

Mr Skosana: So as we speak here, General Mkhwanazi and the other members were arrested for these dockets or were they arrested for being sitting in that panel for the appointment of Brigadier? I do not know.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yeah, the arrest of General Khumalo, and in my discussion with General Mkhwanazi, it was about that matter of sitting in a panel. But in that list, there were dockets of General Lushaba and General Madondo, which I also learned afterwards. So it was General Khumalo. It was also the senior managers from CI.

Mr Skosana: Now, you only became aware that the legal opinion was obtained and signed off by the divisional commissioner, Inspector, on the 11th of December 2024. There was a post that requested my intervention by IDAC. And after I engaged with the divisional commissioner, Inspectorate, the one I asked about earlier, you have attached the annexures here. Now, when there was your intervention, what was the response from Adv Johnson?

Lt Gen Senthumule: As I said, Honourable, mine was just to facilitate. So they had to get the dockets. And I think once they received them, she said to me, we have received the dockets.

Mr Skosana: Now, you spoke vividly about the issue of the police when Honourable, I think, Honourable Sangoni asked you now to say, you said that perhaps it is time for SAPS to reset, you know, because it looks like there is a function within the top echelons of the South African police service. Do you agree?

Lt Gen Senthumule: When I say there are factions, it means that I am saying that we are actually using our functional powers to perpetuate differences or wars. I have seen generals who actually walk the talk, and most of them, you would not even find Honourable Nqola at parties. You know, I would not say there are factions. Yes, there are differing views, but I think in this particular instance, maybe there were two worrying people who fled to people defining the affections.

Mr Skosana: Now, my last two minutes, because we do not have data today. This issue, you know, of Paul O’Sullivan, what is your take? Has he interfered in terms of how SAPS should run, whether it is top echelons, whether it is IPID, whether it is IDAC, or the criminal justice system as a whole, like at the NPA, we found that he was paid R100 000 instead of being paid R23 000 for whatever service, you know, the DPCI. Any of you, what is your take? What is the role of Paul O’Sullivan in this mess, which we find ourselves in as a country?

Lt Gen Senthumule: First off, he is not part of law enforcement, and in my day-to-day engagements, over a period, I have never engaged with him. So, what you hear panning out is also what I hear. In my space where I work, I have never had any situation where I had to deal with him.

Mr Skosana: Now, you heard him saying that he is opening dockets.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I heard that.

Mr Skosana: And taking it to the police to sign.

Lt Gen Senthumule: That would be irregular from whoever would allow him. I think there are processes for how we register dockets, and civilians are not allowed. But he said it himself that he was doing that for all these years. He took one docket to Mandla Mahlangu from IDAC as well. But he has been doing that for many years. And it was boastful when he was sitting here to say that he opened it. And I asked him several times, and I was shocked that the police have allowed him to do that to be the law unto himself because he is not a policeman. And I am saying, as SAPS, what are you going to do with that? Because you know that now.

Lt Gen Senthumule: We have engaged legal services. I think the matter is currently under discussion. As I was not there, I think the better part of last week. So I will only hear from General van Rooyen when I sit with them at the National Commission.

Mr Skosana: And speedy recovery to your son. What you said earlier here is that your son, unfortunately, because of what happened around you, and you know, our kids are innocent. They do not deserve to be affected by all our mishaps in life and all our trials and tribulations. And may the good Lord help him to recover as speedily as possible. I thank you, Chair.

Chairperson: Honourable Sauls, do you want to comment on the last remarks that Honourable Skosana has made?

Mr Sauls: Chair, the Bible says if two agree on earth, it shall be so. I want to agree with...

Chairperson: May you say, may you say, may you say, hallelujah, Honourable Skosana. Thank you so much. Honourable D Kohler Barnard (DA), your time starts now.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Good afternoon. Let me start with the elephant in the room. In paragraph 103, you say that Major General Thema suggested that you should approach Matlala for a donation to the SAPS. But it was surely clear to you that Vusimuzi “Cat” Matlala was deeply involved in serious criminal activities, specifically as a tenderpreneur linked to organised crime and contract killings. Now, we all know that around May 25, he was arrested for allegedly masterminding a botched hit on his former partner, actress Tebogo Thobejane. And yes, you had approached him for a donation a year before that. But even then, he already had a long history of arrests dating back to 2000, including a 2001 conviction for burglary. Now, of course, the 2025 arrest, combined with revelations of R360 million fraudulent SAPS tender and his role in a criminal syndicate, firmly established his status as a major criminal suspect. Now, the question has to be, do you not look into someone before you ask for donations from them? How many times must a person be arrested before the SAPS distances itself from him or her? And where was your due diligence in this instance? Even the smallest entity does due diligence so that they do not find themselves in the headlines as being in the thrall of criminals. And here you were, a highly experienced police officer, highly. We have looked at your qualifications, and you had no idea, and you did not bother to check. I would like to know what the SAPS protocol is in this regard, and how you, at the level you are, have had the wall pulled so successfully over your eyes.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think, as I said earlier, that our over-reliance on the supply chain, I think we are better in the supply chain. At the moment, as I said, it was a hasty request, and I think because of the time, I did not actually see anything wrong. I did not read anything wrong. I did not hear anything wrong. There were no issues that came to my attention about Mr Matlala. So if there was a lapse in judgment for me, I will take that. But I think the supply chain for me at the time seems to say they would then vet if they, Okay, it means the person is fine, or the company is fine.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Just to point out, Google is your best friend. You could have done a three-second search, and you would not have had this issue even being raised. The supplier's accusation that you were not taking the PKTT disbandment seriously speaks to that. What is he on about?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In my view, and I think it will also show with the others, General Sibiya, within crime detection, wanted all of us to actually walk with him on this journey. And I think that there was an instant where he even said to me, and I think I have got WhatsApps that I actually removed, where he said he knows now where I stand. So you are working with a person constantly who doubted you, who did not trust you. So for me to be able to move forward, I was sticking to the process, and I was saying something else to him. And that is for me, as I said, it was part of my operational tradecraft handling of General Sibiya to be able to walk through. Because if you listen to what he said versus the process, you would see that actually, there is a disjuncture there.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Okay, moving on to page 103, there is a reference to CAS 1650/10/2 in which there is a claim one amongst others that Lt Gen Khumalo, for example, instructed other senior members of the service to ensure there would be no investigation into the alleged theft of Major General Lushaba's official firearm, cell phone, and laptop by a sex worker. And it is alleged that the sex worker was paid off using funds from the Secret Service account. Now, the SAPS finding internally was that there was no evidence provided, and there was no charge. In such a matter, is it not the job of SAPS to do a cursory investigation of a matter? Or would they expect, in this case, the MP to investigate the matter personally?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In my view, I thought there was a criminal matter already registered on the same matter. And I think the four cases that I spoke about, one of those was in relation to General Lushaba. When I, after the fact, read some of the summaries, so in my view there is a criminal matter that was registered. And I guess that is why, actually, he was also arrested at some point.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Yes, indeed. There is a criminal matter, but the internal finding was, well, there is no evidence, so there is no case. So you understand they are diametrically opposed. And that, from my experience, is a very typical outcome. Okay, moving on. Now that the PKTT has been disbanded, to acknowledge what has been done in terms of transferring the, I believe it is 96 members of the PKTT to other divisions. Were they being relocated? The plan initially proposed that 68 of them be retained to finalise the 60 cases in court. Of the other cases you list, for example, 43 not guilty, 96 declined to prosecute, nine undetected, and 35 awaiting Constitutional Court decisions. But there were 60 over. There were still, I think, 114 matters that were defined as court-going. So I am trying to reconcile the 60 cases with the 114. Is it possible that dockets perhaps include multiple charges? If you could explain that, please.

Lt Gen Senthumule: The team is still in existence. I can safely say that they are carrying the same dockets and more, because as they investigate, they link some of the dockets. For example, there was one in Musina for a CIT. There was one in Gauteng, for, I think, it was for hijacking. And so more work is coming into their space. We are facilitating that. So the team, I think, as per the action plan and what we did and post-General Mkhwanazi’s press briefing, the team was not disbanded. The team is operational. And Dumisani Khumalo is still the project manager. And those members are still within the team.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Yes, that is what I thought. You said in paragraph 62 that there were deep emotions associated with the dockets from the PKTT. Why do you think that was? Deep emotions in relation to a docket.

Lt Gen Senthumule: In my history in law enforcement, I have had to deal with some of these things; this has been the most emotive matter in policing for those who are for and those who are against. And I think from where I sit, I just wonder, out of all matters that we are dealing with, with a high priority, why under this one? No, but I am not getting any answer, save for what I see panning out in the public.

Ms Kohler Barnard: You said in paragraph 65 that both Lt Gen Mkhwanazi and General Masemola distanced themselves from the issue of the PKTT dockets. Now, surely that is an issue they should have been on top of. I do not understand why they would simply step back or walk away.

Lt Gen Senthumule: General Masemola testified, I am not sure whether at Ad Hoc or at Madlanga, and said that he actually engaged General Sibiya to say that those dockets should not be at the head office. So when I called him, I guess he was surprised as to how the dockets get to the head office. Hence, he said, General Sibiya must see to it that he finishes how he deals with it. General Mkhwanazi, on the other hand, was not aware that the dockets actually were removed from his province to the National. So for that reason, I think they both felt that actually, this is not what we have to deal with. We did not start it. And therefore, whoever started it must deal with it.

Ms Kohler Barnard: But this is the National Police Commissioner. Surely it was his job. I mean, not just say I wash my hands of it and walk off. It seems a bit odd.

Lt Gen Senthumule: He did not say I wash my hands. I think because he gave General Sibiya an instruction to say those dockets should not be at the head office. So, probably his response was based on the fact that I gave an instruction, no action was taken, and therefore, you cannot bring it back to me.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Okay. I see you have great admiration for Minister Mchunu, calling him one of the best ministers you had ever experienced as your political principal, and that he spent much time capacitating the detectives and wanting a plan from you and others, I presume, to further capacitate them and to go ahead with the reenlistments of former detectives, something we suggested in the Police Portfolio Committee some time back. Do you think he would have disbanded what seems by all reports to have been an enormously expensive unit in favour of other entities without giving it due consideration?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The letter that I read from the DNC said an assessment was done. I have never seen the assessment and I think if that assessment were there, I would be able to say maybe he did give a concession. But for now, I cannot respond because I do not know what informed that decision, because it was mostly in the absence of an assessment.

Ms Kohler Barnard: So, your opinion on standing back and looking over this whole debacle, what do you think the real motives were if there were different motives from the obvious?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Minister Mchunu, in my interactions with him, and I think with the Commissioner being there and others, he actually was focused on advancing the organisation. So this side discussion, decision, letter, I do not know what informed it. And I think for the love of God, I do not even know how to dissect it.

Ms Kohler Barnard: You finally referred to how it would have been different if Lt Gen Mkhwanazi had been included in the process of closing the PKTT, which you now have confirmed has never been closed. Why do you think he was excluded from the process if indeed he was? Because I know we saw the movements in the Portfolio Committee, and you brought various reports down the line. But that initial decision, why do you think he was excluded and for what possible reason?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Say up to the time when I spoke to him. My thinking was that the person who is the captain of the pockets is involved. And for me, the process should not even start without. I referred to the previous minutes, which I said to Honourable Mathys, could be asked from the National Commission, where General Mkhwanazi would be the one who would lead the discussions on the PKTT. So if you disband such a structure, at least the least you can do is to be able to refer back to me.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Thank you, Chair. I am done.

Mr Klopper: Thank you, Chairperson, and good day, General Senthumule. How are you?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Good afternoon, Honourable. I am great. How are you?

Mr Klopper: I am great. Thanks. I would just like to thank you first for coming here to assist and for considering what your family is going through. It is acknowledged, and it is appreciated.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Thank you very much for your consideration.

Mr Klopper: I will start with a few direct questions, General, because we are here because there have been many allegations of widespread interference in SAPS and other institutions within the criminal justice system. You earlier said that there is this narrative that you are either here or there in terms, and I think we were speaking about factions at the time, and sitting here day and night, it seems to be, or at least for myself, that there is a glaring split. And I am not sure if I captured you just earlier on. You said that there are no factions. I just want to hear, are there factions or are there no factions? Just categorically, so I hear you clearly.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I have not observed any factions in the police.

Mr Klopper: So there are no factions?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I have observed people in conflict, which will then spill over to other people. I am not sure if you define it as a faction.

Mr Klopper: Yes, you know, General, I think I ask this because I think it is very important, because if there is a split, these individuals that are causing the splits, are they just for personal gain? Because there can be no ethical justification for splitting SAPS into camps and divisions. Do you believe that SAPS have been infiltrated or improperly influenced by any individual organisations? And I want to clarify, I mean any individual, whether it be “Cat” Matlala, whether it be ministers, whether it be ex-ministers, whether it be politicians.

Lt Gen Senthumule: When I look at the police, I look at it like I look at supply chain, where supply chain tenders would drive people's relationship with service providers. In the police, the dockets, and other environments like the supply chain, would attract the wrong people to people who are in power. And so for that reason, I do believe that there is a possibility of infiltration. Maybe the scope of which we will then have to further investigate.

Mr Klopper: General, you are very high up in SAPS. Are you? You are very high up in SAPS. I mean, we are not, you are not on the street. You are a very senior officer. Are you saying you just think it is a possibility, or do you know of anything and you are just not willing to speak of it publicly?

Lt Gen Senthumule: My issue would be on providing evidence because if I say for a fact that yes, there are factions, then I must say, no, sorry, yes, that is influence, then I must say, where did it happen? Who was involved? So at this point in time, I do not have a safe way to understand what we see playing in the public space. So I cannot really speak on authority and confirm such.

Mr Klopper: In your belief, do you think the NPA or IDAC has been compromised in any way?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Again, it is based on what we hear. I have worked with IDAC. I have worked with the NPA, and in my tenure, I have not come across such incidents where I would feel uncomfortable because I would feel that they are captured.

Mr Klopper: Do you think IPID has been infiltrated in any way by any individual or any organisation?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think my response to that earlier question was that, actually, all law enforcement agencies are susceptible to infiltration. So, IPID is not safe either.

Mr Klopper: Did you only receive the written response from General Sibiya on the 4th of February? And why do you think there was a delay in the correspondence from General Sibiya getting the documents and the instructions to you, considering his testimony that he perceived it as immediate disbandment, immediately? Why do you think it took him so long to start getting those documents to you, and then still left out documents like the letter from the Minister and so forth?

Lt Gen Senthumule: My reading of General Sibiya's instruction was that he actually spoke to General Khumalo in my absence when I was on leave. But he expected Khumalo to give me the dockets. And when Khumalo spoke to me, he had an instruction; I did not. And I think I made it clear that I will not really start a process without anything in writing. So whether it took fast or slow for General Sibiya, but eventually he also had to instruct me as the receiver of those documents.

Mr Klopper: Do you think General Sibiya understood the instruction incorrectly? Do you think General Khumalo dropped the ball, giving you the instructions late?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I do not think so.

Mr Klopper: You do not think which one? Do you think General Sibiya misunderstood the instruction that it had to be so hurried? Or do you think General Khumalo was at fault for not giving you anything?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think neither. Because General Sibiya, when he gave General Khumalo the instruction, it was specifically for him to give the dockets to me, but I was not engaged. So for me, it is neither, nor for both of them.

Mr Klopper: You spoke about the Minister’s letter, I think it was paragraph 29 was based on an assessment. And I just want to make sure that you said the assessment to which he was alluding. And if I understood you correctly, you said you never saw this assessment.

Lt Gen Senthumule: The assessment would have referred to probably our efforts yielding results or not, and determining whether the project should proceed. So that would be an assessment that would inform him to say, according to this report, there is no longer a need for the structure.

Mr Klopper: This assessment was given to the Minister, am I correct?

Lt Gen Senthumule: According to his letter, he said he had an assessment.

Mr Klopper: So when we asked the Minister when he was here, he indicated that he came to this conclusion on his own on the 31st of December, the night that the letter was sent, that nothing, it was his own mind that informed him. He never spoke of an assessment that informed him up until that point.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Because he wrote the letter, I think then he will be in a better position to say why he even put it in the report in the first place if it was never there.

Mr Klopper: I question you on this because you did mention that you were with you know, with the IMC and so forth from the beginning, you know. That is why I thought you might have a little bit more insight into this assessment, because you were there from 2018 in the IMCs. Yes. So you are saying you are not aware of this, you do not know of it.

Lt Gen Senthumule: In fact, with the witness, I have never sat in a meeting where any PKTT matter was discussed. You spoke about Lt Gen Sibiya, who said that you are just like Khumalo, you are dragging your feet. And you seem to believe that you aligned with General Khumalo, that the approach that you were taking was correct, not as a faction. And the Minister and General Sibiya seem to be aligned in their expectations. So if I may ask you plainly, were you implementing the plan as you should have? And what was agreed upon on the dates agreed upon? And it can be a simple yes or no.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would like to take it further because I want to provide context. For me, the roadmap was based on the implementation plan. Hence, I said it did not have to do with emotions or whoever feels what. When General Masemola was compiling his affidavit in response to General Sibiya, he called us in, and we were sitting. And I said, General Khumalo, but why did you not tell me about these serious things? And one of the things he said was, we did not know who to trust. So for me, being among these worrying people, I do not think Sibiya definitely did not trust me. Khumalo also, at some point, did not trust me. So it makes it difficult for one to function. Hence, I wanted this thing to be over and done with.

Mr Klopper: Can we just pause there for a second? You are saying that General Khumalo did not know who to trust, and you are saying General Sibiya did not know who to trust. So if there were no factions, why would any of the generals have this feeling of trust issues with each other? It is one unit. It is the SAPS.

Lt Gen Senthumule: If we fast-forward back to General Khumalo's testimony, where General Masemola's testimony, both of them, what they said, in January, they gave a report to the Minister. On General Sibiya's activities. That is not a faction. You know, that is a crime report. I do not think we have to emotionalise crime and make it about people, you know. So, sorry. My question was based on whether such important things are happening; we were supposed to be called in and briefed so that we are aware, you know. But because General Sibiya was seen as a suspect and General Khumalo was investigated, you know. But that does not mean that these are factions. Because there are actually issues of criminality that are being discussed. So in my position, I think you said something where now you just have to focus on the truth. Because if you focus on the people, this side says they are targeting me. This side says the other one is not [6:45:54].

Mr Klopper: I think where it becomes a split is when you have many people jumping on a bandwagon within the same SAPS, not seeing criminal activities. And you have a very same SAPS with a whole bunch of people jumping on the other bandwagon despite possible evidence. And I think that is where I am talking about a glaring split. So it is not about emotionalising crime. It is that a whole group of SAPS members seems to back a certain individual, and a whole group of SAPS members back the opposite. And I think that is where the question comes in. But we will pause there. General, did General Masemola have an agreement with all the timeframes and the transfers of dockets, etc? Because you and General Khumalo were running it. Did General Masemola give his blessing and his okay?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The plan was presented to the Minister, and General Masemola was in that meeting. So when I said that it was tentative, they approved General Masemola, who was also in that meeting, where the approach as we presented was approved.

Mr Klopper: When General Khumalo, and I think I heard you correctly, you said General Khumalo started venting in a meeting on the 27th of March. You said it, and the meeting was prematurely ended. This was when 121 dockets were discussed. Am I correct?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It was the Minister, not General Khumalo.

Mr Klopper: Who ended the meeting prematurely?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It was the Minister. All right.

Mr Klopper: So the Minister, did he basically present and then cut himself short?

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, General Khumalo was presenting, and I think with the issue of the figures, which were diverse from what we presented earlier, the Minister thought this meeting could not proceed.

Mr Klopper: Okay, I understand. So General Khumalo was still doing the presentation, and then when you started speaking about specifics, the Minister ended it. You also spoke early on about other matters requiring your time as well as that of General Khumalo, and it is completely understandable. Or were you pushed into a corner and you were told just do this, or you are just as bad as Khumalo, you are dragging your feet? Is that the impression I am getting? If you could just clarify.

Lt Gen Senthumule: General Sibiya does not hide the fact that he wanted those dockets out of the PKTT. I think he did ventilate on this matter here and at the Madlanga Commission. So, I am not moving, actually, I think probably got to him, and he tried to push me in whatever way, even if it was to a point of maybe saying that now I am like Khumalo, I am not listening, you know. So he was adamant that, actually, those dockets must be out of the PKTT.

Mr Klopper: I just want to move because I am just worried about my time. I think it was in paragraph 92.
