Mr Klopper: So you do not know who invited him?

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, I did not invite him. Okay. And maybe can I honourable say this again? People have sat in this chair, and those who know Matlala have agreed. I do not have a reason to say I do not know a person that I know.

Mr Klopper: If “Cat” Matlala was present, did you understand him to be known to General Sibiya? Was there anything at that party that made you feel that they had a relationship? Just from observation.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yeah, from observation, I never saw anyone get close to him or talk to him. He was just standing there. And for that matter, I do not know how long he disappeared. I know he stayed there only for an hour with General Thema, and we left.

Mr Klopper: And then General Sibiya ever questioned you about your connections with “Cat” Matlala before making allegations in the testimony that you kind of knew him, you made that reference. I think it was in paragraph 92 of yours.

Lt Gen Senthumule: General Sibiya and I never discussed “Cat” Matlala before his arrest. And I did write it here in my statement that the only time General Sibiya called me was at night when “Cat” Matlala was arrested. And he said to me, in Sotho, meaning that the PKTT have arrested Sandra's friend. So, Sandra is my friend, General Thema. So I asked him, "Which friend of Sandra? The only friend I know of Sandra that I was with that weekend was Timora. So I asked, " Is it Timora? He says to me, no, Cat. So I asked him, " Who is Cat? And when I put the phone down, I called Sandra. I asked Sandra, " Who is Cat? Then Sandra says to me, " That's Mr Matlala. The one who gave us the sponsor. She says, yes, but why is Sibiya telling me that he was arrested. That was the last time and he never phoned me after that. So we have never discussed Matlala. We have never sat together with Matlala.

Mr Klopper: General, while I really do not want to know about your personal medical procedures, I just wanted to speak to the allegation that it was funded. And you have categorically denied it. You have explained the funds in your account. However, I would just want to find out if there is any truth that you were at the Medicare 24 facility? And secondly, I would just then ask, did you get any special treatment? Did you get any discounts? Did you get anything of that nature, not direct payment, or was someone paid for you? Were there any incentives given to you for any procedure? And I do not want to know about the procedure itself.

Lt Gen Senthumule: As we sit here, I do not even know what the facility of Medicare 24 is. In my line of work, I never had to deal with not only Medicare 24, but I also do not deal with contractors or service providers, save to ask them for sponsorship. And thirdly, I have never benefited from any of it. I do not know what they do outside, what they did for subs. So I have never benefited.

Mr Klopper: Chair, I do not know how much time I have left.

Chairperson: Do you want to ask your question?

Mr Klopper: No, I have a few questions, but if my time is finished, then I will rest.

Chairperson: You will ask your question, and then you will look at me.

Mr Klopper: General, maybe you can just answer it in one go for me. So I hope it is not overwhelming. But if the dockets were removed from the investigators who were handling them, who would have effectively gained operational control of the investigation? And in your professional experience, are the movements of these sensitive dockets sometimes capable of disrupting the investigations? And would that have then been an advantage to any individual to have them actually, I want to call it an orchestrated disruption? I think that is the easiest way I can ask that in one question. Thank you, Chair.

Lt Gen Senthumule: In the value chain of security, the human factor is always the weakest. Where you have human beings and hands, I think things are likely to be manipulated. When we, where I sit, we first take the movement of dockets. We make sure that the investigator who is receiving sits with the other investigator. There is a briefing, then the diary is updated so that you do not actually interfere with the chain of custody, and they stay connected should there be any further interaction. However, as I say, this is not a perfect process. Where there is a human element, people can deliberately then interfere with the process. But again, as I said earlier, I would not have examples where I have dealt with such. Thank you very much, Honourable.

Chairperson: You still have two minutes.

Mr Klopper: As Honourable Breytenbach always tells me, it is a limit, not a target. Thank you, Chair.

Chairperson: Well, she says you must stop. The EFF, who is starting?

Mr Malema: I am starting with just one question, then she will take the rest of my time. Lieutenant General, yourself and General Thema, both of your medical procedures were done by Dr Pert. Do you know, may her soul rest in peace, do you know Dr Pert's husband?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Unfortunately, I do not. I did not have a personal relationship with her that far.

Mr Malema: Dr Pert's husband is an ANC regional secretary in Tshwane called George Matjila. So now it is important that people at your level, especially with your training, appreciate the company they keep because it comes back to bite you. And especially a female lieutenant general who has worked so hard to be where she is gets to be destroyed by association with lunatics. Because if we are to look for a female general or the police commissioner of the police, I think you stand a very good chance. But it is these nuances that are going to cause a serious problem. And people who know how you guys get trained, especially on intelligence and counterintelligence, there is no way. Because when I met Matlala for the first time, I could not miss him. I did not know him. And I asked someone, who is this guy? And they explained to me, this is so-and-so. Later, they said he wants to greet me. I said, no, I do not want. And that has been my stance throughout. You are a human being. You go around, you go to parties, you go to funerals, and you are going to meet all kinds of characters. But the person in opposition must have the capacity to detect that there is something wrong with what is happening here and be able to immediately withdraw. That is why most credible judges, it does not matter how painful it becomes for them, they are not part of a lot of social gatherings because they avoid coming into contact with the wrong people. And those people are going to end up appearing before them. So I thought, because I am not asking questions, I must say those few remarks to you as a Black sister. Please, you can work so hard and have a moment that destroys everything that you have built for yourself. And that's why even children will attempt suicide because they do not know their mother to be a criminal. And now, when she gets to be projected as a criminal, it frustrates all of them. Like, my mother has been this vulnerable person, a woman of integrity, who enforces the law without fear or favour. The next thing stories are told about your mother, it is bound to affect them. So I am just saying, this is not the end of the road. I think this is just the beginning of life. I mean, everything is a lesson. Going forward, you should be aware that, especially as an intelligence-trained person, you should always be aware of your surroundings. Thank you very much, Chair. Over to you, Honourable Mathys. I am in charge now, whether Khusela likes it or not.

Ms Mathys: Let there be love.

Chairperson: Honourable Mathys, wait. Because I am the one who presides over here.

Ms Mathys: We do have a habit of expropriating without compensation. Sorry, Chair.

Chairperson: Honourable Malema presides over the EFF meetings. So now let me tell you what time it is and give you time to speak. 16:32. Your time starts now.

Ms Mathys: Well, my president pretty much covered me, but I just wanted Lieutenant General, just as another woman, to say when I read your CV. Okay, I am getting teary now. I must not pull a Sarah-Jane Trent. When I read your CV, it was like, wow, we actually have women of this level in our country. And we’re always told that there are not enough African women like that. There are also not enough of our young girls who know the spaces that they can be in. So I also just want to allow you to tell how you are also getting teary. Are we going to have a moment? That is nothing. Tears are not a bad thing. Do not worry. I say that in the EFF all the time. I am a cry-baby in the EFF. So, yeah. What made you get into this space? Because you started off and you served a good 10 years at what was then called the State NIA, and now it is the SSA. So what led you there? Was it accidental or was it deliberate?

Lt Gen Senthumule: State security was deliberate. I think it is based on my history, I think, at the university. And I think most of my colleagues who were already there were already there. So it was a conscious decision that this is where I want to join.

Ms Mathys: And then why did you move to SAPS?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Growing through the ranks, I remember in 2008, when I actually came back from... No, 2007. When I came back from the Northern Cape, I started working internally within counterintelligence. And I went to NICOC. Then there was a request that there is actually an opening in SAPS for a director for counterterrorism. And I want to declare here that, Honourable Malema, whatever I do in my life, regardless of what is being said, money's never been a motivator. I think I fought hard to work as a woman. By the way, if I can say on this live TV, I am divorced twice. So I am not a person who craves attention. I think I can hold it, can hold the knife. I am not available, though, but I am just saying that I am divorced twice. So meaning that money for me was never a motivator because when I moved from state security to SAPS, I was already very senior, and I took a salary cut to join the police. And for a reason that most of the guys who were sitting in my interview thought that I was way too experienced, and also, they would not be able to match my salary. So when they gave me an offer, I think as a way to show that, do you know what, I will prove that I am not here for money. I then took the offer.

Ms Mathys: So, what did you think that you were going to achieve more at SAABS than you would at the state security agency? I would think the state security agency is a little bit more elite.

Lt Gen Senthumule: And I think otherwise because I have been there.

Ms Mathys: Yeah, you would know better.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes, in state security, we are talking assessments, we are talking probably estimates. I have seen in the Northern Cape our products contributing to crime fighting, for example, in organised crime. I think at some time, we had an issue of right-wing extremism in the Calvinia area, where you definitely saw your efforts. But nationally, because you are overarching, you do not see where those products are leading, particularly in tackling organised crime. So, my moving to SAPS was about expanding what I have learned and to be able to use it to guide investigators to be able to catch those that we have identified as our targets. Okay, this is just quickly- And maybe to add, I feel as if for SAPS, your currency is based on arrests, so you can see what you are doing.

Ms Mathys: Just quickly, we had a character, which I am sure you know, that was here, Brown Mogotsi, and he said he was an agent. Is that how you trained agents? First off, just give us a little bit now that we actually have a professional. Yeah, like how would someone who was not an agent, what are they called? Operative? No, he thought he was an agent.

Lt Gen Senthumule: First off, just to clarify, within state security, because we are civilians, we do not call our informers informants; we call them agents. That is why you would hear the concept, your principal agent network, the person who handles other people to be able to get information. But in intelligence, you actually have informants. So the agents are actually our own members who then go undercover.

Ms Mathys: No, explain that again.

Lt Gen Senthumule: So in SAPS, under the crime intelligence, yes, an agent is a SAPS member who is exfiltrated out of a system, erased from the system. And actually, we could call him either your infiltration agent, or maybe penetration would have been from inside. But he will work with the syndicate, you know, but there is legislation around it. And please do not make me go further.

Ms Mathys: No, we do want to know. Because we also have to make a decision about this at some point. So you are here. So I am sure you had some of his testimony, both in the commission and here. So, tell us if that would be some sort of training.

Lt Gen Senthumule: He is from outside the organisation, so he would become an informer. So we have an informer network. As I said, a former member who is exfiltrated, then becomes an agent. And you never get to know them. They are not on our systems. We do not even see them or know them.

Ms Mathys: And would they go around saying they are an informer?

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, because that actually would be blowing their cover and compromising their operation.

Ms Mathys: Clarity, thank you. We just want to go quickly. Let us start with the disbandment of PKTT. So Lt Gen Mkhwanazi said that that was actually a mistake in identity. Because there were PKTT members who moved to Gauteng to assist. What is your take? Were it PKTT members that arrested Matlala?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The advantage I have in responding to your question is that I did not have the information they had. I only had an instruction and a plan. So I heard when he said that. So, probably, I am sure he would not just say it. It was based on the knowledge that they had as to why there was that mistake in identity.

Ms Mathys: But you were in charge that time of these dockets and the arrests and overseeing Gauteng, and it happened in Gauteng. So when that whole arrest happened, you did not know absolutely anything about it.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I thought you were asking me about the dockets and the fact that maybe; in following the dockets, it was a matter of thinking that the PKTT was the team that arrested Gauteng.

Ms Mathys: No, the question here is that he has characterised that whole, has characterised this disbandment of PKTT, right? As it was a mistake in identity, this is how this whole thing has been blown out of proportion.

Lt Gen Senthumule: So these sergeants who are working on the Armand Swart matter, when the issue of compromise or security breach came, they approached General Mbuso Khumalo, who approached me, I approached General Khumalo to say, take over this team. So maybe the people who thought it came from KwaZulu-Natal actually started here in Gauteng. Yes.

Ms Mathys: Are there cartels in South Africa?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Cartel, I think, is a big word.

Ms Mathys: What word would you use?

Lt Gen Senthumule: You see, if you look at the history of your organised crime, your mafia would be linked to families. And your cartels, it is now when all the big bosses of the families of the mafia come together for a purpose, then it is called a cartel. I have not evidenced that in the investigation. Maybe there is, maybe we are missing it. But in your typical organised crime countries, like your Mexico, your Colombia, your Brazil, those exist.

Ms Mathys: So are we heading in that direction?

Lt Gen Senthumule: If we let things spiral out of control by not addressing organised crime, I think we can easily spiral in that direction.

Ms Mathys: And then the big five, what do you know about it? I mean, I am sure it is either under investigation, but is there some sort of reality? And is there some evidence?

Lt Gen Senthumule: When you conduct your organised crime investigation, you are bound by the trade to do your link analysis. And I think when the team did their link analysis and identified people who actually are part of that particular syndicate, I would say that it was not done lightly. It was done based on either documentary evidence or evidence from the digital space that actually links the individuals.

Ms Mathys: Were you part of that process?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I was not.

Ms Mathys: You could be part of a process like that. Because you are pretty senior and you have an organised crime background. Respectfully to the committee members, we would not know anything and everything as tenors in the police. And I will tell you why. I gave an example where two weeks ago we were sitting in a meeting, and then there was a compromise of that particular meeting. And that is why you cannot know all the documents because it could be a friend of mine. I mean, we are human beings, you know, not that we would give out the information for that matter. I actually led my own nephew to the police station to get arrested when he attempted to rape my niece. You know, so law for me is law, and law does not look at families or who works for what. So what I am saying is that you are not bound to know everything. Even the national commissioner would not know everything about the cartels, about the individuals.

Ms Mathys: But cartels, Lieutenant General, is a big thing in organised crime. So we are not asking about one of so many cases of rape. So a cartel or a mafia, so to speak.

Lt Gen Senthumule: An example I can give is with the gangs in the Western Cape. So we would know, for example, the type of gangs. I mean, in Westbury, for example, I mean Honourable James is here. Those, because they are within organised crime. But, probably, say, in our briefing with the members, if some would slip into our hands, it does not mean that we were ignorant. Probably could have been that at that time, we did not know.

Ms Mathys: Okay, let us go to General Jacobs. Why did you call him and not send him a formal or whatever the formal process was? For him to release the file.

Lt Gen Senthumule: And if maybe, let us look at my responsibility and his responsibility as he would have been with ESKOM. If Adv Johnson writes down and says, this is a docket for IDAC, and this docket is based on Section 28, and General Jacobs has those dockets, understanding the system, I know that IDAC does not have a system where a docket is registered; it is moved to IDAC. So they will have to ask the dockets from SAPS. So mine was to say to General Jacobs that she was to say that you were keeping those dockets. I think one of the things she wrote was that the complainant was –

Lt Gen Senthumule: That is the process.

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, I did not.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think that that is the unfortunate part because neither he nor General- What Adv Johnson specified, but understanding the nature of –

Ms Mathys: No, you keep saying that, Lieutenant General. What I am getting to be that it is a bit bizarre that you are called to intervene to get these files, right? And then no one even randomly mentions what these files are about. So you even have to call Jacobs, who is actually hiding these files. And then he does not even mention, or you do not even ask, but why are you not giving the files to Adv Johnson?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Maybe let me start by explaining the process of registering a high-profile case. And I think I will start with one of General Sibiya and Minister Mchunu. The DPC has an obligation to call and inform me, and I will inform the national commissioner. And I think the same happened in the Western Cape when the National Commissioner’s case was open. So in that case, I would have known. I probably would have known, okay, now there is a document that General Khumalo. At that time, there was no report, either to me or to –

Ms Mathys: But you are not answering. I am saying, you are saying to me that Jacobs did not even tell you these files are in relation to, and we are curious as to why he did not want to even send these files to Adv Johnson. Because it is very likely –

Lt Gen Senthumule: For me, maybe now on his side, and in Venda, we say, the water has already spilt, but I can talk to it. He could have said, General, can we discuss the matter? I think that would have made a huge difference for me. Because he and I were, I mean, even today, we are very close. So he could have done that. But I suppose maybe at that time, either there was discomfort, or maybe, I do not know.

Ms Mathys: Why have you not acted on him for keeping those files in his safe?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I posted here on the statement; there is a letter from General Hankel stating that there is no policy regulating their taking the documents. And for that reason, if you actually want to act on a person based on what, however, the practice is that we do not take the documents away from the station. So it is what we always preach. Rather, we make copies of it.

Ms Mathys: No, but Lieutenant General, this is a bit layered, right? So outside, let us even talk that the statement's been taken from the station, right? It was in his safe. You had to even get a call and an email from Adv Johnson to get it, and you still call him. It cannot just be that it can be parked. It was deliberate, and it is not one file. It is a set of files on a particular matter, and in retrospect, you can see what the issue was, and you still have not acted on it.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Can I request that maybe General Jacobs be allowed to clarify why he had those documents actually in his safe?

Ms Mathys: But that was your job to find out, not ours.

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, I am not saying you.

Ms Mathys: So let us move on from there. I think we have answered that. Oh, when Lt Gen Khumalo was arrested, what was your personal response to it?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I sent him a WhatsApp message. Not only him. I sent Zipho. I called General Gabela. I sent Lekalakala. I worked with all of them. As I said, I was really, and I can give you my phone. You can just see Khumalo and me, and because my father is a Khumalo, we call each other but, since even now, when he calls me “sisi,” I say “bhuti,” you know? So I really was one of those who were just shocked and hurt. But I did, I can show you my phone. I did text him to say, Mbulazi, I am really hurt, and I hope he is going to come to pass.

Ms Mathys: Were you surprised that something like this would have happened? Not that you guys on the top, Lieutenant General, should be above the law, but the way it happened and how it happened.

Ms Mathys: There was a meeting, that meeting in February 2022, where the IMC agreed that the PKTT should be enhanced and continue its operations. And that was the last meeting that you remember.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think there was another meeting in 2023. We will have to check the minutes, but I know I did not attend the last meeting that was there.

Ms Mathys: Then that meeting on the 6th of March 2025, it was the National Commissioner, Lieutenant General Mosikili Khumalo, and they took a position. But also because we are the ones who produced the proposal. That, on its own, says that we were actually for the winding-down approach.

Ms Mathys: Okay, we are going to wrap up right about now. So you spoke earlier that SAPS members, because of these proceedings, I guess, both the ad hoc and the commission that is going on, they are kind of being forced to pick a side or pick sides.

Lt Gen Senthumule: And I did not say that we are forced to pick sides. I say, by virtue of your testimony, that you are attached to a faction. Or you are attached to the other faction. Yeah, people would say, no, this one is from Mkhwanazi, this one is for the committee, you know. But I think for me, it should not be, and I am talking about generals that I know, that I have worked with, who actually are not even part of this whole thing. They are observing it from a distance. We are in it. And I have never experienced them choosing a side.

Ms Mathys: So what are we going to do as South Africans? Because if Lieutenant Generals and the rest of you on that highest level cannot find each other because you are spending more time opening cases against each other, getting people to do, do you know how many sworn statements we have here, Lieutenant General? I do not even know what we are going to do during deliberations, because we do not even have an investigator. Maybe you can send us some. I was throwing shade at Honourable Sangoni, but she did not get it. But I am saying, like, what is going to happen? Because if Lieutenant Generals are on that level, if you are not protecting us, and you are spending so much time opening cases, I mean, oh, this was also a question. Lieutenant General Phahlane, was it procedural, or was it out of character for the North West to conduct that whole investigation into O'Sullivan and Sarah-Jane Trent?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I actually do not have details, I think, about that investigation.

Ms Mathys: But it was done in the North West. So my question is, it is not the details, but is it something that would normally be done? And what circumstances would there need to be?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The process is, if, for example, let us say we are dealing with the CITs and we require expert skills that will come from provinces, so we can convene a team. But for me, it is about fighting a crime sketch that has become unmanageable for the country. In relation to this one, honestly, I think I would rather reserve my view because I do not know what actually prompted the entire investigation.

Ms Mathys: It all went to that; that is what his testimony was. One statement said it was just; everything was just there in Northwest. So it was not a matter of what you were saying, bringing criminals in. But you do not want to talk to it.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I have never been involved in such a practise. So if I give it –

Ms Mathys: So it was a very bizarre and unfounded thing. Okay, I want to talk about this, and Honourable Malema touched on it. The thing about being at that party. I am not even a trained intelligence agent or whatever, whatever. But that whole setup, as you described it, you have the wife of someone come and sit next to you, there with your friend. You do not think that your friend, who was it, Thema, was actually trying to introduce you. I mean, it actually looks like Lt Gen Sibiya had these parties to link up members of SAPS with criminals. Or maybe they were not all criminals, but business people or people who needed favours from SAPS. And that is what those parties were all about. Because it was not about, from what I understand, networking and SAPS members finding each other and building teamwork. So when did Lt Gen Khumalo stop attending the parties? Or was he stopped being invited to the party?

Lt Gen Senthumule: As I said, General Sibiya would change. Initially, General Khumalo was the one invited. And then later, it was I and others who were being invited. But I want to clarify –

Ms Mathys: But what I am asking is that, you are sure it was an invite-related or it was not that Lt Gen Khumalo just stopped coming and was still invited?

Lt Gen Senthumule: They were not talking.

Ms Mathys: Okay, and when did this happen?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I am not certain.

Ms Mathys: More or less?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would say around last year.

Ms Mathys: Before the press conference of –

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, actually, the year before last. 2024, not 2025.

Ms Mathys: So these parties. And then have you engaged Major General Thema about the role? Because perhaps she is also involved in this whole thing of networking and setting up. It makes no sense. And I am not even on your level. And I would have known straight away that this is very dodgy. And then the next minute, she is saying, oh yeah, yeah, his number. Call him, ask him for fridges. And then how? Okay, respond to that. And then the next question, if you can also respond to, yeah. How much were those fridges that were donated that Lt Gen Sibiya even got?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I think it was R3 999. It was small bar fridges, I think. I did not even see any of them, you know. But also, if I may proceed, you know. And I know how it is projected. For me, this becomes one of the saddest moments. Because when we went there, there was no preoccupation about who was going to be there. We did not know. And as I said that, you know, there were times where actually I would not go to these parties, you know. There were times when, for example, I would say, Ah, no man, you know. But then I think of that day because he is like, you know, General come, we are having something in the house, and we go. And I said to General Thema that there was no discussion. General Sibiya himself said at some point to me that, actually, you are a very good woman, you know. I do not project two faces. Yes, I am an operative. But what you see is what you get. When I am angry, you see it. And when I do not want something, I then say it. But then there are times when I feel I cannot go on. Do you know what? Let us just go with the flow. And I think probably for me, that was the mistake of that day. Because when something says to you, do not go to a place and you go, this would not have happened. However, those were not hook-up parties. We go to General Sibiya's.

Ms Mathys: They are not hook-ups as in sex hook-ups.

Lt Gen Senthumule: No.

Ms Mathys: I am saying as in... I am not even... No, not those. Business people to do favours.

Lt Gen Senthumule: But actually, there is nothing more I can say to convince anybody otherwise that this was just an innocent going to a party. And again, there was no intent in terms of asking for that sponsorship. For that reason, I did not even see one of the images. I did not get any of them.

Chairperson: Thank you so much, Honourable Mathys. We shall take a 20-minute break and come back at 17:20.

[Break]

Chairperson: I want to say to you that we have come back from the comfort break. And I now want to allocate you your 20 minutes. Your 20 minutes will start at 17:25 and will end at 17:45. You decide if you want to start now.

Mr Mncwango: You actually mentioned here in your testimony that your offices were actually raided by IPID somewhere in 2023. What were the issues there?

Lt Gen Senthumule: It was in relation to that allegation of General Sibiya saying that I employed ghost workers or the people that actually were providing security for Captain M. We are actually not providing security for Captain M, but they were under the guise of protecting him.

Mr Mncwango: Is that investigation still going on?

Lt Gen Senthumule: In 2024, I think in March, the gadgets which were taken for investigation were taken. So they were returned with no report. And up until today, nothing happened.

Mr Mncwango: In paragraph 25 of your testimony, Minister Mchunu actually testified before this committee that the continued activity or operation of the PKTT beyond 2022 was actually illegal. You are saying that you attended a meeting of the IMC in February 2022, where the IMC actually took a resolution to enhance and also extend the term of operations of the task team. Was the Minister not aware of this extension? If not, why would he choose to deliberately mislead the committee?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I do not think it was even an extension. The IMC's meeting was continuing, and I think in those meetings, decisions would be taken to say the team needs to continue, and it needs to be strengthened. So there was never a point of an intention to actually curtail the functioning of the team. For Minister Mchunu to say that its existence beyond 2022 was illegal, I would not be able to answer as to what informed that. But in the environment that we operate in operationally, we do not have any end to any intervention as illegal. For example, you would have authority for the team to be deployed. So as long as that is still in force, if the information is not approved for the deployment, that particular operation has to proceed. So the illegality for me is not even supposed to be in this discussion.

Mr Mncwango: What would be the standard measure of the effectiveness of any task team which would actually inform the decision whether to extend its life or to terminate its life? What would be the standard yardstick that you would use to actually determine whether a task team is actually effective?

Lt Gen Senthumule: If I may, maybe use the Magnus team as an example, I think we had problems in Manguzi, particularly in relation to, I think, there was an issue of stock theft, and I think the issue of the Mozambican cross-border issues. And a team, full team was deployed there to sit. But as time progressed, the problems and the challenges were probably, let me say, stabilised, and we had to decommission. So after an assessment in terms of looking at the crime picture, pre the intervention and post the intervention, and we look probably at the addressing of the people that we would have deemed either person of interest, suspects, or targets. And once the environment gets into what we could term a stable environment, then you begin to decommission. And decommissioning, you also inform, for example, I was informed as the head of detectives to say, this is the instruction based on the assessment, then we are decommissioning. And we, as the detectives, because we still had dockets, we then had to ask for an extension, particularly for investigators. So that is basically the standard that we use. In the Western Cape, we have had a lockdown; now we are on lockdown three, because the problem persists. So you strengthen the team based on the assessment that we are actually generating more crime in the environment of the gangs than actually neutralising. So what do you do? You then force multiply and bring in more capacity to stabilise.

Mr Mncwango: Now, who generates that kind of report? How does it actually land on the desk of the minister?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Within the police, we have got what we call the National Operational Coordination Centre. And that is the central hub or the fusion centre for all our operations. There are people who are receiving reports from all ends, particularly on these projects. And as they receive, an assessment is compiled that shows the picture of what we are dealing with. And based on that, a determination is made to either decommission or strengthen the teams.

Mr Mncwango: Would that process have been followed in relation to the PKTT report upon which the Minister actually based his decision to disband?

Lt Gen Senthumule: The NOCC, General Khumalo, before he became the project manager for the PKTT, he was actually the national head of operations, intervention operations at the NOCC. That is why he then got involved with the project. And that structure then appoints a project leader. It is driven from there. The PKTT was no different. That is why even the budget had to go through the NOCC for approval.

Mr Mncwango: At the meeting of the 6th of March, which you attended with the Minister, General Sibiya was actually very unhappy with the presentation that was made there. And the Minister and the chief of staff actually shared his sentiments. Is it normal operating procedure in the SAPS, given the fact that you actually have been there, you know, even before the tenure of Minister Mchunu, to have a chief of staff actively participating, I mean, in operational matters of the South African Police Service, and of actually expressing his position on matters that are being discussed?

Lt Gen Senthumule: If the committee can permit me to use the military terms of the chief of staff, and that would be a staff officer. Normally, a staff officer, as I have behind me, would not participate in management meetings, unless maybe for a point of clarity for record keeping. So I am not sure, maybe this was something that was agreed upon, to say that he can participate. But I am really not aware of any situation where we would have the chief of staff or the staff officer participating in executive management meetings.

Mr Mncwango: Well, my colleagues have actually asked the question about the exclusion of Lt Gen Mkhwanazi in the whole process. And I am concerned that perhaps by omission, you may actually also be lumped up with the group that actually deliberately excluded Lt Gen Mkhwanazi. Because when you were approached to actually receive the files, and start the process of winding up the operations of all those dockets, I do not hear you saying that you actually picked up the phone and contacted Lt Gen Mkhwanazi. Why was that?

Mr Sauls: You say that the vision of the Minister that you celebrate him for was to capacitate detectives. Just take us through paragraph 71. You just briefly let us know why. Why are you saying you celebrate him for his vision? Why?

Lt Gen Senthumule: When the Minister came into the police, I think the first thing he asked was for us to come up with a plan. Actually, the issue was more on the docket load on the system, but also cases which were older, which would be deemed backlog. And his request was, what can we do to deal with the backlog? And we had to define the old cases, as we would call them, in terms of what we would need to do to reduce the workload. And we came to the conclusion that these would be cases that are older than two years. But we are focusing more on contact crimes like murder and rape. And we then had a plan that we would then do a scientific study where those dockets were counted to say, this is the number of dockets. How many people would we then need? And we costed that. And the Minister said he would even approach the Treasury if there is no funding. Lo and behold, General Dimpane did provide funding. That is why we were able to recruit those detectives. But also further, in adding to the Minister’s initiative, the health commissioner also added, in terms of the number of detectives, I think we were given 4 000 detectives from the colleges, and we were given 600 personnel for the local criminal records centre.

I want to use one case that we dealt with, I think in Gauteng South, where the child was found dead, and we had to use all the under one roof to be able to then plot the suspect on the case. Lt Gen Senthumule: Actually, it was the detectives in Gauteng who were investigating the Armand Swart murder. But they, because of the security breach, I think that has been spoken about, including the issue of having been told about the envelopes, then crime intelligence had to be deployed as part of the security of those members, as well as combat members. So people then thought it was the PKTT. It was not the PKTT; it was Gauteng detectives. I think, as recently as now, again, when we are doing the awards, it was those three detectives who received an award.

Ms James: So you agree with me that there has been a continuous assumption that it was PKTT after Cat Matlala and not the detectives, as you actually say. And this is why we basically saw the disbandment of PKTT and not this other task team.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I would not take it there, but I think the misunderstanding, I would agree with you.

Ms James: Because if you look at the occurrence of the events and if you look at the dates in terms of how things unfolded, there was the raid on the Matlala residence, and then there was this New Year's Eve decision to disband immediately. Then you have General Sibiya saying to you, you are dragging your feet. When we heard testimony from General Mkhwanazi, he also said that when he spoke to the chief of staff in the Minister’s office, the chief of staff, Mr Nkabinde, told him that there was pressure. Do you see the link?

Lt Gen Senthumule: If I were to take this information as we see it in the public space and based on what you say, and I were to plot it on the analyst's notebook, definitely the links would be there.

Ms James: There is pressure from Sibiya. The dockets go to Sibiya. There is pressure from Sibiya. Mr Nkabinde confirms, General Mkhwanazi says that he said there was pressure. The Minister is also unhappy with the team for dragging their feet. What were the Minister’s words? I think it was to the SAPS officials in terms of the disbandment, in terms of the pace.

Lt Gen Senthumule: He was dissatisfied with how far we have moved or how we have not moved.

Ms James: So, do you think we have been barking up the wrong tree here?

Lt Gen Senthumule: I actually wish I could have supported you in relation to forensic investigation because then we would know whether we are barking at the wrong tree or not. But that is required because some of the things require more than just talking. I think we need to follow some of the issues.

Ms James: I want to ask you, this issue of the fridge is bothering me. What is the mandate in layman's terms of counterintelligence?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Counterintelligence, I think we have two angles. We have got the offensive one. The offensive one basically deals with dealing with your threats as they manifest. For example, if you have a threat of espionage, sabotage, subversion, and corruption. So then you would go offensive because you are responding to a problem.

Ms James: Threats within which space?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Threats within SAPS, according to the Act, within the whole of SAPS.

Ms James: So, threats that would compromise the integrity of SAPS?

Lt Gen Senthumule: Yes. That is the offensive side. And then the defensive side is looking at the people who are deployed to fulfil functions in terms of the organisation. So here we are talking about personal security. General Senthumule, do I have the integrity to sit where I sit? Are there any threats in my life that could compromise information? Things that you are looking at now, do you have relations with questionable people who actually could compromise your work? So that is part of counterintelligence. And it does not only talk about personal security. Also, it talks about document security. I think we have also seen how people are moving and sharing documents. That is the mandate of counterintelligence.

Ms James: So, in layman's terms, and simply put, it is to detect threats and risks within SAPS associated with SAPS members.

Lt Gen Senthumule: And you have three options. You mitigate, which means you deal with it to manage it. You neutralise; you remove it. So sometimes it could even mean dismissing a person or charging them. But at the end of the day, you monitor that threat that does not re-manifest.

Ms James: Are they effective, and are they present at the moment? Based on everything that we have heard here, how many SAPS members have been compromised? Did you say counterintelligence is effective, and they are present and working effectively in SAPS?

Lt Gen Senthumule: For example, if they were effective, our physical security, even the stations, would be intact. So what we have done, unlike I would say security, all these are lumped within one environment in counterintelligence. So you would have people working on, and please, Honourable Malema, I am going to use it as an example, working on maybe your office. They would not just look at you. They would look at your office security, your physical security. They would look at your personal security and your documents. But in that, yes, everything, including your interactions. Also, such things we would not be dealing with because they would have been picked up proactively. But also, maybe to take it further, in SAPS, the functions are not lumped. Physical security belongs with auxiliary services. And I think we just do the basic minimum to check cars that are coming in and out. We are not conducting counterintelligence.

Ms James: Okay, so with you having explained to us, in layman's terms, the mandate of counterintelligence to monitor interactions, bank accounts, and relationships of SAPS members with certain individuals. And then, with all of this knowledge, general, and at one stage, I think you were heading up this department, you did not see anything wrong with asking for a sponsorship from “Cat” Matlala, as someone who was the head of the counter. The head of the counter goes to someone who is not only a service provider, but also remembers your mandate, as the counter is to detect interactions and improper relationships. And here you are telling us, as the former head of counterintelligence, you approached a service provider, which is number one, improper and makes this questionable. And you approach someone with 17 charges. That was more than just a sad moment in your career.

Lt Gen Senthumule: It actually is regrettable. And much as I can say, it was innocent. You know, I think from the look of things, it does not sound right. It does not sound correct. And it is something that will follow me. And no matter how much I can argue it out to say it was innocent, it remains. And again, as I said, it also exposes the vulnerabilities of us letting our guard down. Relying specifically on the supply chain to clear this sponsorship for us. I think it should go beyond that. So I do understand and agree with you.

Ms James: You know why this is bothering me? Because I know we have these victim-friendly rooms at police stations that could have benefited from a fridge in one of our communities. You could have much rather donated that fridge to one of these impoverished communities. So we are very, and the reason I am asking you these questions, we are not only probing criminality. We are also trying to establish the people who are in these positions, and whether they are fit for these positions. And are they able to make sound decisions in the best interest of South Africans? And this is why I am harping on this. And with this matter, you are left wanting.

Lt Gen Senthumule: I totally understand, Honourable. Thank you.

Chairperson: Honourable. Honourable James, you are asking a question about the credibility of the company that made that donation. And you, once again, on a moral standing, say the fridge could have been given to a charitable organisation.

Ms James: No, Chie, I am saying we have victim-friendly rooms at SAPS, part of SAPS. Not a separate organisation. It could have been used at the community level, this is what I am saying, at a police station.

Chairperson: Yeah, the question that you ask first is, where does the fridge come from?

Ms James: The fridge was donated to SAPS, was it not?

Chairperson: By whom?

Ms James: By Cat.

Chairperson: Okay. And did you say it could have been used elsewhere charitably?

Ms James: Because she did not have the history of Cat. So even if it came from a credible person, donating the fridge back to someone who earns a massive salary, that does not make sense.

Chairperson: Why do not we say that an honest police service disposes of such a –

Ms James: Yes, but that is what I have been trying to paint here. The fact of the matter is, based on the fact that Jindal was the head of Crime Intelligence, this should have never happened. But since we are here now and we have these fridges, it could have been donated or given to one of the SAPS at the victim-friendly rooms. That is what I am saying.

Lt Gen Senthumule: No, I could not. I mean, I just gave you an example now, and I can open my app for you to see. I cannot transfer EFT from my credit card. It must go into my savings and then into my check. I mainly use my FNB account. That is my main account.

Adv Mkhize: Thank you, Chairperson. I think we have covered the entire testimony.

Lt Gen Senthumule: Princess.

Chairperson: Yes. Khosi in Tshivenda is not a princess. And you are a descendant of the Ramabulanas. Mr Nqola: Thank you very much, Chair.

Mr Nqola: Good evening, Mr Adams, and welcome back. Look, there is no doubt that what you experienced is something so unprecedented. It is something of which we have never heard. It is actually shocking. That is why you say, at first, you did not even know what to do with the information. And there is no doubt that it is something you are going to remember for the rest of your life. How come you forget the date when it happened?

Mr Adams: Honourable members, it is a real pity my wife is not here tonight. Because she could tell you I forget my children's birthdays. And there are days I walk around in the house, and then they tell me, do you know it is your birthday today? I am terrible with dates. I am terrible with small details. I do understand where you are going. But trust me when I tell you, in all honesty, I do not remember the date.

Mr Nqola: I am sure you sometimes even forget your birthday.

Mr Adams: I actually forget my birthday every day.

Mr Nqola: Okay, now let us take you to paragraph nine and paragraph eight in your statement, yes. So, you say in paragraph eight that you decided to hand over the information to SAPS. But in paragraph nine, you say you decided not to hand over the evidence. But later in your statement, you would have handed over the information to someone from the State Security Agency. It is quite confusing to determine from whom the information was handed over.

Mr Adams: Chief, initially, when I walked to the police station, I mean, I walked from Parliament. I thought to myself, I am not going to hand these documents to the constable or the sergeant or whoever is going to take the case. Because I mean, one of them is marked top secret. Thinking to myself, I am going to give it to the investigating officer when he contacts me. So we can shorten the line of people who know what is in this docket. But then, when I was tipped off to the fact that my dockets had gone missing, I decided I was not going to give it to SAPS. I was going to, well, not right then, but when the IGI contacted me, I decided this is where my information is going to go. And I must tell you, in all honesty, it was not because I thought or knew that this was the right place to leave the documents. It was just that I literally wanted these documents off my hands, but I did not want to give them to just anybody if you follow me.

Mr Nqola: Okay. Now, you get this information, and you rightfully say that you got confused to the extent that you did not know how to handle this information. And with the possibility that this information may also be fraudulent. So, when did you decide that it is an authentic report?

Mr Adams: I did not. Remember, I handed it to the IGI for further investigation. Because I had this feeling in the back of my mind, man, that somebody wants me to run to the newspapers with this. And I am thinking to myself, but what if it is fraudulent? What if it ends up with egg all over my face? For this reason, I kept it quiet at the time.

Mr Nqola: So you have your friend, Lincoln. Was he working in the Western Cape or the Cape Town Central Police Station?

Mr Adams: I actually would not be able to tell you. What I can confirm is that he was the first head of the AGU [Anti-Gang Unit]. And he was a very bitter man, because they had been promised the type of resources allocated to the PKTT. And then the plug was pulled. So he was very bitter towards the SAPS.

Mr Nqola: So, how does he get to know when, by whom, and how were your dockets collected from the police station?

Mr Adams: I would have no clue, sir.

Mr Nqola: And you did not ask?

Mr Adams: I did ask.

Mr Nqola: Okay. Let us go to the IGI. The IGI calls you and asks you about your cases. Does he tell you how he knows that you have opened the cases?

Mr Adams: No, sir. He does not disclose that.

Mr Nqola: He does not tell you. Are you people generally who call each other even before the incident?

Mr Adams: If you had to ask me the day before who the IGI was, I would not be able to tell you. We have never called each other before.

Mr Nqola: Okay. Did he know the information contained in the reports you received from the door?

Mr Adams: You know, I actually would not be able to answer that. But what he did say to me is, I have an interest in the cases that you have laid. My office, rather. I think I am quoting him. And that was it for me.

Mr Nqola: So it did not strike you that, at least you must be aware, where did the IGI get your contacts? How did he get your contacts? In the usual course of events, you would say, I am Xola Nqola, the Inspector General of Intelligence. I got your number from Khusela Sangoni. I am calling you in respect of this. I got everything from so-and-so that you would have found yourself in this situation.

Mr Adams: I would love to encourage you, my brother, to think a little bit more about intelligence services. They have their ways and means of extracting information, like telephones, like emails. The IGI found my number. Maybe if Honourable Malema insists, he gets an opportunity to sit here in this chair, he can tell us how. I would actually also like to know. I do not know how he got my number. And you know what the funny thing is? I do not think I was using the state-sanctioned WhatsApp at the time. I was not using the phone that was given to us, so that is actually quite an interesting question.

Mr Nqola: You are not using the parliament one?

Mr Adams: No, I do not use that for WhatsApp because I am told that those things are hackable.

Mr Nqola: Do you know why the IGI was suspended by the President?

Mr Adams: No. Maybe the President will tell us.

Mr Nqola: You are not aware?

Mr Adams: I am not aware.

Mr Nqola: Now, you received a call from a lieutenant colonel, checking you about your cases and wanting to meet you. So you say you successfully avoided meeting him because you were suspicious. So maybe you can, in the paragraph, you do not specify why you were suspicious of this police officer. And you say you were later vindicated. How were you vindicated?

Mr Adams: So, I have laid charges. The dockets are collected. We have now established that it was in a safe somewhere. So there was no intention from the SAPS to investigate. So now, a lieutenant colonel calls me, telling me he is going to investigate. Now, why I felt vindicated is because, according to the SAPS structure, a lieutenant colonel does not investigate a general. So even if this guy was a genuine lieutenant colonel, he did not have the authority to conduct an investigation. And for this reason, whenever he called me, I just palmed him off politely. No, man, I am in Joburg. I am sorry, I am stuck in committee all week. I just gave him an excuse because he called a few times. I just gave him an excuse every time until he went away. Okay.

Mr Nqola: I have never seen a grabber. At what times does a grabber become redundant? What did you actually mean?

Mr Adams: You know, technology is a very funny thing. It evolves consistently. Like an iPhone 6 is no longer in use, because it is obsolete. So the grabber that was purchased by Crime Intelligence, by Dumisani Khumalo, mind you, they bought an obsolete grinder. See, the construction work is coming up. Obsolete grabber. They bought a 4G grabber in the 5G era. But I have a suspicion that they knowingly bought that 4G grabber. I have a suspicion that they never intended to buy something that worked. Because if you look at what the rumours are, that the price was so inflated, I think they just bought a grabber for the sake of buying a grabber, so they could loosen some money. That is what my suspicion is, but it is not something I can confirm. Like General Mkhwanazi would say, I thought to myself.

Mr Nqola: Do you know which year this grabber was?

Mr Adams: I actually do have the data written. It is either in my office or at home, but I do, and I can supply it to the government.

Mr Nqola: You said you are not good with dates.

Mr Adams: I am terrible with dates. Terrible.

Mr Nqola: Was it not 2017?

Mr Adams: I do not want to lie. It could be.

Mr Nqola: The evidence that we have here is that there was an attempt to buy a grabber by Crime Intelligence in 2017. But the grabber was not bought. There are a lot of issues here, they say.

Mr Adams: You know, Honourable Nqola, because I am speaking on the oath, I do not want to feed you the wrong information. So if I do have the chairperson's email, I can mail him the date posting in the morning. Yes, please do.

Mr Nqola: Chair, no further questions. Thank you.

Ms Sangoni: Thank you. Thank you very much, Chairperson. Mr Adams, tell me, now that you have delivered your testimony, when all is said and done, what do you want us to take out of it?

Mr Adams: I would like for this committee to just look at the facts. Do not take my word for anything. Look at the facts that I have supplied. Look at the facts that the accusing team have supplied. And make a reasonable judgement and make reasonable recommendations. But I would like this committee to understand that at the very top of the South African Police Services, there is a cabal so corrupt that it threatens all of us. All of us. Honourable Kohler Barnard, whom I must admit, I do not have much of a liking for. But she spent 25 years in this Parliament doing her best. For a man to come, go on national TV, and accuse her almost of being part of a cartel, and then offering no evidence, which was defamation under oath.

Ms Sangoni: Sorry, I am going to push you because of time. Let us look at these facts that you say we must look at. Now, the accusing team, I learned a new term today. I am assuming that you and Ms Mashale are part of the accusing team.

Mr Adams: No, we are the accused.

Ms Sangoni: Oh, are you the accused? Okay. Now that we have clarified that, Ms Mashale says the information was not classified. You say it was classified. Which is it?

Mr Adams: I thought parts of it were classified. Patricia would not know any better because I never showed her the information. And I do think that she is trying to protect me somewhat. Which you know what I love about her.

Ms Sangoni: How does that help us? Because you said we must look at the facts. She had the same information that you had. She says it is not classified. You say it is classified.

Mr Adams: Okay, how do you know it was classified? It was in one document. It was written. Top secret.

Ms Sangoni: Okay. And now, I am trying to get to the facts. One of the criminal documents you opened related to the abuse of the Secret Service account by Lt Gen Khumalo, whom you accuse of channelling funds of the PKTT. How much did he channel or syphon, whatever the word is, and how did he do this?

Mr Adams: You know, Honourable Member, if you look at the 2021 uprising in KZN, when General Mkhwanazi and then Minister of Defence, I forget her name now, there we go, they were having a hell of a spat. And it was ugly, and it was public. And General Mkhwanazi accuses her of staying at a five-star hotel.

Ms Sangoni: Honourable Abrams, my question is about General Khumalo of syphoning funds –

Mr Adams: I am getting to it. Then the General accuses our own Minister of staying at a five-star hotel, as if it is illegal. But the PKTT is staying at five-star hotels. And I am telling you that this is one of the ways they are syphoning money. So if you are asking me for a total, I am not an accountant. I am not a forensic examiner. I am not even an investigator. I am asking this committee to look at it. I am asking the committee to summon the ledgers –

Ms Sangoni: Okay, you can assist us with evidence that they were staying against government regulations, I am assuming you mean.

Mr Adams: Yes.

Ms Sangoni: Okay. Then you say the PKTT is a private service of former Minister Cele. Can you substantiate why you made that allegation?

Mr Adams: 100%. So earlier, I heard testimony that the PKTT did not do the raid on Vusi “Cat” Matlala’s house. The leader of that raid was, in actual fact, Lieutenant Colonel Khumalo, from the PKTT. So I believe, and I believe that investigation will prove, that the PKTT is being used by former Minister Cele to bully people.

Ms Sangoni: Mr Adams, you have opened dockets, right? So I want to know, when you are making these allegations, what evidence are you relying on?

Mr Adams: I never opened a docket saying that the PKTT is being used as a… I never opened that docket.

Ms Sangoni: Are you part of the dockets which accompanied Lt Gen Mkhwanazi's affidavit, where he lists each and every single docket that you put there?

Mr Adams: So, because General Mkhwanazi says it is a fact?

Ms Sangoni: Okay. What did you then, in your docket, indicate?

Mr Adams: Show me the page number, please, ma'am.

Ms Sangoni: I am not sure if you have Lt Gen Mkhwanazi's affidavit.

Mr Adams: I do.

Ms Sangoni: I will tell you the page number now, as soon as I find it. If you look at page three of eight, it is the complaint. Okay, I do not have the pagination now. I can share it with you. But he says Orlando CAS number, and then he writes it out. There, you spoke about Major General Madondo failing a polygraph. The next one, you accused General Khumalo of having a secret service account. The next one, you said, General Khumalo was involved in a cover-up of Villieria. And then, you said General Khumalo and Major General Lushaba are utilising funds to purchase vehicles. Now, do you have any evidence to base any of these allegations?

Mr Adams: I do not want to delve into the merits of the case, but all the evidence that I had about those dockets was handed to the IG.

Ms Sangoni: Do you have evidence?

Mr Adams: I handed it all to the IG. All of it.

Ms Sangoni: So, you said that, and I am quoting here, that in a civilised country, if I could find it, in a civilised country, if Lt Gen Mkhwanazi did what he did, going around and speaking on things without evidence, it would have been dismissed or something to that effect. Why must we take you differently from Lt Gen Mkhwanazi? Because you come here, and then you just say –

Mr Adams: Do not take me differently. Take the judge who signed off on the arrest warrants differently. Because Khumalo is in court. Madondo is in court.

Ms Sangoni: So, you claim to have evidence of all of this?

Mr Adams: I gave the evidence to the IG. I do not know how the IDAC got it, but what I can tell you is that everybody in this room knows that General Khumalo was arrested.

Ms Sangoni: Okay. In paragraph 25, you said General Mkhwanazi would have been dismissed— oh, sorry. In paragraph 45, you say that no one disagreed with the disbandment of the PKTT, and here you are referring to the Police Portfolio Committee. No one disagreed with the disbandment of the PKTT until a reason was needed to attempt to complete the capture of SAPS. What do you mean by that?

Mr Adams: Exactly what I say, ma'am. I was in the Police Portfolio Committee. Honourable Skosana was in that committee. Honourable James was in that committee. Honourable Kohler Barnard was in that committee. And the disbandment of the PKTT was read to us as a fact. General Khumalo brought the presentation. There was not one word mentioned against it by anyone, including Mkhwanazi, until they needed a reason to call a press conference. Now, please understand, when General Khumalo says that we have had one politically-motivated killing in KZN this year, and therefore he essentially says the PKTT has come to the end of its life, I accept it as a fact. But on the other hand, Mkhwanazi feels different.

Ms Sangoni: So are you saying that the Portfolio Committee, as well as these generals, then got a reason to complete the capture of SAPS? I am confused.

Mr Adams: Not the Portfolio Committee. What was the reason for the 6th of July press conference? It was, according to General Mkhwanazi himself, because the National Commissioner was at the gates of prison. It was to defeat the Indians of Justice if he was telling the truth. It is about the capture of the SAPS. It had nothing to do with the PKTT. The PKTT had been agreed upon in Portfolio, in front of all of us, that it was no longer necessary.

Ms Sangoni: So you think Minister Mchunu was correct to disband the PKTT?

Mr Adams: I think that the Minister may have overreached. I think he should have discussed it with the IMC, which I did not think of at the time. But I am willing to concede that because I was in full support of the Minister. However, there was no disagreement on the disbandment of the PKTT.

Ms Sangoni: Okay. Do you know Mr Cedrick Nkabinde?

Mr Adams: I have met him.

Ms Sangoni: Before or after you wrote to the minister?

Mr Adams: I walked into Mr Nkabinde on the road outside here. It must have been a few weeks after.

Ms Sangoni: So you did not know him before?

Mr Adams: I never met the man before.

Ms Sangoni: Okay. Now, on your annexures, and I do apologise, I do not have them in front of me now. Where you emailed the Minister, where did you get the Minister’s email? Email address?

Mr Adams: If I do not want to lie, I am almost sure that I got three emails from General Lincoln, and then he called me and told me the first one does not work. I think it was three. But I think I sent it in two emails. I think.

Ms Sangoni: Okay. So you emailed the Minister at 8:21, and then four minutes later you emailed Nkabinde. So what happened in those two minutes? To whom did you talk?

Mr Adams: No. Nothing happened in between those few minutes. I got the email addresses from General Lincoln in the evening, and the next morning, he called me and said to me that the first email did not work, and then I sent the other two.

Ms Sangoni: Okay. Now, why did you go to IDAC and not IPID again?

Mr Adams: IPID is toothless and useless. How many cases have they refused to investigate? How many recommendations have they not made?

Ms Sangoni: Why was this within the mandate of IDAC, according to you?

Mr Adams: I did not know if it was in the mandate of IDAC. I am testing anyone and everyone. If IDAC did not get back to me, I would email the FBI if I needed to, but we needed action on this thing.

Ms Sangoni: But you chose not to go to IPID? No. Okay. So General Lincoln informed you that dockets were missing in the Western Cape. Who notified you in Gauteng?

Mr Adams: General Lincoln again.

Ms Sangoni: Oh, he also has access to Gauteng.

Mr Adams: That man was a legend in the service.

Ms Sangoni: What exactly did he do?

Mr Adams: People respect General Lincoln for being who he was, for risking his life to fight for those of us sitting here today.

Ms Sangoni: So what was his role that he could access such information?

Mr Adams: I have no clue.

Ms Sangoni: Very convenient, Mr Adams.

Mr Adams: Why is it convenient, ma'am?

Ms Sangoni: No, because General Lincoln is not here to give us this information.

Mr Adams: I do not know if this is going to come as a surprise to you, but all of us will die. All of us.

Ms Sangoni: So tell me, you said, okay, two days or three days after you messaged the Minister, you contacted IDAC. Who did you contact at IDAC, and how did you get this person?

Mr Adams: I was given a number by General Lincoln. He did not give me a name. He just gave me a number. It was a female. I did not ask for a name. I said that I need someone to contact me. I am a member of parliament.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, you had very scathing remarks to say about General Mkhwanazi and the 21 July unrest. You said you took leave. How long was this leave?

Mr Adams: I do not know how long paternity leave is. I have never taken paternity leave. Where I am from, when your baby is born, you leave work, you go and check if the child has ten fingers, ten toes, you make sure he has the family nose, and you go back to work. That is what I understood.

Ms Sangoni: Are you from KZN with a wife from the Eastern Cape?

Mr Adams: I am from the Cape Flats.

Ms Sangoni: Okay, let me finish my question, if you do not mind. General Mkhwanazi testified that his wife gave birth in KwaZulu-Natal. She is from the Eastern Cape. He drove through the night, took her home, where we come from, that is what you do. Now, he went back to work, having checked the nose the following day. Now, that is why I am asking you, contrary to what General Mkhwanazi said.

Mr Adams: I am not, you are going to have to argue with Minister Nqakula, because she is the one who says that they will help the people.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Let us start with what is your personal theory on who is leaking information generally from crime intelligence, let alone classified information? And to what end? Because there are now constant reports, weekly reports, pouring out of crime intelligence, in the newspapers and on the news online.

Mr Adams: The honest answer is I do not know. It is a concern because if you go onto social media, there are like seven or eight people constantly posting sensitive things. I mean, the fact that the IGI's report has been leaked to the media is a concern. I do not want to mention a name without even a little bit of information because I do not know really... I do not really know people at crime intelligence, to be honest with you. Because General Tiyo, in our last discussion, which must have been two weeks ago, told me that this is a ticking time bomb. He said it is only a matter of time before someone gets killed because of these leaks. So it is a real concern.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Look, I agree with you in your statement that the JSCI did not exist at the stage when you received the documents. It was only constituted on the 1st of April 2024. So your options were indeed very limited. And that fact meant you had to go to the SAPS or the Inspector General of Intelligence or both. So what outcomes were you looking for in terms of both of those visits?

Mr Adams: I was just looking for justice. And I told someone, I think it was the weekend, I wish I had thrown those documents in the bin and just forgotten about it, because it has actually turned my life into a nightmare. There was a stage, and there were people behind me who could confirm it right now, where I would have to have armed people collect my kids at school, where I would ensure that my wife did not walk anywhere, not even to the shop, because it was a strain on my family. For all the good that may come out of this committee, I do not know if it was worth it. I do not know. The times I had to move my family from our home, because we were just concerned about the number of cars stopping, when the neighbours tell you about people stopping in front of your house, taking pictures of your home. It was a nightmare. So there is a big part of me that wishes I had torn those documents up and just walked away. But when I look at what these people have allegedly done, I am actually very glad that I did not.

Ms Kohler Barnard: I am not sure if you're aware of the 2018 high-level review panel report on state security and the State Security Agency, known as the Mufamadi report, which found that excessive secrecy and the cloaking of activities under national security were used to conceal widespread criminality, corruption, and political factionalism within the South African intelligence services across the board. Now, that was released by the president in 2019 and provided a damning indictment of the agency's operations. It seems you're claiming, or agreeing, that the same thing is happening in crime intelligence, using secrecy to cloak criminality.

Mr Adams: I was not aware of the report, but it does not surprise me.

Ms Kohler Barnard: It is not a secret.

Mr Adams: But I had not read it, but it does not surprise me. And I am 100% in agreement with what was said, because it does appear as if there are people at Crime Intelligence and at SAPS as a whole, who see the funds from Crime Intelligence as their own personal ATM.

Ms Kohler Barnard: This committee is looking at possible criminality within our security services. Now, what you have raised here speaks precisely to that. I have never seen classified documents outside of the JSCI, so it is a mystery how you came by them. Mr Adams: No, ma'am, I do not. I do not.

Ms Kohler Barnard: So, where are the leaks from?

Mr Adams: I tell you again, I do not know where those documents came from. And if I knew, I would certainly tell someone. Maybe not you, but I would have told someone. I do not know who put those documents there. I am hoping the cameras will reveal something. That is the reason why there are cameras. If I wanted to lie about where those documents came from, I would have said someone left them on my windscreen, someone left them at Mitchell's plane office. There are no cameras there. I am telling the truth that I walked into my office, and there it was.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Okay, your theory on why this particular entity is leaking like a sieve. To what end?

Mr Adams: I think that it is a factional thing. I think that one set of crooks might be trying to oust the other set of crooks. But we also cannot discard the possibility that there may be good policemen left in Crime Intelligence who are really trying their best to clean up. I do not know. I cannot give you a definitive answer. But it would not surprise me if one band of crooks wanted to get their hands on it all.

Ms Kohler Barnard: I do believe there are good cops within Crime Intelligence, within the state security, everywhere. And I have seen what's happened elsewhere, when the good cops become overwhelmed. They've now reported things, they're ignored, it is shoved under carpets, something breaks eventually, and this information starts pouring out. My personal theory is that's what's happening now, but we'll have to wait and see. Mr Adams: I have had the feeling that I was being followed many, many times. My children have told me that there is a white car driving around, around every second corner they walk. We've had that. And General Lincoln told me that he was looking forward to a fight with the people who run the SAPS. And I must be very honest with you; I was looking forward to having him sitting behind me tonight. It is a real pity that he's not here.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Paragraph 13. You say you were deeply suspicious of the motives of a man who asked to meet with you as he was to investigate the cases you'd opened. Mr Adams: Yes, he called. And my issue was, I had laid the charges, my dockets had been collected. So there was no intention to investigate until I had spoken to the IG and the IDAC. But when he introduced himself as Lieutenant Colonel, because, again, I said to him, I think my first conversation went along the line of we can meet next week. And he said, Lieutenant Colonel cannot investigate a general. And I said, what does this mean? Break it down to me. I am stupid. I am slow. Then he said to me, Fadiel, this is going to be covered up. It is at this point that I decided I am not meeting you, Lieutenant Colonel.

But what I am saying is that it is widely said everywhere you go that there are many, many dozens of generals who do not have security clearance because some of them cannot even apply because they've got criminal records. Now, these are the people that the force cannot keep up with. We cannot under any circumstances. So the reason that I am so upset about all of this is that I cannot verify it, but I have read somewhere that our intelligence budget was cut by R100 million rand to purchase these hotels.

Now, R100 million-rand boots on the ground, it puts drones in the air, and it puts investigators on the road. Now, children are being raped and killed daily because there are not enough funds. Right around the corner, here sits organised crime. Policemen who are expected to combat cartels are working from cell phones because they do not have laptops. But we're buying “larney” hotels, R45 million grabbers that do not work, top-of-the-range sports cars if we want to. Meanwhile, poor children continue to die.

Ms Kohler Barnard: Paragraphs 23 and 4, you seem to link the 26 June 2025 arrests of the Crime Intelligence 7 to the press conference of the 6th of July. Mr Adams: You know, Honourable Member, I watched that press conference at home and I said to my wife, this has got nothing to do with anything except the arrest of Khumalo. It was a show of force to threaten all of us, arrest my friends, and you'll see what happens. There was no other reason for the guns behind him. There was no other reason for the camouflaged uniform. Mr Klopper: No problem. And I will leave that. Honourable Adams, I am not an expert, just like you. You have said it, and I am not shy to say I am not an expert at these matters either. Mr Klopper: All right. I just ask because I have also listened to statements, and I have made my own notes, and I mean, we also have to make something out of it. So it is a no-brainer.
