My last question to you is, do you refer to General Mkhwanazi as a constitutional delinquent? And is it because you describe him like that because of the July 2021 riots, or is it because of his interpretation of the classified documents on criminalising crime?

Mr Adams: 100% chief. He makes this allegation that I belong in Pollsmoor. You know, it is a real proud coloured stereotype. But then why do I belong in Pollsmoor? Because I laid charges. Because corruption must be classified. He's a constitutional delinquent; I will say it to his face.

Mr Klopper: Thank you, Honourable Adams, I really do appreciate all your answers and to your family that's been here again with you after we were not able to question you last time and spent the whole day here again. Thank you very much.

Chairperson: Honourable Tshotetsi, good evening and how are you? Welcome. EFF, next speakers.

Mr Adams: Chairperson, Honourable Malema has a really stern look on his face right now. I am feeling intimidated.

Mr Malema: No, thank you, Chairperson. I am not going to ask any questions. Where you are seated, you must know there is nothing wrong you have done. No one must ever, ever try to tell you nonsense. There is nothing wrong you have done. You come across information; you go to the police. And what the police do with it is none of your business. You have reported it to the authorities. That's how it works. Where should you take that information to? To the same corrupt politicians who are in cahoots with thugs, whose political parties are formed with blood money and tell you, no, take Parliament, take it to Parliament. Which law is that?

You come across nonsense; you report it to the police. It does not matter who is being implicated in the nonsense. There are no holy cows here. There are no holy cows here. We are here to investigate wrongdoing. It does not matter who has done it. No one. No one is above this constitution. No one is above the law. No one. I would have done the same. I did the same in 1998. When some of them were in Pampers, I reported the SARS rogue unit. It was reported by me for the first time. And where did I get that information? The same way you got the information. Sneaked under the door of the ANC Youth League when I was the president of the Youth League. That we were being listened to by the SARS rogue unit, all those who supported Jacob Zuma at that time. So, how you get top secret information and things like that, there is no formula. You reported that matter to the police. That's the beginning and the end of it. Do they want something from you? If they are legit, they call you. You give them whatever you have.

That was the right thing to do. That's what we members of parliament must do. We come across anything; we report it to the police. How are you going to know that it is top secret if you do not open the envelope? How do you hold an envelope like this intelligently and say this is top secret? Because you have to open the envelope and you have to find it written, top secret. For you to declare it top secret.

Mr Adams: I believe Honourable Skosana knows how.

Mr Malema: There is absolutely, members of parliament have a duty to report wrongdoing to the police. They can still come here and hold the authorities accountable. But that does not take away the fact that if you see anything wrong, you must report it to the police. Whether you are right, whether you are wrong, it is not for me to judge. They must do an investigation, and they must come back to you and say, Honourable Adams, you were wrong. There is no prima facie evidence. Whatever you gave us does not work. So those are my remarks. Please, you must never be worried. Some of us have been worse than what you are going through. We joined this thing. The first thing they told us when we joined the struggle was that we must never be scared of policemen. Why should they use a policeman to scare us? We must never be scared of death. And we joined this thing. We put our families at risk. We do it consciously for the good of society. So you must never, through intimidation of the kids and the wife and all those, be made to retreat and be scared of thugs that want to intimidate you. Stand with the truth. That's all you need. Otherwise, after standing with the truth, even if you die, you die with a clear conscience that you were never on the side of wrongdoing, even when it was fashionable to do so. I have never compromised the truth for political expedience. Over to you, Honourable Mathys.

Ms Mathys: We do not have many questions. Thank you, Chair. Honourable Adams. It is more of an observation, and you can comment on it. When they were contacting you and you said that you gave them the run-around because they were not supposed to be allocated those cases, do you not think that it would have been better for you to say “Hey Chief”, like how you tell us here, and explain to them that from what you understand, that you should have not been allocated these dockets to investigate as well because you also contributed to the confusion later on that happened around the dockets?

Mr Adams: You know, Honourable Mathys, in my view, the confusion was created by the generals who came to sit here. I did not want to let them know that I knew more than what they thought.

Ms Mathys: So you were also –

Mr Adams: No, the fact that a full general, well, a Lt Gen can come and sit in this chair and tell you, as Honourable members, that a colonel was going to investigate a general, it tells me what he thinks of you.

Ms Mathys: But that was before. I mean, what happened when you went to open the cases? It was way before we had that here. So I am saying you already had information, right, that you must just give them the run-around, and you did not want to let them know what it is you were doing. So that's what I want you to understand. On the mandrax case, as a committee, we must write to the National Commission. We must also let that go under the radar. Honourable Adams has brought it to our attention, and we must get exact details and ask specific questions about what happened in the case, why the case is now… What, an arms… Firearms case.

Mr Adams: It has become a firearms case.

Ms Mathys: Yeah, and it must be done as soon as possible. Just quickly, you have not spoken much about your relationship with Lt Gen Sibiya. Or do you have one?

Mr Adams: I have more of a relationship with General Mkhwanazi than Sibiya because I actually sent Mkhwanazi a WhatsApp once. I have never spoken to Sibiya. I have never seen him outside of Parliament. And after what I have heard, I never want to either.

Ms Mathys: That was my next question about whether you were surprised. So you do not speak kindly about the KZN Provincial Commissioner, Lt Gen Mkhwanazi. Mr Adams: Because I know all the other parts were rubbish. I know the talk of the PKTT was rubbish. Okay, at the time, there were 121 files, and there was a question mark about it, but the people who've testified have put even more shade on that. I know that Honourable Kohler Barnard and I are not involved in cartels. So the only conclusion I can come to is about the arrest of his friends. There is no other conclusion for me. If General Mkhwanazi can come and sit in this chair and produce one ounce of proof about the allegations he made, then we're good. He has not. I watched him. I listened to him. His testimony is, I thought to myself, and no General Khumalo will come and confirm, but then he does not. The man lied to Parliament. He lied repeatedly. And I hope he does not get away with it.

Ms Mathys: Were you surprised when he said that our National Commissioner was at the gates of prison? Were you surprised?

Mr Adams: I was.

Ms Mathys: And you believed him immediately?

Mr Adams: Well, General Mkhwanazi says it. He's got a relationship with Masemola, not me. So obviously there was a discussion, and I can even tell you that there was probably a discussion about the press conference as well, because he called that press conference and he got away with it without any disciplinary measures. So I am assuming the National Commissioner gave him consent to do it.

Ms Mathys: And do you know anything else, any other information outside of what Lt Gen Mkhwanazi said about the National Commissioner being in prison?

Mr Adams: No, ma'am.

Ms Mathys: You do not have any information that we can follow up on?

Mr Adams: I am actually busy working on the Red Cross issue right now, so I am done with that. I have had enough of this.

Ms Mathys: And then just my last, and you can comment on it, but, Chair, we should also make the decision that we follow up on the hotels and that we write in, we get whatever information that we need on the ledgers to be able to do that and not let this also go under the radar. And when we go into deliberations, we cannot take positions or make findings or do recommendations without having that information as well. And I hope we do not have too many hurdles with us being able to access that information. That's all. Thank you, Chair. I think the President just has one word.

Mr Malema: Sorry, Chair. I just want to say this thing that the Honourable Member says. It is here. Mr Sauls: Chair, Honourable Mncwango says he's donating his minutes to me. This is a very special day. I have the honour of questioning Honourable Fadiel Adams. Chair, can we please just confirm what time I am ending? Let me be sure. So that you do not –

Mr Adams: Thumma Ameen.

Mr Sauls: Who is the person on your T-shirt?

Mr Adams: This is Lushaan Hendricks. Lushaan Hendricks was 15 years old. He was shot in Hanover Park. Gangsters were firing on each other. They cornered one. One gangster held him as a shield. The other one fired right through him. He was 15 years old. Today, we won a small victory. The killers were denied bail. But this happens in the Cape Flats 10 times a day. Young boys and girls get gunned down like they're nothing. This is personal to me.

Mr Sauls: You speak of General Andre Lincoln. I want to be sure that it is the same general that I have read about. And you will confirm. General Andre Lincoln, a classified coloured hero, unfortunately in a permanent hole in the cemetery. But his blood still speaks from the ground. He's a liberation struggle hero. He served as a high-level intelligence operator from uMkhonto weSizwe, risking his life to dismantle the apartheid state from within. He's the president's shield, handpicked by former President Nelson Mandela for his personal security detail. He is historically credited for uncovering and neutralising an assassination plot against former President Mandela during the 1994 presidential inauguration.

Former President Mandela appointed him in 1996 to lead the Presidential Investigation Task Unit. This mission was to hunt down the Sicilian mafia and investigate high-level rot between organised crime and the police. He was the first victim of state capture. Because he refused to be corrupted, he was targeted by the old guard generals and hit with 47 fabricated charges. He was hounded out of the police for 15 years, becoming the first historical example of the criminal justice system being weaponised against an honest investigator. Despite being framed, he fought a 15-year legal battle to clear his name, eventually winning an acquittal on all charges and returning to SAPS with his integrity intact. He was also the commander of the Anti-Gang Unit in 2018. He was appointed to lead the first specialised unit to tackle the gang wars on the cave flats. He led from the front, becoming a symbol of hope for the forgotten communities of the Western Cape. Honourable Fadiel, is this the same General Lincoln who gave you advice?

Mr Adams: That is but a glimpse of the man. General Lincoln was one of the finest men I ever met. I would have given my life for him. It is a sadness that I did not get the chance to. I miss that brother. He was a big brother, he was a friend, he was an advisor. I mean, this is the man, this country trusted Mandela, Mandela's life, they put Mandela's life in his hands. This is the man who stood side by side with me, while other people want to protect FW de Klerk's boy. Think about this for a second.

Mr Sauls: Did what General Lincoln told you is going to happen to the case dockets really happen?

Mr Adams: I am convinced that it has.

Mr Sauls: It is not just you saying it; we just heard proof or testimony from General Senthumule, who confirmed that these dockets were never seen by a detective or crime office, but ended up in General Jacob's safe. So this substantiates what General Lincoln warned you about when he said to you, they're going to cover this thing up. Is your submission based on that testimony and the fact that we know it is factual that those dockets were in a safe in Pretoria? Is your submission to the committee today that it is clear evidence of defeating the ends of justice?

Mr Adams: Yes, it is, chief. I also want to urge this committee to ask the question: Who instructed Lt Gen Jacobs to collect those dockets? Because he did not wake up one morning and think, let me go and collect case dockets. Someone gave him that instruction. Someone superior. I mean, it is not a long list, but this committee must ask that question.

Mr Sauls: As a member of parliament sitting on the portfolio of police, you say that you asked for a list of generals not having security clearance from the National Commission of Generals Masemola. Has the National Commission ever supplied that list to date?

Mr Sauls: Is it your testimony that the National Commissioner Masemola lied under oath that General Khumalo's clearance was above board, as per your evidence in FAA1, suggesting it was fraudulent?

Mr Sauls: But is it your testimony then that the National Commissioner lied under oath?

Mr Sauls: The case of the vanishing drugs, case number 111-2024, that you speak about, you enquired about a R100 million mandrax bust that received no media attention. What is the current status of that case today?

Mr Adams: The information on that case number refers to firearms. It does not mention drugs. It is something we cannot understand. We believe that the case was tampered with at the time. The docket has been tampered with. We cannot prove this. Therefore, we are asking this committee to investigate.

Mr Sauls: Now, how is it possible that a clandestine lab bust resulting in R100 million worth of drugs now only reflects firearms in the case diary? How is that even possible?

Mr Adams: With these people in charge of the South African police services, my leader, anything is possible. How is it possible that the SAPS busted R100 million worth of mandrax, and there is a media blackout? It is not mentioned on the radio. It is not on TV. It is not in the print media. If they were to eat at a shebeen today, Masemola would be throwing out the alcohol. We have seen the pictures.

They hid 100 million rands worth of mandrax, and they say nothing. And then one year later, Mandrax is not mentioned in the docket anymore. I tell you as a man who understands police corruption, who in his younger days benefited from police corruption, I am telling you that it has made its way to your Eldo’s, to your Westbury’s and to your Cape Flats. I give you that guarantee, and I give you the further guarantee that there are policemen who have unlawfully benefited from that.

Mr Sauls: What do you mean by that? It was stolen by the police and sold on the streets.

Mr Adams: That is exactly what has happened.

Mr Sauls: Are you saying that the drugs that are imprisoning our children are made available by the police?

Mr Adams: Chief, the drugs that are poisoning a generation of our children are made available to these drug lords by the police. The guns which kill our children by the dozen a day are sold to the gangsters by the police. Look at the Colonel Prinsloo issue. 27 000 guns sold to the Cape Flats gangs. 27 000. And they let one policeman take the fall. One. And he got sentenced to 15 years. And he served seven. And I asked the SAPS, “Why did you not object to his parole?” And we did not get an answer. Now you tell me, how is it possible for 27 000 guns to be moved by one man? It was a network. Meanwhile, we are burying 10 children a day. And this is what is expected to go on.

Mr Sauls: You testify that PKTT members are living in a hotel purchased from the slush fund that is used as a washing machine. You call it a washing machine. In other words, you are saying that it is used for money laundering.

Mr Adams: Money laundering and racketeering. Let me explain something to you, if you may. I know it is your time. The PKTT is housing five-star hotels, but they are still claiming meals and S&Ts. They are still claiming meals and S&Ts. How many hours must you work a month to claim R40 000 overtime, but they are claiming R40 000 overtime per month? And we are being asked by Khumalo, Masemola, and Mkhwanazi to accept this. I do not believe that there are mad people on this committee. This committee should not accept that.

Mr Sauls: In your testimony, it was made to appear as if you are denying that some members of the crime intelligence are part of the PKTT. But you are actually saying that you do accept that some members of crime intelligence are living at these properties. But what you are saying is, if I understand you correctly in your previous testimony, that the other members that are part of the PKTT that are not part of crime intelligence are also living, allegedly, at these venues.

Mr Adams: That is 100% what we are saying, Chief. But you must also be careful of the semantics and the gymnastics that these people play with, because I can tell you now that the raid on Vusi “Cat” Matlala’s house was led by Lieutenant Colonel Khumalo, who is a member of the PKTT, not counter-intelligence. Be very careful of how they play. We have learned how they play. We have been playing with them for 18 months.

Mr Sauls: What is the name of the general responsible for purchasing these properties from the slush fund?

Mr Adams: In my opinion, remember I am not the investigator. It can only be Major General Lushaba or Lt Gen Khumalo. Those are the two people assigning powers of crime intelligence, if my understanding is correct, and I believe it is.

Mr Sauls: What is your message to every coloured child in this country watching you right now?

Mr Adams: I was a shooter once. I hurt people. I was a robber once. I robbed people. I was a druggie once. For many, many years, I was addicted to it. It was sold to me. I did not know it was supplied by the police. I did not know the guns I held were supplied by the police. I did not understand the systemic issues. This carnage must stop by any means necessary. I am tired of burying 15-year-old boys. I am tired of seeing sad mothers and paying for funerals. I believe we need better. I believe the Cape Flats, the Eldo’s, the Westbury’s, the Ennerdales, they deserve better. But we are not going to get better with these people in charge. People make fun of my past. But it is my past. It is the only past I have, but my past is a reflection of a million “laaities” here on the Cape Flats. And do you know what I offer? I offer hope. I offer hope that you can get off your knees, and you can stand on your feet, and if you work hard enough, you can make it here. You know me, and you were not allowed here. We had to fight to get here. You know that the system is set up against us, irrespective of what anyone says. You know that a “laaitie” has to dodge bullets on his way to school. Then he has to dodge bullets on the way home again. Then you find out that the South African Police Service is supplying those bullets, and it makes me angry. And this is why when people ask me what I have got against Mkhwanazi, I have a lot against Mkhwanazi because he is defending this.

You know Cheslin Kolbe, you know Benny McCarthy, Wayne Roberts, we have got a million like them. They are just not given the opportunity. I am a proud man. I do not beg, but I will beg. I will beg for children whose names I do not know. That they be given an opportunity. That they do not have to dodge bullets. This is not what we fought for. Maybe one day they will get it. But we are not going to get it if we do not fight for it. My message to any coloured “laaitie” out there is stay in school, respect your mother, and do what you need to do and stay away from the drug dealers. There is nothing there for you. Thank you for letting me get that out there.

Mr Sauls: Honourable Adams, your presence here is the fulfilment of a mission started by your dear friend, the late General Andre Lincoln. Lincoln was a man who survived the trenches of the struggle only to warn us that the police service he helped build was being eaten from within. He warned you that the dockets would move, that the truth would be buried. Today, you have honoured his memory. You have proven that whilst they can move a docket to a safe in Pretoria, they cannot move the truth once it is in the hands of the people’s representative. Your spirit today is that of our Khoi ancestors, the first defenders of the soil. You carry the unyielding flame of Ashley Kriel and Anton Fransch. The resilience of Basil February and the fierce sacrifice of Stanley Lollan, the Rivonia trialist. Like them, you have refused to be silenced by a system that demands your compliance in exchange for your conscience. You have reminded this house that true oversight is not a press conference, but the relentless exposure of the rot that hides behind classified stems. Honourable Adams, you have done exactly that. You have brought the light of evidence into the darkest corners of our security services. You have been criticised for it. You have been called names. But here you are. You are still standing on behalf of every coloured child watching you tonight. I am tempted to call you a hero with holes, but I will not do it because I do not want this moment to be messed up by people who do not understand what is happening here tonight. Mr Wessels: Thank you, Chairperson, and good evening, Honourable Adams. Just a few questions. Mr Wessels: But you did not open the case because you wanted to get General Khumalo out of the way. Or did you?

Mr Adams: I wanted justice, and General Khumalo is not the only person that I laid cases against. So I do not know if people need to think that to make themselves better. If people need to think that to hold on to their generals’ benefits, that is their issue. The fact of the matter is, I did what I felt I needed to do, and I do feel vindicated.

Mr Wessels: Because what you are being accused of is that, yes, that you tried to protect, you are responsible for this whole thing because you exposed alleged corruption that led to the arrest of General Khumalo, and that is actually hindering the fight against these cartels.

Mr Adams: 100%.

Mr Wessels: But you are not guilty of that. Is that your evidence?

Mr Adams: I do not think I fit the profile of someone that cartels would want to associate with, Chief.

Mr Wessels: Because I agree with the Honourable Malema that I personally do not see anything wrong with a Member of Parliament receiving information, taking it to the police, and having it investigated. So you did not get that information through some illegal channel. It was pushed through your door.

Mr Adams: You know, Honourable Wessels, in our office in Mitchells Plain, there is generally a stack of paperwork waiting for me, which people have come to drop off. Some of it involves indigent grants, some of it involves SASSA issues, and some of it involves fraud and corruption. I have laid eight cases of fraud and corruption in the City of Cape Town. Some of them will be before the court soon. One of them is before the courts. I did not play my very first charge against General Khumalo. We have been fighting fraud and corruption for the last seven years. This is not a new thing to us.

Mr Wessels: Okay. I think that is well established. Honourable Adams, there is just one thing that bothers me, and that is a lot of conclusions that you make, assertions that you make that are not necessarily substantiated. Let me give one example. You earlier said that the reason you believe the grabber that was procured is redundant is that it is 4G technology in a 5G environment. Am I correct? But that grabber was purchased in 2017 at the NASREC conference. Am I correct? At that stage, 5G was not in South Africa.

Mr Adams: But you do not buy a R45 million rand if you are only going to use it for 18 months. 5G entered South Africa less than two years later.

Mr Wessels: Three or four years later. But you see, it is an assumption that you made.

Mr Adams: No, I do agree, Chief. But you do understand where I am going with that.

Mr Wessels: I do understand, but you do not have evidence of it.

Mr Adams: No, I do not.

Mr Wessels: Okay. The Mandrax that you say is not mentioned in the docket anymore. How do you know that?

Mr Adams: We have asked someone who is with the NCC to look into it for us because we are concerned that no work is happening.

Mr Wessels: So you got access to the docket.

Mr Adams: No.

Mr Wessels: But how do you then know that mandrax is not mentioned in that docket anymore?

Mr Adams: Because we asked someone what the progress is with that case, because we were promised further investigation. Then this person came back to us because we did not see the docket and said but are you sure this is the right case number? I said it is the one in the minutes. They said, but this thing mentions firearms. I said, well then, the Mandrax is stolen.

Mr Wessels: You also spoke about the hotels and that you are worried about that or the hotel purchase. Do you know for a fact that that hotel is still being operated as a hotel? That it is not just a property that was bought, a shell?

Mr Adams: The hotel was bought as a going concern. That hotel is operating tonight.

Mr Wessels: Do you know that for a fact?

Mr Adams: I am telling you as a fact.

Mr Wessels: How do you know that as a fact?

Mr Adams: Because this is the way they operate.

Mr Wessels: Maybe you see that it is not substantiating.

Mr Adams: But Honourable Klopper, this is why in my testimony I asked this committee to please subpoena the ledgers and that will confirm it.

Mr Wessels: No. You make intelligent assumptions that are probable, but they are not necessarily evidence. And that is what worries me because you are creating a little bit of noise that is deflecting away from why you actually are and what the actual essence of the complaints are that you laid based on information with which you were provided. But these other things are assumptions.

Mr Adams: The complaint that I laid against Honourable Klopper was against the purchase of the hotel.

Mr Wessels: Wessels.

Mr Adams: Sorry, Wessels, because I am looking at Klopper. So Honourable Wessels, the complaint that I laid was because of the purchase of the hotels, because, as per the documentation that I handed over to the Chairperson, they had no right to buy those hotels. We were later reliably informed by a very disgruntled former member of SAPS, whom I will not name, that the PKTT is staying there and that they are charging for it. So I am saying to you that the case has been opened. The IGI has made a finding. I am not reading the media reports. I am reading it, but I am not taking note of it. But it appears as if there is malfeasance there. So I am asking this committee if we are to get to the bottom of this, if we are to know who the bad guys are, get those ledgers before Parliament and deal with that. If I am wrong, I will put my hand up and say I was wrong. But I am saying this loudly, knowing that Khumalo and Masemola cannot lay charges against me tomorrow morning because we will have to have the ledgers in court.

Mr Wessels: Thank you, Chairperson.

Mr Adams: Chairperson, can I just request the two-minute break, please?

Mr Adams: I have always loved Soweto. I mean, I love driving past there, and I decided to go there.

Adv Mokhoaetsi: So you drove past all these stations? So, are you familiar with Orlando Station?

Mr Adams: No, I would not say familiar because I actually had to look for it. But there are two places in Joburg I love driving past, and I have never actually had the opportunity to really go to Soweto and Alex. And I wanted to drive past, and so I went there.

Adv Mokhoaetsi: Okay, I have a third question. You have attached a statement from one of the accused persons of a cash-in-transit thing that happened, which is also related to the death of Sindiso. And now, in the statement, I am not quite sure what the purpose of the statement was. And I just when we later go on to reflect on this, I just want to understand that we use the document for what you have intended, or at least we consider why you have included this in your bundle at the very end of this statement. The deponent indicates that he says, I want with this statement, I want justice for the family of Sindiso and also for myself. And he wants the nation to know about his challenges with the criminal justice system. And so he goes forth about the things he wants. And the last paragraph, he says, what is upsetting is that I listened to a voice note from the Ad Hoc Committee of General Mkhwanazi speaking to Cedrick Nkabinde, saying that criminal intelligence policemen must not be arrested even if they commit a crime. They must be placed elsewhere. And he said not to touch intelligence, as they can burn the whole nation. What is the purpose of the statement, and who was it intended for?

Mr Adams: I actually would not be able to tell you, Advocate. No, no, that is not my position. That is something that I attach because it is something that I know about. I think he's upset because I think he was promised things if he kept his mouth shut. I think I did not ask. Remember, this man does not speak a word of English, and I do not speak two words of Zulu. So there was a problem there. But I think he's very upset with certain generals who made him certain promises to kill Sindiso. Now, you know what upsets me about the death of Sindiso? His mother was promised justice. That man is in a little cell. He can be fetched right now to confess. But what are we told? Oh, it is under investigation.

Adv Mokhoaetsi: Just one more question on this. You travelled to KZN in November. Were you assisting him in getting this affidavit deposed and signed? You must have had a reason why you travelled there to go and get this statement.

Mr Adams: I actually went because I wanted to hear his story. Because I knew about the death of Sindiso Maqaqa. I knew that he and Honourable Malema were very tight. I believe Honourable Malema to be a good man. And before Julius Malema gets shot, let us find out what happened here. And he gave me a statement, but it was in Zulu. So it was worthless to a man like me.

Adv Mokhoaetsi: Thank you, Honourable.

Mr Adams: Thank you, ma'am.

Adv Mokhoaetsi: Thank you, Chair. I am done.

Chairperson: Honourable Skosana.

Mr Skosana: You know, I get irritated, Chair. When I have to speak, and then you make some gestures here. I need your protection.

Chairperson: Where is that, Honourable Skosana?

Mr Skosana: You gave me an opportunity here to speak, and somebody is whispering behind me. And I do not want to point him out because he'll be embarrassed. But let me go on. I just want to make a statement to you that it should not be misquoted here. That you should not open a case against anyone in this country. I am a member of Parliament. I can do that. I have done that before. And I have been blamed by Minister Mchunu and General Sibiya. I have done that. Now, the issue that we are here to test these allegations against Mr Adams. You know, the way you obtained what you said to us. We are testing that. You know, but it should not be misquoted here to say, no, there is something wrong. No, no, no, there is nothing wrong. The issue is that we are testing, like any other witness, to find out the truth. Because as I am sitting here, you know, it is, I would say, ambiguous. Perhaps it is an understatement. You know, I have mixed feelings about your testimony. You know, you said many things. You know, we are testing everyone. And we are not testing as patients. We want to get the truth. Because we have to make a report as a committee to Parliament. Now, my last one was what you said. I wanted to find out who contacted you from IDAC.

Mr Adams: It was Brigadier Perumal.

Chairperson: Thank you so much. Do we still have any other honourable member who wants to speak? I do not see any hand. Honourable Adams and your beloved family, your comrades, and friends. We want to thank you so much for coming and spent, for having come and spent the hours that you did with us. We want to thank you so much. And on your behalf, I must thank these people for the support that they have given you. Thank you so much, honourable members.

Mr Malema: I like the colour of the file of Honourable Adams.

Mr Adams: My second favourite colour.

Chairperson: Tomorrow, Honourable Members, is 10:00. Thank you so much. The proceedings have come to an end. At the same venue.

Mr Adams: Chairperson, am I dismissed?

Chairperson: Yes, you are released. You are no longer under oath.

The meeting was adjourned.
